ii
Abstract
Embodied artificial intelligence requires agents that can perceive, navigate, understand,
and act upon user-defined tasks within complex environments. Achieving this demands
a perceptual system that can bridge the gap between the reconstruction precision and the
semantic richness respectively required for control and reasoning. This thesis proposes
that Neural Radiance Fields (NeRFs) can serve as the foundation for such a system,
evolving from their original role as a view-synthesis tool into a robust, queryable scene
map that anchors a modular embodied ecosystem.
We first address the foundational need for a reliable, in-the-wild geometric mapping,
introducing RING-NeRF. This architecture integrates structural inductive biases to solve
the robustness gaps of standard NeRFs, enabling stable, incremental and adaptive reconstruction from sparse or imperfect multi-view imagery. Building upon this, we enable
the agent’s self-functioning capabilities by developing direct interpretation methods. We
propose architectural enhancements and generalization techniques to perform semantic
segmentation directly within the NeRF model, providing the fixed-category understanding essential for navigation and object manipulation.
Finally, we address the challenge of open-ended, user-driven scene understanding.
We first introduce DiSCO-3D, an open-vocabulary NeRF segmentation framework that
allows the agent to focus locally and decompose specific concepts based on arbitrary text
queries. Complementing this local understanding, we develop LLaVA³, a global reasoning
pipeline that interfaces the 3D NeRF representation with Large Vision-Language Models,
enabling the agent to interpret the environment holistically and plan complex tasks without
fine-tuning of the foundation model.
Through these contributions, the thesis establishes a comprehensive framework where
a robust, semantically grounded NeRF serves as the central state representation, supporting specifically designed modules for both the functional reflexes and the cognitive
reasoning of an embodied agent.
Keywords: 3D Scene understanding, NeRF, Embodied AI, 3D Reconstruction
iii
Résumé
L’intelligence artificielle incarnée nécessite des agents capables de percevoir, de comprendre et d’agir au sein d’environnements complexes. Cela requiert un système de
perception conciliant la précision de reconstruction pour l’action physique et la richesse
sémantique pour le raisonnement. Cette thèse propose d’utiliser les champs de radiance
neuronaux (NeRF) comme fondation, les faisant évoluer de la synthèse de vues vers une
représentation de scène robuste et sémantique, socle d’un écosystème incarné modulaire.
Nous abordons d’abord le besoin fondamental d’une cartographie géométrique fiable
en conditions réelles en introduisant RING-NeRF. Cette architecture intègre des biais inductifs structurels pour combler les lacunes de robustesse et d’adaptabilité des NeRF standards, permettant une reconstruction stable et incrémentale à partir d’images éparses ou
imparfaites. Sur cette base, nous permettons à l’agent d’assurer son auto-fonctionnement
en développant des méthodes d’interprétation sémantique intrinsèques. Nous proposons
des améliorations architecturales et des techniques de généralisation pour effectuer une
segmentation sémantique au cœur même du modèle NeRF, offrant ainsi l’identification
de catégories prédéfinies indispensable à la navigation et à la manipulation d’objets.
Enfin, nous relevons le défi d’une compréhension de scène interactive, pilotée par les
tâches données par l’utilisateur. Nous introduisons d’abord DiSCO-3D, un module de
segmentation NeRF à vocabulaire ouvert permettant à l’agent de focaliser son attention
sur des concepts en réponse à des requêtes textuelles libres et de les décomposer. En
complément, nous développons LLaVA³, un système de raisonnement global qui connecte
la représentation NeRF à de grands modèles de vision-langage (VLM). Cette interface
permet à l’agent d’appréhender l’environnement de manière holistique et de planifier des
tâches complexes sans nécessiter d’affinage (fine-tuning) du modèle de fondation.
À travers ces contributions, cette thèse établit un cadre complet où une représentation
NeRF robuste et sémantique constitue le pivot de l’écosystème, soutenant des modules
désignés pour les réflexes fonctionnels et pour le raisonnement cognitif d’un agent incarné.
Mots-clés : Compréhension de scènes 3D, NeRF, IA Incarnée, Reconstruction 3D

1
Introduction
1.1
From Digital Algorithms to Embodied AI: Confronting the
Physical World
The last decade has witnessed a paradigm shift in Artificial Intelligence. We have entered
the era of Generative AI, where systems operating in the digital realm can now synthesize
complex code [175], converse fluently [1, 4, 165, 169], and essentially "dream" entire
photorealistic worlds [7] from simple text prompts. In stark contrast, the physical realm
has witnessed a relative stagnation in the capabilities of autonomous robots [42]. While
software agents demonstrate near-human capabilities, physical agents still struggle to
perform basic tasks in unstructured environments. Transferring intelligence from the
error-forgiving world of bits to the unconstrained and noisy world of atoms remains a
grand challenge [152, 182, 107]. Unlike Generative AI, which focuses on synthesis, the
physical world requires grounding, i.e. the ability to truthfully map sensory inputs into a
coherent, actionable understanding of the environment. This is the domain of Embodied
AI [37, 190, 96]: the quest to create autonomous agents that do not merely process data,
but perceive, reason, and act within the physical world we inhabit.
To operate effectively in the real world, an embodied agent requires more than just
mechanical actuators; it relies on a sophisticated control architecture capable of both lowlevel sensorimotor reflexes and high-level reasoning. This duality mirrors the cognitive
framework proposed by psychologist Daniel Kahneman [71], who distinguishes between
two modes of human thought: "System 1", which governs fast, automatic, and intuitive
operations, and "System 2", which handles slow, effortful, and analytical reasoning.
The foundation of this control hierarchy corresponds to the agent’s Functional Core
(System 1). This layer acts as the robot’s "reflexes", handling the self-functioning and
immediate safety of the machine. It executes tasks that are known at design time: predefined routines such as incremental environment mapping, collision-free navigation, and
precise object manipulation, which must operate reliably and automatically regardless of
the situation and the user input.
Above this sits the Cognitive Core (System 2), increasingly powered by Large Language
1
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
Models [1, 4, 165, 112]. This high-level planner is responsible for the unknown, i.e. tasks
that cannot be anticipated before deployment. It interprets ambiguous, live user intent,
breaks down long-term goals (e.g., "Clean the living room"), and reasons about openended concepts that were not explicitly programmed. While the functional core ensures
the robot can move and act safely, the cognitive core determines why and where it should
do so based on the user’s immediate needs.
While these two systems operate at different levels of abstraction, they share a fundamental requirement: they both rely on a shared understanding of the robot’s environment.
While a growing body of research advocates for "model-free" or end-to-end approaches
which attempt to map sensor inputs directly to actions without an explicit intermediate
representation of the scene [113], deploying such systems for general-purpose tasks remains difficult. Crucially, these reactive policies lack persistence: they struggle to retain
information about regions currently out of view or recall past observations necessary
for long-term planning. We argue that maintaining a persistent internal scene model is
preferable. Such a model acts as a shared memory that enables the robot to remember
unseen areas, reason about past states, and ensure geometric constraints that black-box
policies may violate.
The challenge, however, lies in the nature of this representation. The functional core
demands one capable of efficient and incremental reconstruction to maintain a real-time
map of a dynamic world, while providing the precise geometry and basic object-level
semantics required for self-functioning physical tasks like navigation, object grasping and
manipulation. Simultaneously, the cognitive core requires a representation that can act
as a connective interface between the physical world and the agent’s "brain", capable of
grounding abstract language concepts into specific spatial regions. Consequently, the central bottleneck in embodied AI is perception: constructing a shared 3D representation that
is robust and precise enough for the body’s reflexes, yet semantically rich and queryable
enough for the mind’s reasoning.
Historically, robotics has relied on discrete geometric representations such as point
clouds, voxel grids, or explicit meshes. While effective for specific sub-tasks, these classical
formats struggle to serve as an effective backbone for modern embodied intelligence
Point clouds [94, 126] are unstructured and sparse. While sensor-native (e.g. from
LiDAR) and thus easy to capture, they remain a collection of discrete samples rather
than a defined shape, leaving the space between points geometrically ambiguous. Voxel
grids [180] offer structure but suffer from cubic memory growth, strictly limiting their
resolution in large environments. Explicit meshes [156] also provide surfaces but are
rigid and difficult to update incrementally as new data arrives. Beyond these structural
limitations, another primary challenge lies in constructing these representations from the
raw sensor data (like point clouds, depth maps, or multi-view images). Converting noisy,
partial sensor streams into clean, watertight meshes or dense voxel grids often requires
complex, heuristic fusion pipelines that are prone to failure in cluttered real-world scenes.
Finally, these discrete structures are ill-suited for the rich semantic understanding required
2
1.2. NEURAL RADIANCE FIELDS: AN ADAPTED NOVEL PERCEPTUAL 3D
REPRESENTATION
Figure 1.1: Novel View Synthesis Paradigm. We visualize the set of 100 views of the synthetic Drums scene randomly captured on a surrounding hemisphere and used as a NeRF
input, and we show two novel views rendered from the optimized NeRF representation.
Figure is from [120].
by the cognitive core. Assigning meaning to sparse point clouds or coarse meshes
is inherently difficult because the geometric primitives rarely align with the semantic
boundaries of objects. This sparsity prevents the system from building a comprehensive,
dense semantic map, forcing it to rely on interpolation or heuristics to fill in the gaps.
Ideally, the cognitive core requires a representation where any point in the continuous
scene volume can be queried for its semantic identity rather just a subset of discrete surface
points.
These limitations highlight the need for a paradigm shift: to bridge the perceptual
gap, we require a representation that can be derived directly and incrementally from raw
sensors into an adequate scene model capable of natively fusing dense geometry and rich
semantics.
1.2
Neural Radiance Fields: An Adapted Novel Perceptual 3D
Representation
To address the limitations of classical explicit representations, recent research has converged toward a powerful novel paradigm: optimization-based representations [73].
Unlike classical pipelines that explicitly construct geometry via depth fusion, meshing,
or feed-forward large generative AI models, which are all prone to noise and integration
errors, these methods define the scene itself as a learnable model, parameterized either by
a continuous neural network [120] or a set of discrete primitives [75, 52, 114]. Crucially, the
geometry is not manually assembled but is discovered via gradient descent, optimizing
the model parameters to reproduce sensor observations (typically RGB images) through
differentiable rendering. This shift moves the burden of reconstruction from heuristic
geometric algorithms to gradient-based optimization, allowing for more robust handling
of noisy sensor data and complex scene topologies.
Among these approaches, the Neural Radiance Field (NeRF) [120] has emerged as
the paradigm-defining example. Fundamentally, a NeRF represents a 3D scene as a
continuous volumetric function typically parameterized by a neural network that maps
spatial coordinates to density and color. By utilizing differentiable volume rendering to
optimize this function against a set of 2D multi-view input images, it learns to synthesize
3
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
novel views with photorealistic fidelity while implicitly reconstructing the underlying
scene geometry, as illustrated in Figure 1.1.
This unique formulation stands out as a very promising representation for our unified
agent architecture, addressing the specific requirements of both intelligence cores.
For the functional core, NeRFs simplify the mapping process: they can be optimized
directly from standard, unconstrained video streams and inherently support incremental
updates by constantly refining the function as new data arrives. Furthermore, their
spatial continuity provides infinite spatial resolution. This offers not only the precision
required for physics-aware tasks like navigation and object manipulation, but also a dense
foundation for basic semantic understanding. By associating a semantic class to every
point in the continuous volume, the agent can reliably distinguish traversable terrain from
obstacles or identify object boundaries for precise manipulation.
Crucially for the cognitive core, NeRFs are fully differentiable. This property is key to
bridging the gap between geometry and meaning, as it allows us to easily backpropagate
high-dimensional semantic signals directly into the 3D structure, usually from 2D segmentation masks or language embeddings. This transforms the representation from a purely
geometric map into a multi-modal backbone capable of fusing appearance, geometry, and
semantics into a unified, queryable scene memory.
However, adapting Neural Radiance Fields from a computer graphics tool into a robust
representation adapted for robotics is non-trivial. Much like the Generative AI models
discussed at the opening of this chapter, standard NeRFs were originally conceived as
generative models for the digital realm, specifically for the task of Novel View Synthesis,
i.e., generating photo-realistic views of a scene from viewpoints not present in the input.
Their primary utility lies in applications such as cinematic rendering, virtual reality asset
creation, or photorealistic product visualization. In these contexts, the data capture is
perfectly planned, with a static environment and carefully curated camera trajectories,
and the ultimate metric of success is visual fidelity for a human observer. This goal stands
in stark contrast to the requirements of an Embodied AI agent operating within real-life,
uncontrolled environments. This misalignment creates three critical gaps that this thesis
aims to bridge:
• The Robustness and Adaptability Gap: Unlike a studio setup where dense camera
coverage is available simultaneously, a robot incrementally explores "in-the-wild"
via sparse and noisy sensor streams. Standard NeRFs fail here in two critical
ways. First, they are fragile: optimization is prone to severe geometric artifacts
like "floaters" or geometric collapse when faced with unconstrained data. Second,
they lack structural extensibility: standard models are typically trained on a fixed
number of images with a fixed resolution across the whole scene. They suffer
from catastrophic forgetting (as the robot explores new areas, the network tends
to overwrite its memory of previously visited regions) during sequential updates
and cannot dynamically adjust their level of detail to match the varying complexity
4
1.2. NEURAL RADIANCE FIELDS: AN ADAPTED NOVEL PERCEPTUAL 3D
REPRESENTATION
Figure 1.2: The Dual-Core Architecture of our Embodied Agent. We conceptualize the
agent as two distinct systems operating at different levels of abstraction. The Functional
Core (System 1) handles fast, safety-critical reflexes and pre-defined known tasks (e.g.,
environment mapping), requiring precise metric geometry. The Cognitive Core (System 2)
handles slow, deliberative reasoning and unknown user queries, requiring rich semantic
abstraction. This thesis proposes multiple contributions that connect a shared NeRF
representation with both cores.
of a large-scale environment or the computational constraints of the agent. These
failures are critical for the Functional Core, as they directly compromise the integrity
of the geometric mapping process.
• The Semantic Gap: While geometrically dense, standard NeRFs are semantically
"blind." They represent the world strictly as a continuous field of density and
color, lacking any inherent concept of object instances or categories. For a robot,
a reconstructed table is indistinguishable from the floor it stands on. To serve
the Functional Core, the representation must be actively enriched to distinguish
traversable surfaces from obstacles and identify manipulable objects.
• The Interaction Gap: Finally, a standard NeRF is a passive data structure; it can
be rendered, but not easily semantically queried from a user query. This presents
two distinct hurdles for an intelligent agent. First, the representation lacks semantic
queryability: a NeRF can render an image, but it cannot search for scene content like
"Where is the mug?" or "How many chairs are there?" because it lacks the ability to
perform keyword-based search. Second, it lacks a cognitive interface: there is no
direct mechanism to feed a NeRF model into the "brain" of the agent. Unlike 2D
images that can be tokenized and given as inputs to LLMs, the implicit Neural Field
representation is opaque to high-level reasoning engines. To support the Cognitive
Core, we must bridge this divide, transforming the NeRF into a representation that
is both searchable by the agent and interpretable by its brain model.
5
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
1.3
Thesis Statement: A Unified Ecosystem for Embodied
Perception
This thesis argues that Neural Radiance Fields can be fundamentally evolved from a passive
visual representation, originally designed for novel view synthesis, into the foundational
3D representation for a comprehensive Embodied AI perception system. We propose
that this evolution requires systematically addressing the critical gaps identified above,
specifically the lack of robustness and extensibility, semantic meaning, and interactive
capabilities. By bridging these divides, we argue that a NeRF can serve as the central scene
memory of an autonomous agent navigating in real-life environments. This representation
is uniquely positioned to bridge the perceptual bottleneck: it provides a single, continuous
structure that simultaneously satisfies the rigorous mapping requirements of the robot’s
functional core and the rich, open-ended semantic requirements of its cognitive core.
Crucially, we position our work not as one single "end-to-end" model where a network
attempts to map pixels directly to actions, but rather as a modular ecosystem anchored
by the NeRF mapping. This architectural choice effectively decouples the construction of
the scene model from its usage. By maintaining the NeRF as a persistent, shared "source
of truth," we allow different downstream processes to access the same underlying scene
map at the specific level of abstraction they require.
In this framework, the NeRF does not attempt to be all things at once. Instead, it
serves as a stable and precise representation upon which specialized interpretive modules
operate. This allows the system to seamlessly switch modes: relying on efficient, fixedcategory segmentation for high-frequency control loops, while reserving computationally
intensive, open-ended reasoning tools for complex user interactions. This ecosystem
approach ensures that the agent’s perception is scalable and adaptable, providing a
foundational memory that remains valid regardless of the specific task at hand.
1.4
Thesis Organization
The remainder of this manuscript is organized into six chapters, tracing the progressive
evolution of Neural Radiance Fields from their origins as a novel view synthesis tool
in computer vision into a comprehensive scene representation tailored for embodied
agents. Each chapter addresses a specific layer of the proposed ecosystem, identifying
and resolving a critical limitation that currently prevents standard NeRFs from serving as
a usable and robust scene representation for autonomous agents.
Chapter 2 establishes the theoretical background of this work. We begin by reviewing
the history of 3D scene representations, tracing the paradigm shift from classical discrete
structures to modern differentiable approaches, with a particular focus on Neural Fields.
We then provide a detailed analysis of Neural Radiance Fields (NeRFs), and present
the challenges they face in uncontrolled, real-world environments. We then bridge the
6
1.4. THESIS ORGANIZATION
gap between geometry and semantics by first reviewing the principles of 2D visual
interpretation and then examining how this research can be transferred to NeRF.
Chapter 3 addresses the first critical barrier to deployment: the lack of geometric
robustness and extensibility of standard neural fields. Before an agent can understand
its environment, it must be able to map it reliably and consistently under real-world
constraints, as part of its Functional Core capacities. In this chapter, we tackle the
limitations of stability and scalability by introducing RING-NeRF, a novel architecture
designed to serve as a robust geometric backbone. We propose a set of specific inductive
biases, implemented via a residual, multi-resolution grid formulation, that enable stable,
incremental and precision-adaptive reconstruction from sparse image streams. This
contribution transforms the NeRF from a Novel View Synthesis tool into a scalable and
persistent map suitable notably for in-the-wild robotic operations.
Chapter 4 focuses on the semantic understanding of the Functional Core, equipping
our robust geometric representation with direct semantic meaning. While RING-NeRF
provides the mapping, it remains semantically "blind." To overcome this, we investigate
methods to perform semantic segmentation directly from the model, enhancing the agent’s
self-functioning capabilities by enabling it to identify the fixed categories necessary for
navigation and object manipulation. This chapter introduces two key sets of contributions:
first, architectural enhancements that ensure accurate, fast and robust scene-specific
interpretation, even under noisy supervision; and second, a foundational approach for
Generalized Semantic Segmentation. By proposing mechanisms to interpret geometry
without scene-specific labels, we address the critical NeRF limitation of per-scene semantic
training, removing a fundamental barrier for comprehensive Embodied AI.
Chapter 5 marks the transition to the Cognitive Core, addressing the need for flexible,
user-driven interaction. While fixed semantic categories enable navigation, they are insufficient for understanding open-ended user requests. To bridge this gap, we introduce
DiSCO-3D, a method for Open-Vocabulary Sub-concept Discovery. Unlike standard segmentation which maps the world to a pre-defined list of classes, this module plugs into
a NeRF to allow the agent to dynamically "focus" on specific regions of the scene based
on arbitrary text queries. Furthermore, by combining unsupervised scene decomposition
with open-vocabulary guidance, DiSCO-3D goes beyond standard open-vocabulary segmentation: it can semantically decompose query-relevant objects into their constituent
sub-concepts. This capability significantly broadens the scope of interpretable queries,
allowing the agent to react to conceptual requests rather than just keyword matching. We
finally demonstrate how DiSCO-3D serves as a specialized local perception tool, enabling
the cognitive core to identify and retrieve fine-grained object details relevant to the user’s
immediate task.
Chapter 6 completes the architecture by enabling global reasoning. While the local
focus of DiSCO-3D is essential for detailed interaction, it does not provide the holistic
situational awareness required for high-level planning. To address this, we introduce
LLaVA3 , a pipeline that interfaces the NeRF representation directly with the Cognitive
7
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
Core modelled as a Large Vision-Language Model (VLM). By transforming the neural
field into a structured, object-centric, and omni-directional visual description, we allow
the cognitive core to perceive and reason about the entire environment simultaneously,
without requiring any fine-tuning of the large model. This contribution equips the agent
with the global context necessary to answer complex questions about scene layout, relationships, and potential affordances, effectively bridging the gap between 3D perception
and linguistic reasoning.
Finally, Chapter 7 concludes the dissertation. We synthesize the contributions of
this thesis and demonstrates how the proposed ecosystem transforms a vision tool into
a comprehensive 3D representation tailored for embodied AI. Then, we discuss the
remaining open challenges and outline a visionary roadmap for the future of the embodied
AI field.

2
Background
Achieving comprehensive embodied AI requires a scene representation that supports
both accurate, robust geometric reconstruction and deep semantic understanding of
the environment. This background chapter establishes the conceptual and technical
foundations upon which our contributions toward robust, semantic, and open-ended 3D
scene understanding are built.
To situate our contributions within this context, we first review the evolution of 3D
scene representations, from classical discrete models such as meshes, voxels, and point
clouds to modern differentiable representations, with particular emphasis on Neural
Radiance Fields (NeRFs). We then shift our focus from reconstruction to interpretation,
first tracing the development of 2D visual understanding and then extending it to 3D
NeRF semantics, ultimately paving the way toward a representation capable of supporting
high-level reasoning and embodied spatial intelligence.
2.1
Foundations of 3D Scene Representations
2.1.1
Goals of 3D Reconstruction and Scene Understanding in Embodied AI
Reconstructing and understanding the three-dimensional world has long been a central
pursuit in computer vision. 3D reconstruction seeks to capture the geometry of a scene,
while scene understanding enriches this representation with semantic and contextual
knowledge about objects, their attributes, and their relations. Together, these capabilities
form the foundation for systems that not only perceive but can also reason about and
interact with their surroundings.
The importance of 3D reconstruction and scene understanding spans a wide range of
applications. In autonomous vehicles [211, 59, 97], 3D geometry enables robust navigation
and localization, while semantic understanding allows systems to identify signs, obstacles,
and dynamic objects. In augmented and virtual reality [174], accurate and semantic reconstructions provide the foundation for immersive and physically consistent experiences. In
medical imaging [138, 194, 147], semantic volumetric reconstruction of anatomy supports
diagnostics, treatment planning, and surgical guidance. Similar technologies also enable
10
2.1. FOUNDATIONS OF 3D SCENE REPRESENTATIONS
Figure 2.1: Examples of Explicit 3D Representations of the Stanford Bunny model.
Figure is from [60].
the exploration and preservation of real-world sites at unprecedented scale and fidelity,
from historical landmarks to industrial environments designed for safer and more efficient
workflows [51, 27].
In recent years, these advances have converged toward the emerging field of embodied
AI [137, 190, 96, 44], mostly within autonomous robotics [173], where perception, understanding, and action are integrated within interactive agents. Here, 3D representations
serve as the bridge between sensory observations and decision-making. An embodied
agent must reconstruct its surrounding environment from raw sensory inputs (e.g., RGB
or RGB-D streams), understand the objects and affordances it contains, and reason about
how to act to fulfill user-specified goals. This requires not only accurate and robust
3D reconstruction, but also semantic grounding and high-level reasoning capabilities,
ultimately linking 3D perception with language and task planning.
This dual objective has driven the evolution of 3D representations, from discrete
geometric primitives such as voxels, meshes, and point clouds to differentiable fields
that can encode jointly geometry and semantics. In the following, we review these
representation families, outlining their core principles, strengths, and limitations.
2.1.2
Classical Discrete Representations: Meshes, Voxels, and Point Clouds
Since the beginning of 3D vision, 3D scenes were traditionally modeled using discrete
or geometric data structures such as voxels, meshes, and point clouds. These classical
representations, illustrated in Figure 2.1, have been the foundation of computer graphics,
computer vision, and robotics pipelines for decades, and remain widely used in practical
systems. However, they also come with fundamental limitations that motivated the
emergence of novel 3D scene models.
Voxels. Voxel-based representations extend the notion of pixels within 2D images and
discretize the 3D scene space into a regular grid, where each cell encodes occupancy, color,
or signed distance values [26, 183, 184]. They are conceptually simple and have been used
extensively for many AI-related tasks notably due to their compatibility with many neural
networks architectures (e.g. 3D convolutions for 3D object detection and occupancy completion) [191, 57]. Their main drawback is their scalability: memory requirements grow
11
CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND
cubically with resolution, making it impractical to represent large or detailed environments. Coarse voxel grids have poor geometric fidelity, while fine grids are prohibitively
expensive to store and process [5]. Although solutions to improve the resolution without
additional cost exists (e.g. hashgrids [166] or efficient space partitioning [28, 56, 148]),
they still struggle to describe precisely detailed scenes. Moreover, in contrast to pixels,
voxel representations are not directly sensed but instead reconstructed through complex
pipelines, such as multi-view stereo or deep learning depth estimation, that approximate
rather than capture reality. As a result, they inherit both the computational overhead and
the inaccuracies of these upstream models.
Meshes. Meshes describe surfaces as collections of vertices connected by edges and
faces, most commonly triangles [180, 72]. They are compact, efficient for rendering, and
can capture accurate geometric detail. As a result, they form the backbone of many computer graphics pipelines. However, they require an explicitly defined topology, meaning
that the connectivity between vertices must be specified and consistently maintained.
This makes meshes difficult to generate directly from real-world objects or 3D scenes,
where data is often noisy, incomplete, or unstructured, also requiring heavy pipelines.
Furthermore, their reliance on handcrafted connectivity reduces their compatibility with
neural representations, limiting their usefulness for learning-based or semantic scene
understanding [156].
Point Clouds. Point clouds represent a scene as an unstructured set of 3D points, typically
captured directly by LiDAR or depth sensors. They are simple, flexible, and widely used
in robotics for mapping, navigation, and perception tasks, particularly in autonomous
vehicles [94]. Contrary to voxels and meshes, point clouds are sensor-native and can
directly encode rich geometric information. However, they are inherently sparse and
lack explicit surface connectivity, which complicates downstream processing and surface
reconstruction [126]. While many neural architectures [139, 140] have been developed to
process point clouds effectively, the absence of inherent topology still poses challenges
for tasks requiring dense surface understanding or fine-grained geometric reasoning.
Consequently, tasks such as semantic scene understanding, surface completion, and point
denoising remain active areas of research [187, 15, 208].
Discussion. While voxels, meshes, and point clouds each provide effective ways to
represent 3D geometry, they also illustrate the trade-offs inherent in classical representations. Voxels offer a structured format that facilitates interpretation, but they struggle
with scalability, fine detail and generation. Meshes efficiently capture continuous surfaces
and details, yet require explicitly defined connectivity, which complicates reconstruction
from raw data. Point clouds provide flexible, sensor-native access to geometry, but their
sparsity and lack of inherent surface structure can limit semantic understanding.
12
2.1. FOUNDATIONS OF 3D SCENE REPRESENTATIONS
These limitations highlight a persistent gap: classical representations excel at encoding
geometry, but they all do so at the cost of crucial limitations for modern embodied AI scene
understanding applications. They either lack the ability to represent details or large-scale
scene; the flexibility to be easily derived from accessible captors (i.e. cameras); or are
limited in semantic understanding applicability.
The Evolution of Explicit Methods: Feed-Forward Point Maps. It is worth noting that
very recent advances in deep learning aim to overcome those discrete representations
limits. Methods like MAST3R [178, 88], VGGT [176], or MapAnything [74] treat 3D reconstruction as a direct regression problem, training massive neural networks to predict
dense 3D point maps directly from multi-view images in a single feed-forward pass. A
"point map" is a dense 2D image where each pixel encodes the 3D coordinate (𝑥, 𝑦, 𝑧)
corresponding to the image’s pixel’s projection in 3D space, rather than a color, treating
geometry as a multi-channel image generated by the network. These approaches achieve
impressive speed and robustness, effectively bypassing traditional photogrammetry. However, despite their modern architecture, they inherit the fundamental limitations of the
point cloud format: the output remains a collection of discrete, unorganized points without connectivity or a continuous surface definition. Moreover, their feed-forward nature
prevents the maintenance of a persistent scene map, as they essentially regress a new,
independent reconstruction at every inference step rather than incrementally updating a
shared internal state.
Obtaining easily reconstructible and interpretable large-scale 3D scenes has motivated
the rise of novel 3D representations. Specifically, recent representations focus on recovering
high-fidelity geometry and appearance directly from images while supporting richer
semantic and task-driven interpretation. Such advances are particularly relevant for
embodied AI, where agents must not only perceive their surroundings but also reason
about and act within them, relying on 3D scene representations that are both accurate and
semantically meaningful.
2.1.3
The Rise of Differentiable 3D Scene Representations
While classical representations such as voxels, meshes, and point clouds have enabled
a wide range of robotic and computer vision applications, they remain fundamentally
constrained by various limitations. Those have spurred the emergence of differentiable,
learned continuous representations, which reformulate the 3D reconstruction paradigm
as an optimization problem where a model is overfitted to a specific scene’s observations.
Early works on differentiable 3D modeling introduced implicit neural representations,
where a neural network parameterizes a continuous function defining the surface of a
scene or object. Examples include DeepSDF [133], which represents geometry as a signed
distance function, and Occupancy Networks [118], which learn to predict the likelihood
13
CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND
of occupancy at any spatial location. However, these methods typically rely on explicit 3D
supervision such as meshes or point clouds, complicating the reconstruction of in-the-wild
scenes.
To overcome the need for 3D supervision, the field shifted toward optimizing representations directly from 2D images via differentiable rendering, where gradients flow straight
from the image loss back to the 3D parameters. Within this paradigm, two dominant
families of approaches have emerged: optimization of implicit volumetric functions and
optimization of explicit primitives.
Implicit Volumetric Functions: Neural Radiance Fields. The first realization of this
paradigm is the Neural Radiance Field (NeRF) [120], which forms the basis of this thesis. NeRFs introduce the notion of learning continuous volumetric functions as neural
networks parameters directly from multi-view imagery. In this framework, a neural
network takes a 3D spatial position as input and outputs the corresponding density and
radiance. By jointly modeling them both within a fully differentiable volumetric rendering
process, NeRF parameters are optimized end-to-end by comparing rendered views with
ground-truth observed images. This produces continuous, photo-realistic and compact
reconstructions without requiring explicit 3D supervision or depth data. Crucially, the
differentiability of this pipeline allows integration with modern semantics frameworks,
while the continuity of the representation enables spatial querying at any resolution.
Explicit Differentiable Primitives: 3D Gaussian Splatting. More recently, alternatives
have sought to improve the computational efficiency of this process by returning to
explicit primitives. The leading approach in this direction is 3D Gaussian Splatting
(3DGS) [75]. Moving away from continuous fields, 3DGS represents the scene as a cloud of
3D anisotropic Gaussians, each optimized with explicit attributes for position, covariance,
opacity, and color. The key innovation is a fully differentiable rasterizer that projects these
3D Gaussians into 2D "splats" on the image plane, allowing for optimization of explicit
3D parameters. This approach has revolutionized the field notably by enabling real-time
rendering. However, fundamentally, 3DGS remains a collection of discrete primitives,
mirroring an advanced unstructured point cloud, with the same limitations. Some recent
works like Radiant Foam [52] and EVER [114] have emerged, intending to improve the
quality, speed and functionalities of primitives-based differentiable 3D representations.
While 3DGS offers superior rendering speed, we argue that NeRFs remain a more
suitable representation for scene understanding in robotics for various reasons:
• Continuity: NeRF defines a continuous volumetric field, effectively functioning as
a dense 3D map. An agent can query geometric and semantic information at any
spatial coordinate to resolve collisions or plan grasps. In contrast, 3DGS consists of
discrete primitives with undefined empty space in between, acting fundamentally as
14
2.2. NEURAL RADIANCE FIELDS FUNDAMENTALS
Figure 2.2: Optimization-Based Representations. Those differentiable representations
either implicitly store density and radiance fields inside a neural network or optimize
explicit primitives to fit the scene. Figure is from [30].
a scene renderer optimized for 2D visual projection rather than a spatially consistent
scene model.
• Compactness: Because NeRFs represent the scene as an implicit function rather
than an explicit collection of millions of Gaussians, they require significantly less
memory to store and visualize complex environments. This compactness is crucial for
embodied agents operating with limited onboard storage and bandwidth, allowing
them to maintain extensive maps of large-scale scenes.
• Structure: As we will explore in the next chapter, modern NeRF architectures often
use structured data formats (such as grids) rather than unstructured lists of primitives.
This regularity facilitates the integration of deep learning operations, making it easier
to inject and process semantic features directly within the representation.
This transition toward differentiable representations marks a major conceptual shift
in 3D modeling. Instead of reconstructing geometry explicitly, the representation itself is
optimized to fit the real world through continuous and differentiable processes. This reformulation not only unifies the traditionally separate steps of perception and reconstruction
but also enables gradients to flow across them, allowing models to be trained jointly for
both geometry and semantics. In the context of embodied AI, such differentiable pipelines
are particularly appealing, as they provide a unified representation through which agents
can perceive, reason, and act within a coherent, continuously learned 3D scene model
derived directly from 2D visual observations.
2.2
Neural Radiance Fields Fundamentals
In this section, we explain the operation of the Neural Radiance Field (NeRF) [120] 3D
representation. We begin with the fundamentals of NeRF, outlining how those continuous
scene representations are learned and rendered. We then describe advances in grid-based
encodings that crucially improve efficiency and scalability, followed by SDF-based extensions that enhance geometric fidelity. We also discuss common challenges encountered
when deploying these methods in real-world applications and some respective solutions.
Finally, we describe some datasets, metrics, frameworks and basic baselines commonly
15
CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND
Figure 2.3: Overview of the NeRF pipeline. To optimize a NeRF, we first render rays from
supervision images and sample points across the rays. Each point is represented with a
3D coordinate and a ray direction (a). Those are fed to the NeRF model, an MLP, which
will predict for each point a density and an RGB color (b). Using volumetric rendering,
we can combine the samples to obtain per-ray colors (c), which will be used to optimize
the MLP by computing the loss between the groundtruth pixel values and the predicted
ray colors (d). Figure is from [120].
used when working with NeRF and 3D multi-view reconstruction and interpretation, that
will be used all throughout this thesis.
2.2.1
Core Formulation
Overview. Neural Radiance Fields (NeRFs) [120], illustrated in Figure 2.3 represent a 3D
scene as a continuous volumetric function. The key idea is to encode a radiance field that
jointly describes color and density at every point in space. Formally, the model defines a
mapping:
𝐹Θ (x, d) −→ (c, 𝜎),
(2.1)
where Θ are the parameters needed to describe the field, x = (𝑥, 𝑦, 𝑧) is a 3D location,
d = (𝜃, 𝜙) is the viewing direction, c = (𝑟, 𝑔, 𝑏) is the emitted color, and 𝜎 is the volume
density. This model can then be sampled at any 3D location to obtain the corresponding
radiance values. NeRF are called implicit 3D representations as they do not store the scene in
an explicit manner but rather allows arbitrary querying of the implicit model. In practice,
in the original work, 𝐹Θ is approximated by a multi-layer perceptron (MLP), also called
artificial neural network, optimized and overfit specifically for each scene through gradient
descent using posed multi-view images of the 3D scene.
While NeRF are dense 3D representations, one very important aspect of neural fields
is their ability to synthesise photo-realistic renders (i.e. 2D images) of its scene from
any user-defined point of view (both camera position and view direction) using volume
rendering.
16
2.2. NEURAL RADIANCE FIELDS FUNDAMENTALS
Novel View Synthesis. From a NeRF, we are able to produce a 2D image given the
corresponding camera parameters (extrinsics and intrinsics) by using ray casting and
differentiable volume rendering techniques. Let’s say we want to synthesize an image
from a novel viewpoint defined by known camera parameters. Each pixel in the image
corresponds to a camera ray 𝑟(𝑡) emitted from the camera center through the pixel in
the camera viewing direction and parameterized by depth 𝑡. The color of this pixel is
obtained by integrating the radiance accumulated along that ray through the continuous
volumetric field predicted by the NeRF model. For a camera ray 𝑟(𝑡) = o + 𝑡d, with origin
o and direction d, the predicted color is computed as

(2.2)
where 𝜎(𝑟(𝑡)) and 𝑐(𝑟(𝑡), d) are the density and color at point 𝑟(𝑡), respectively. The
term 𝑇(𝑡) is the transmittance, i.e., the probability that a ray travels from 𝑡 𝑛 to 𝑡 without
encountering occlusion:

The accuracy of this approximation depends heavily on the sampling strategy used
along each ray. The original NeRF formulation employs a two-stage hierarchical sampling
scheme: a coarse network first samples points uniformly through the ray to estimate a
rough density distribution, and a fine network then resamples additional points according
to the learned importance distribution.
Subsequent works have introduced more efficient and stable sampling strategies for
large or unbounded scenes. Mip-NeRF 360 [10] notably replaces fixed hierarchical sampling with multiple smaller per-scene proposal networks that predict per-ray density
distributions, also enabling adaptive sample allocation where it is most needed.
Finally, the full image is obtained by aggregating the predicted colors of all pixels, each
computed from its corresponding ray. This rendering process not only enables novel-view
synthesis but also serves as the basis for training NeRF models by comparing synthesized
and ground-truth images as discussed next.
17
CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND
Figure 2.4: View-Dependency and Positional Encoding. To obtain photo-realistic renders,
NeRFs add view dependency and positional encoding to the base architecture. View
Dependency enables the modelling of radiance instead of color, thus allowing notably
reflecting effects. Positional encoding improves reconstruction of high frequencies in the
scene, i.e. small-scale details. Figure is from [120].
Training. NeRFs are scene-specific, meaning that each model is trained to represent a
single scene and must be optimized individually for it. As mentioned earlier, this optimization relies on multi-view images of the scene and their associated camera poses,
leveraging the same differentiable rendering process used for novel-view synthesis. During training, rays are randomly cast from all input images using their known camera
parameters (see Figure 2.3). For each pixel, the rendered color 𝐶(𝑟) obtained from volume
rendering is compared to the ground-truth observed RGB value. The network parameters
Θ are optimized by minimizing the mean squared error (MSE) across batches of sampled
rays. Over time, this supervision encourages the synthesized views to reproduce the
captured images, progressively encoding both the scene geometry (through densities) and
appearance (through radiance) into the network weights.
View-Dependency. The original NeRF formulation explicitly models view-dependent
appearance to reproduce non-Lambertian effects such as specular reflections and glossy
surfaces. The radiance field is decomposed into two components: a view-independent
density 𝜎(x), which encodes the scene geometry, and a color term 𝑐(x, d) that depends on
both the spatial position x and the per-camera viewing direction d:
𝐹Θ (x, d) = (𝜎(x), c(x, d)).
(2.6)
As shown in Figure 2.4, this conditioning on the direction d enables the model
to synthesize viewpoint-dependent effects such as reflections, refractions, or shading
variations. In contrast, a purely view-independent color prediction would only capture
Lambertian surfaces, leading to flat and unrealistic renderings. By incorporating viewing
direction, NeRF produces photorealistic images that better match the complex appearance
changes observed in real-world scenes.
18
2.2. NEURAL RADIANCE FIELDS FUNDAMENTALS
Figure 2.5: Overview of the Grid-Based NeRF pipeline. Rather than using a large MLP,
Instant-NGP introduces multi-resolution learnable hashgrids which provide for each 3D
point a feature as the concatenation of each grid. This feature is then fed to a small MLP
called decoder to predict both density and color. Figure is from [124].
Positional encoding. A standard MLP has limited ability to capture high-frequency
variations, which for a NeRF results in overly smooth reconstructions with loss of fine
detail. To overcome this, NeRF employs positional encodings that map input coordinates
to a higher-dimensional feature space. For the spatial coordinates x, Fourier feature
mapping is used:
𝛾(x) = sin(20 𝜋x), cos(20 𝜋x), . . . , sin(2𝐿−1 𝜋x), cos(2𝐿−1 𝜋x) ,

(2.7)
where L controls the number of frequency bands.
For the viewing direction d, low-order spherical harmonics [122] are used to encode
angular dependencies. Spherical harmonics form an orthonormal basis over the sphere
and provide a compact way to represent smooth angular functions. This makes them
well-suited for capturing view-dependent effects, such as specular reflections.
The radiance field can then be expressed as:
′
𝐹Θ = 𝐹Θ
◦ (𝛾(x), SH(d)),
(2.8)
′
where 𝐹Θ
is the MLP function, 𝛾(x) encodes the 3D spatial coordinates, and 𝑆𝐻(d)
encodes the view direction.
This dual encoding allows the network to capture both fine geometric details and
smooth view-dependent variations, mitigating the low-pass bias of MLPs and enabling
high-quality, photorealistic renderings, as illustrated in Figure 2.4.
2.2.2
Grid-Based NeRF Representations
To represent complex 3D scenes, original Vanilla NeRFs rely on a large and deep multilayer
perceptron (MLP) to approximate the radiance field function defined in equation (2.1).
This design is highly expressive but comes at a significant computation cost. First, training
and rendering are expensive, as each forward pass cost suffers from the large size of the
MLP. More importantly, during optimization specifically, any MLP’s weights parameters
19
CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND
update impacts the whole reconstruction, which slows convergence and may even lead to
instabilities during training depending on the spatial distribution of sampled points.
To overcome these issues, Instant Neural Graphics Primitives (Instant-NGP) [124],
illustrated in Figure 2.5, proposed a more efficient encoding scheme based on multiresolution learnable hashgrids. The key idea is to replace the large MLP with a compact
structure that explicitly encodes spatial information. Concretely, the scene is partitioned
into a hierarchy of 𝑖 3D grids 𝐺 𝑖 of increasing resolutions. Each vertex of a grid is
assigned to a trainable embedding vector, which is optimized directly during training
via gradient descent. For any 3D query point, features are retrieved from each grid by
trilinear interpolation of the surrounding embeddings. The concatenated features across
all resolutions are then passed through a much shallower MLP called decoder, which
predicts color and density.
Although this scheme shares some similarity with voxel-based representations (since
both discretize space into a regular grid), it differs in two crucial ways. First, the interpolation across grid vertices (combined with the decoder) enables a continuous representation,
avoiding the blocky artifacts typical of voxel grids. Second, the learned embeddings
act as a compressed latent code rather than storing explicit occupancy or color values,
which enables the representation to be significantly more detailed and expressive. This is
why, although Instant-NGP adds explicit partitioning of the scene, it is still considered an
implicit representation.
To further address the memory bottleneck of storing multiple high-resolution feature
grids, Instant-NGP employs a multi-resolution hash encoding. Instead of assigning a
unique feature vector to every grid vertex, a per-resolution fixed-size hashing table is
used, and vertex indices are mapped to this table via a hash function. This strategy allows
embeddings to be shared across vertices, drastically reducing memory consumption while
still preserving the ability to capture fine detail.
Compared to the original Neural Radiance Fields, this design provides two major
efficiency benefits. First, each forward pass is much faster using this architecture in
contrast to the very deep MLP. More importantly, the representation becomes more locally
constrained: apart from the shallow decoder, only the embeddings associated with the
neighborhood of a sampled point are updated during training, in contrast to global weight
updates in vanilla NeRF. Together, these factors yield significant improvements in both
training time (reducing convergence from several hours to a few minutes per scene) and
rendering speed, making NeRFs far more practical for large-scale or real-time applications.
Other existing variants were introduced later on, with various types of scene decompositions (e.g. tri-planes, ...). However, those methods received less interest from the
community and will not be further mentioned in this thesis.
20
2.2. NEURAL RADIANCE FIELDS FUNDAMENTALS
Figure 2.6: Signed Distance Function Explanation. This figure illustrates the principle of
an SDF representation of the Stanford Bunny model. (a) depicts the underlying implicit
surface SDF = 0, (b) a 2D cross-section of the signed distance field and (c) the rendered
3D surface recovered from SDF = 0. Figure is from [133]
2.2.3
SDF-based Neural Fields
Due to the purely 2D supervision, one disadvantage of NeRF representations is that
their geometry estimation can be rather inaccurate, especially regarding details on object
surfaces. While such imprecision may have limited impact on view-synthesis quality (as
the rendering process inherently projects these geometric ambiguities back into the 2D
domain), the 3D reconstruction itself may often contain many holes and surface errors.
This is problematic for most 3D downstream applications that require accurate surfaces.
In particular, modern industrial reconstruction pipelines often convert NeRF into explicit
3D representations such as meshes, where surface inaccuracies translate directly into
geometric errors that harm precision-sensitive applications. Additionally, in embodied
AI, accurate surface mapping is crucial. From navigation to semantic scene interpretation,
the agent’s ability to function relies on geometric fidelity: if the reconstruction of a chair
is fragmented, the agent can neither reliably recognize it nor interact with it safely.
Several strategies have been proposed to improve surface quality, ranging from regularization terms to depth-guided supervision. A prominent research direction [130, 196, 177,
95, 179] has focused on representing geometry by combining NeRF with Signed Distance
Functions (SDFs), which provide a more explicit and smooth formulation of surfaces.
Formally, a signed distance function is a 3D representation based on a continuous
scalar field
𝑠 : R3 → R,
where for each 3D point x ∈ R3 , the function value 𝑠(x) gives the distance to the closest
surface, with the sign conventionally negative inside the object and positive outside. The
surface itself is thus defined as the zero-level set {x | 𝑠(x) = 0}.
In SDF-based neural fields, instead of predicting a density 𝜎(x) directly, the network
predicts an SDF value 𝑠(x), which is then converted into a density to enable volumetric
rendering. Different conversion functions have been proposed. For instance, VolSDF [196]
21
CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND
converts SDF values into densities via a Laplace cumulative distribution function, while
NeuS [177, 179] uses the logistic distribution to better approximate surface rendering.
These mappings provide a differentiable way to concentrate densities near the zero-level
surface, leading to sharper reconstructions.
Because NeRF do not supervise empty areas by design (samples in empty areas have
null contributions in the volume rendering formula), training SDF-based fields requires
additional supervision to enforce valid distance-function properties everywhere in the
scene. The most common approach is the Eikonal loss, which enforces the gradient of the
SDF to have unit norm for every sample:
h
ℒeik = Ex ∥∇x 𝑠(x)∥2 − 1
2i
.
This regularization ensures that the predicted SDF behaves consistently as a true distance
field, thus improving the geometry and stabilizing training.
Beyond the basic formulations, a variety of improvements have been proposed to
further enhance reconstruction quality and training stability. Some contributions focus on
smoothing the reconstructed surfaces through regularization. For example, Neuralangelo
[95] introduces a curvature-based ℒ2 regularization loss and leverages analytic derivatives of the SDF for finer gradient control when computing the Eikonal loss. Others
focus on improving supervision or optimization strategies, such as coarse-to-fine training
schemes [95] or auxiliary depth and normal supervision [199].
Overall, SDF-based NeRFs constitute a powerful line of research for achieving accurate
and consistent surface reconstructions, offering a stronger geometric prior than densitybased formulations while maintaining compatibility with neural volumetric rendering
frameworks. Nevertheless, SDF-based NeRFs are generally harder to train and more
prone to instabilities than density-based formulations, which explains why most NeRF
variants continue to rely on density prediction despite the geometric benefits of SDFs. In
practise, SDF NeRFs are mostly used for specific use cases which needs precise surface
reconstruction. In the following, density NeRF will be used unless specified otherwise;
however, the concepts discussed throughout this thesis also apply in principle to SDFbased variants.
2.2.4
Common Challenges in Real-World Scenarios
The original NeRF formulation was mostly demonstrated on isolated objects or restricted
indoor scenes under controlled conditions. Extending these methods to real-world scenarios introduces several challenges that must be explicitly addressed, ranging from adapting
to large-scale scenes to handling imperfect data. We highlight below the main issues and
representative research directions.
Large-Scale and Outdoor Scenes. Neural fields are well suited for restricted indoor and
object-centric scenes, but they struggle to represent large environments such as unbounded
22
2.2. NEURAL RADIANCE FIELDS FUNDAMENTALS
outdoor settings. Since the radiance field is usually defined over a limited volume, naively
applying the formulation to large areas by scaling the scene to fit this volume leads to
poor reconstructions and distortions, not to mention issues with representing infinitely
far areas (e.g., the sky).
To address this, most methods separate the 3D scene into two parts, the region of
interest (ROI) at center of the scene which needs to be reconstructed with great care, and
the background part. To perform this separation, several methods propose alternative
space parameterizations that compactify the unbounded background 3D space into a
manageable domain adjacent to the ROI. NeRF++ [204], for example, introduces an
inside/outside split by combining a bounded NeRF for the near field with a second radiance
field defined over the exterior of a unit sphere (which is contracted into a finite space).
MipNeRF360 [10] further improves large-scale reconstruction by applying smoother scene
contraction, making it possible to represent unbounded outdoor environments while
maintaining continuity and resolution inside a single NeRF.
However, when ROI of the scenes become extremely large (such as city blocks or very
long trajectories), single-field representations might become impractical. Block-NeRF [163]
addresses this by partitioning the environment into spatially localized NeRF, which are
trained and rendered independently but combined for global consistency. This strategy
significantly reduces memory and computational demands while scaling to kilometerscale reconstructions. Another family of solution [201, 47] relies on proposing dynamically
allocating model capacity for grid-based solutions by using octrees and expendables grids
around task-relevant areas, improving both efficiency and reconstruction quality.
Overall, these advances extend NeRF from controlled, object-centric scenes to largescale in-the-wild environments, paving the way for real-life applications.
Distance of Observation and Aliasing. The original NeRF formulation implicitly assumes a constant observation distance from the scene. In practice, however, the apparent
footprint of a pixel on a surface increases with distance, which is not reflected in the
standard rendering process. Since NeRF estimates color and density at discrete sample
points along each ray, without accounting for the varying spatial extent represented by
each pixel, renderings often suffer from aliasing artifacts when synthesizing views at
scales or distances different from those seen during training. This limitation, highlighted
in Mip-NeRF [11], arises from the absence of a mechanism that adapts the image synthesis
to varying observation distances.
To address this issue, several works have introduced Level of Detail (LOD) mechanisms into NeRF representations, allowing a scene to be represented at multiple spatial
resolutions simultaneously. From those representations, aliasing artifacts are removed by
sampling coarser LODs as the camera distance increases. These methods build LOD-aware
latent spaces by explicitly conditioning the NeRF input on the desired scale, ensuring that
the network retrieves features appropriate for the sampling resolution [11, 188, 171, 12, 62,
213]. While such formulations improve visual consistency and robustness across scales,
23
CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND
they often require explicit supervision for each LOD, making generalization to unseen
observation distances challenging.
A complementary line of work foregoes explicit LOD representations and instead
focuses on improving the sampling process itself by modeling volumes rather than
points during ray integration, thus aggregating latent features over continuous regions
of space [11, 62, 12]. By accounting for the continuous nature of pixel footprints, these
techniques effectively mitigate aliasing, albeit at the cost of increased computational and
memory overhead.
Few-View Reconstruction. By construction, NeRF is subject to the shape–radiance ambiguity [204]. When supervision from multiple viewpoints is sparse, the optimization
process can easily overfit the observed views while failing to produce geometrically consistent reconstructions or generalize to unseen ones. This challenge is particularly critical
in real-world settings where dense multi-view captures are impractical or impossible.
We can distinguish two types of solutions. A first family of approaches seeks to
mitigate this issue through regularization terms that guide the optimization toward geometrically plausible solutions. Geometric priors such as smoothness or normal consistency
constraints help stabilize the learned density field and reduce overfitting [129, 199, 185].
Other works instead leverage semantic regularization to enforce cross-view consistency
[65], promoting reconstructions that align with high-level scene structure.
The second family of solutions relies on a progressive reconstruction of the details of
the scene, as introduced in FreeNeRF [193] and Nerfies [135]. Restraining the ability of
the model to reconstruct a complex scene at early stages enforces the consistency of the
reconstruction over the different supervision viewpoints. This presents the advantage of
bringing stability while keeping a fairly simple training process.
Imperfect Camera Poses. Accurate camera parameters are a strong requirement for NeRF
training, regarding both intrinsics (e.g., focal length, principal point) and extrinsics (i.e.,
6DoF poses). In standard offline workflows, datasets often rely on Structure-from-Motion
(SfM) pipelines like COLMAP [153, 154] to estimate parameters a posteriori. Similarly, in
online embodied AI scenarios, agents typically track their pose incrementally via Visual
Odometry or SLAM, which inherently accumulate drift and noise. Regardless of the source
(SfM or SLAM), these imperfect estimates can break multi-view consistency and lead to
severe reconstruction artifacts. To mitigate this, several methods jointly refine camera
parameters and radiance field weights during training. For instance, BARF [98] optimizes
extrinsics only, while NeRF– [181], SC-NeRF [67], NoPE-NeRF [16], and CamP [134] also
refine intrinsics, achieving improved robustness to noisy or incomplete camera calibration.
Dynamic Lighting and Appearance Variability. Real-world image collections often
exhibit inconsistent lighting, transient objects, or even weather variations between captures.
These factors violate the assumptions of the original NeRF, which presumes static geometry
24
2.2. NEURAL RADIANCE FIELDS FUNDAMENTALS
and constant appearance. NeRF-in-the-wild [115] and its successors [24, 155, 119] address
this by disentangling scene content from appearance factors, enabling reconstructions
that are robust to variable illumination and transient occluders common in unstructured
image collections. In practice, they introduce learnable per-image latent appearance
codes, which are taken as input of the network alongside the samples, to explain perview variability such as lighting, tone, or transient content. This formulation allows the
core geometry and consistent aspects of the scene to be reconstructed, while residual
factors are absorbed by the appearance codes. An interesting consequence of this is the
possibility to render views of the scene with varying lighting and/or weather by exploring
the corresponding learnt appearance latent spaces. Extensions such as GRAF [155], HaNeRF [24], or RawNeRF [119] further refine this idea, for instance by explicitly modeling
shadows, exposure, or weather conditions.
Incremental Reconstruction. In many real-world NeRF reconstruction scenarios, the
input images are provided sequentially. For instance, in embodied AI, autonomous
agents reconstruct their environment progressively as they navigate and capture the scene.
Moreover, such scenarios often involve exploring large environments, resulting in a high
number of captured images with high memory usage, thus requiring to discard older
frames. In this online incremental reconstruction paradigm, the model must integrate new
viewpoints as they arrive, while avoiding the important issue of catastrophic forgetting,
i.e., the tendency of neural networks to overwrite previously learned information when
updated with new data.
With vanilla NeRF, this issue is severe: every optimization step updates the shared
MLP weights globally, which can cause previously reconstructed regions which are not
supervised anymore to degrade as we supervise novel views within new areas. Gridbased representations partially mitigate this issue thanks to their spatially localized feature
storage. Nevertheless, forgetting still occurs both due to the decoder and the hashgrid
dictionary. A common strategy to partly address this limitation is to employ pre-trained,
scene-agnostic decoders that remain frozen during the feature grids optimization [212].
This design enables new grid cells to represent unseen areas while preserving the integrity
of already reconstructed regions.
In summary, adapting NeRF to real-world scenarios requires tackling inherent challenges of imperfect data, large-scale scenes and incremental reconstruction. These research
directions collectively aim to close the gap between the ideal conditions of benchmarks
and the complexity of in-the-wild visual data.
2.2.5
NeRF in this Thesis
In the previous sections, we discussed Neural Radiance Fields (NeRFs) from a conceptual
and methodological perspective. In this section, we adopt a more practical view, detailing
the key implementation components, evaluation protocols, and datasets that form the
25
CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND
Figure 2.7: Overview of the nerfacto pipeline. The model uses many additional improvements to the base Instant-NGP, notably appearance embeddings, camera poses refinement
and proposal sampler networks. Figure is from [164].
experimental foundation of this thesis. Unless stated otherwise, all experiments and
analyses are built upon the Nerfstudio framework, using its Nerfacto pipeline as the
default reconstruction baseline.
Frameworks. Nerfstudio [164] is a modular and open-source Python framework built
upon the PyTorch library, designed for the development, training, and evaluation of
NeRF-based methods. To combine flexibility with high computational efficiency, it integrates tiny-cuda-nn [123], a highly optimized C++/CUDA library used to accelerate
the core components of the architecture, specifically the feature hash grids, MLPs, and
various positional encodings. Nerfstudio provides a unified interface for data preprocessing, model configuration, optimization, and rendering, supporting a wide range of
architectures and datasets. Its modular design enables rapid prototyping and systematic
comparison between different approaches, while ensuring reproducibility through standardized evaluation protocols. All NeRF models and extensions proposed in this thesis
were implemented within Nerfstudio. In particular, all experiments involving SDF–based
NeRFs were developed and evaluated using SDFStudio [200], an extension of Nerfstudio
focused on implementing several SDF methods, which follows the same modular and
reproducible pipeline.
Nerfacto. Nerfacto, the default baseline model in Nerfstudio, integrates several advances
from recent NeRF literature into a single, efficient pipeline for in-the-wild reconstruction.
As illustrated in Fig. 2.7, it combines a hash-grid encoding [124] for compact and expressive
scene representation, Mip-NeRF 360 [10] proposal sampling for efficient ray sampling,
and several secondary improvements. These include the distortion loss from Mip-NeRF
360 to stabilize convergence, scene contraction to handle unbounded scenes, camera poses
refinement and appearance embeddings to account for illumination variations across
input images. Its robustness, efficiency, and ease of use make it an ideal baseline for the
methods developed in this thesis.
Evaluation Metrics. Following standard NeRF evaluation protocols, most reconstruction
and interpretation metrics are computed by comparing rendered views with ground-truth
images from held-out test viewpoints. Regarding novel view synthesis (reconstruction
