Datasets. Our experiments leverage a variety of established benchmarks to evaluate
reconstruction quality and generalization. The Mip-NeRF 360 [10] and Tanks and Temples [81] datasets provide high-resolution, densely covered, unbounded outdoor and
indoor scenes for view-synthesis evaluation. ScanNet [29] and Replica [160] offer richly
semantically annotated indoor environments from natural cameras trajectories, making
them ideal for reconstruction and segmentation tasks in embodied AI. Finally, DTU [66]
proposes multi-view front-facing captures of various real-world object. While unused
in this thesis, there exists many other existing newer large-scale datasets such as ScanNet++ [198], DL3DV-10K [99], and the Nerfstudio dataset collection [164]. Together, these
datasets provide a comprehensive basis for studying both geometric and semantic aspects
of NeRF representations across diverse embodied scenarios.
2.3
Interpretation in 2D Vision
Although embodied agents ultimately require semantic understanding of full 3D environments, we first consider the 2D setting, where many of the core ideas of 3D NeRF
interpretation originated. Interpreting 2D visual information has been a central challenge
of computer vision since its inception. At its core, interpretation in 2D vision consists of
transforming raw pixel arrays (i.e. images) into meaningful abstractions that can support
object understanding and high-level reasoning. This process has almost always relied on
the ability to extract and represent features: numerical descriptors that capture relevant
aspects of the visual world, such as texture, shape, or semantics. The history of 2D vision
can therefore be read as the story of evolving feature representations, from handcrafted
descriptors to deep learned embeddings, and more recently to foundation models trained
at unprecedented scale. In this section, we first trace this trajectory of representations,
before turning to the major families of interpretation tasks they enable. Finally, we analyze
the shortcomings of the 2D vision paradigm to interpret complex 3D scenes notably for
embodied AI applications.
2.3.1
Foundations of Visual Representations: Encoding Images into Features
The history of 2D vision can be understood as a continuous effort to design or learn
increasingly powerful features that enable machines to interpret images. Features are
descriptors which represent visual information of an image. Those can be of varying
scales (i.e. dense per-pixel features, per-group of pixels, also called patches, or per-image)
and can be obtained in many different ways. Specifically, the features gradually became
more complex and abstract as the methods and models to extract those descriptors also
grew more complex.
First, early computer vision relied on handcrafted descriptors such as SIFT [111], SURF
[13], or HOG [31], which performed pre-defined computations on neighboring pixels of an
image. These features captured local geometric and photometric cues like corners, edges,
28
2.3. INTERPRETATION IN 2D VISION
and textures, and were specifically engineered to be robust to specific transformations
such as scale or rotation. While unexpectedly effective compared to their simplicity, their
utility was limited for low-level shape recognition and inter-images correspondence tasks,
as they lacked semantic meaning and context, making them impossible to scale toward a
more complex or open-ended interpretation.
The rise of deep learning marked a turning point in the interpretation of images,
opening the way to true semantic downstream tasks. Convolutional Neural Networks
(CNNs) [84] introduced the idea of learning optimal features from annotated image
datasets by optimizing the parameters of local pixel-processing operations, rather than
relying on hand-engineered, sub-optimal formulas. By stacking and combining these
learnable layers into deeper architectures known as backbones [84, 58, 151, 162], they
emerged as powerful models for extracting informative features from images. Trained on
large-scale image datasets [32, 41, 14], these networks learn hierarchical representations
that progressively capture low-level cues such as edges or textures to high-level semantic
concepts such as object categories. Their output typically consists of high-dimensional
features grouped into feature maps (2D grids whose resolutions are lower than that of the
input images due to the convolutions) or into global per-image embeddings. This shift
enabled breakthroughs in more complex semantic tasks such as classification, detection,
and segmentation, laying the foundation for modern computer vision.
Building on this success, Vision Transformers (ViTs) [35] demonstrated that attentionbased mechanisms could model long-range dependencies and global context more effectively than CNNs, leading to more performant backbones [144, 110] and more expressive
and flexible visual embeddings.
The scaling of those deep architectures, combined with the appearance of many massive
and diverse datasets, gave birth to very large, powerful and generic models, which we
call foundation models [19, 132, 70, 159, 80, 146, 142, 25, 202, 50, 145, 150]. Those models
can encode images into very complex and high-dimensional embeddings, each with their
own specificities and downstream applications. For instance, DINO and its follow-ups [19,
132, 159] propose ways to scale up the number of used images without any semantic
annotations (self-supervised paradigm) for multi-tasks feature maps while SAM [80, 146]
specializes on encoding segmentation-focused features.
Another line of works studied multi-modal foundation models and especially imagetext combined modalities. Notably, CLIP (and its following works) [142, 25, 202, 50, 145]
learned to align images and text encodings in a shared embedding space using datasets
of image-textual caption pairs. By bridging these modalities, such models have unlocked
open-vocabulary understanding, enabling systems to interpret and respond to arbitrary
language queries about visual content.
These foundation models illustrate how large-scale pretraining can produce versatile,
reusable features that generalize beyond any single benchmark, fundamentally changing
the way visual representations can be applied.
29
CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND
Figure 2.8: The Evolution of Downstream Tasks. We present examples of tasks covering
both pixel and textual outputs.
2.3.2
From Features to Interpretation: Addressing Known and Unknown
Tasks
Having discussed how visual representations evolved from handcrafted features to deep
multimodal embeddings, we now turn to how these representations are used to interpret
images. In computer vision, interpretation refers to the process of extracting structured
meaning from pixels in order to understand what objects are present, where they are
located, and how they relate to one another or to a broader context. Historically, following
the increase of the features complexity, this field has progressed from rigid, fixed-label
systems to flexible, open-ended reasoning engines. This evolution aligns perfectly with the
dual requirements of an embodied agent: the need for reliable perception of tasks explicitly
defined at design time (for self-functioning), i.e. Known Tasks, and the adaptability to
handle user-driven requests that remain unknown until deployment (Unknown Tasks).
Some downstream tasks example are illustrated in Figure 2.8.
Interpretation for Known Tasks: Fixed-Category Perception. At the foundational level,
interpretation focuses on recognizing a pre-defined set of concepts essential for the system’s
routine operation. In these "closed-set" paradigms, deep learning models are trained to
classify, detect, or segment objects from a fixed vocabulary defined during training (e.g.,
the 80 classes of the COCO dataset or the standard categories of CIFAR-10) [121, 69],
without the need to adapt to dynamic, user-specified queries. The pipeline is generally
consistent across those tasks: feature maps extracted from the backbone, as the foundation
to interpret an image, serve as the input for a lightweight task-specific neural network,
usually called "decoder" or "head", and learn on a small amount of annotated data to map
those features to the class labels.
For an embodied agent, this form of interpretation serves the Functional Core. It
provides passive perception: a constant, reliable stream of semantic information required
30
2.3. INTERPRETATION IN 2D VISION
for self-functioning tasks such as navigation (distinguishing "floor" from "obstacle"),
affordance detection, or basic object manipulation. Because the categories are known a
priori, these models can be highly optimized for speed and stability, acting as the agent’s
semantic reflexes.
Interpretation for Unknown Tasks: Query-Driven Focus. However, a robot operating
in human environments must also respond to requests that cannot be anticipated during
training. This moves interpretation into the domain of Unknown Tasks, where the target
concepts are open-vocabulary. The first tier of this flexibility is query-driven focus. Instead
of predicting all possible labels, models leverage multi-modal alignment to compare visual
features against arbitrary text queries (e.g., CLIP [142], OpenSeg [25]). This enables many
open-vocabulary downstream tasks, such as detection, segmentation, image retrieval and
classification. Similarly, promptable segmentation models like SAM [80] allow users to
define a target by pointing at them (e.g. clicking) for instance segmentation. SAM2 [146],
its follow-up, extends the idea to videos, enabling long-term interactive object tracking.
In the context of Embodied AI, this capability acts as a local cognitive tool. It shifts
perception from passive pre-defined monitoring to active task execution conditioned on
user intent. When a user asks "Find my keys," the agent uses these open-vocabulary tools
to dynamically filter the scene, focusing its attention solely on the regions relevant to the
immediate user query.
Interpretation for Unknown Tasks: Global Reasoning.
Finally, addressing unknown
tasks often requires more than just localizing specific objects based on keywords; it
demands a holistic understanding of the scene to answer open-ended questions, explain
visual content, or reason about complex spatial and semantic relationships. This capability
stems from the development of Vision–Language Models (VLMs) [92, 91, 2, 103, 89], which
align visual and textual representations in a shared embedding space to be processed by
Large Language Models (LLMs). In such architectures, visual features, typically obtained
from pre-trained 2D foundation models like CLIP, are projected as "visual tokens" into
the same latent domain as encoded textual inputs. Recent systems such as BLIP-2 [92, 91],
Flamingo [2], and LLaVA [103, 89] demonstrate this capability, integrating frozen visual
backbones with pre-trained LLMs to achieve zero-shot understanding and multi-modal
conversation.
For an embodied agent, this serves as the global cognitive core. It enables the robot
to answer abstract questions ("Is this room safe for a baby?") or plan long-horizon tasks
("Clean up the table") by interpreting the visual scene holistically. This layer bridges the
gap between seeing the world and thinking about it, allowing the agent to formulate plans
based on a deep, contextual understanding of its surroundings.
31
CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND
2.3.3
Limitations of 2D-Only Representations for Embodied AI
Despite the growing interest in 3D perception, many embodied AI systems still rely
primarily on two-dimensional vision. Using 2D sensors, typically RGB cameras, remains
the most accessible and cost-effective way to perceive the world. This simplicity has
made 2D-based approaches a natural foundation for many interactive agents, especially
when combined with recent advances in deep learning and large multimodal models.
They allow robots to benefit directly from the progress of image-based perception and
reasoning models, without requiring expensive 3D sensors or intermediary geometric
reconstruction.
These 2D-based strategies span multiple levels of embodied intelligence. Some approaches [39] infer task-relevant representations of the visual environment, delegating
decision-making to a separate cognitive or planning module. Others operate in a fully
end-to-end manner, predicting decisions directly from raw RGB inputs. Depending on
the framework, these outputs may take the form of high-level textual instructions for a
robot [17] or even low-level motor commands [18, 68], particularly with the recent emergence of vision-language-action (VLA) models [113]. Together, these examples illustrate
that 2D perception remains a powerful and versatile interface connecting vision, language,
and action.
However, despite their versatility, 2D representations remain fundamentally constrained by two intrinsic limitations when applied to embodied agents: the lack of explicit
geometry and, crucially, the lack of structured temporal persistency.
The Geometry Gap: Ambiguity and Viewpoint Dependency. First, a single 2D view is
merely a projection of a three-dimensional scene. Multiple distinct 3D configurations can
produce the same 2D image, introducing inherent geometric ambiguity regarding depth,
occlusion, and object volumes. This makes it difficult for agents to reason about physical
interactions or spatial layout. Furthermore, features extracted from one viewpoint rarely
generalize across novel perspectives. As an agent moves, the 2D appearance of an object
changes drastically, leading to inconsistent semantic interpretations unless grounded in a
unified 3D structure [168, 149].
The Persistence Gap: On-the-fly Processing vs. Map-Based Reasoning. Second, and
perhaps more critical for long-term autonomy, is the issue of memory. Pure 2D approaches
typically process the environment as a continuous data stream of observations. The agent
reacts to the current frame or a finite context window of recent frames. This streambased paradigm struggles with global context: if a user queries the agent about a room
visited ten minutes ago (e.g., "Where did you leave the keys?"), the model must either
search through its entire video history, which scales poorly in terms of compute and
latency, or risk catastrophic forgetting. In contrast, a 3D reconstruction acts as a persistent,
structured memory. By integrating observations into a spatial map, the agent aggregates
32
2.4. 3D NERF INTERPRETATION
information over time, resolving inconsistencies and occlusion. This allows the agent to
query the representation at any moment regarding any location without needing to retain
or re-process the raw sensory stream [209].
These challenges motivate the development of methods that reason directly in 3D
space. Yet, while 3D data promises richer geometric and semantic understanding, it
also introduces new costs: acquiring dense geometry or semantic annotations is often
expensive and impractical. In this context, image-based 3D representations such as
Neural Radiance Fields (NeRFs) offer a promising middle ground. NeRFs reconstruct
3D structure directly from 2D observations, alleviating the limitations of purely 2D
models while retaining compatibility with 2D annotations and pretrained vision–language
representations. In the following, we explore how NeRF-based 3D representations enable
richer scene understanding and serve as a natural bridge between 2D perception and
embodied 3D reasoning.
2.4
3D NeRF Interpretation
While 2D vision provides powerful tools for image understanding, it remains inherently
limited for interpreting three-dimensional scenes. These limitations directly affect how
embodied agents can perceive, reason, and act within real-world environments.
Among existing 3D paradigms, Neural Radiance Fields (NeRFs) offer a particularly
compelling foundation. Learned directly from multi-view imagery, they encode a continuous volumetric representation of a scene that can be either interpreted directly or enriched
with semantic information.
In the context of embodied AI, interpreting NeRFs enables agents to maintain a
coherent and semantically grounded model of their surroundings, supporting both passive
scene understanding and active task execution. In this section, we review the main
approaches to 3D NeRF interpretation, first focusing on direct neural field interpretation
and then on the integration of 2D feature representations within the radiance field. Finally,
we conclude this background section by discussing how interpreting Neural Radiance
Fields connects to Embodied AI and Spatial AI.
2.4.1
Interpreting Radiance Fields as 3D Representations
While originally designed for photorealistic novel-view synthesis, a NeRF is fundamentally
a 3D representation that implicitly encodes both geometry and appearance. Beyond
rendering, its continuous volumetric formulation offers compelling properties for 3D
scene understanding. Consequently, a growing body of work explores how to interpret
NeRFs directly as 3D representations without taking advantage of NeRF differentiability
for scene-specific fine-tuning. In this context, we refer to generalizable semantic NeRF any
method capable of extracting semantic information from any pre-trained NeRF using a
single forward pass.
33
CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND
One line of work leverages hypernetwork-based approaches, which process raw Vanilla
NeRF network weights to function as backbones and produce holistic scene-level embeddings useful for interpretation. For instance, NeRF2Vec [143, 8] encodes a NeRF’s MLP
parameters into a compact vector representation usable for downstream tasks like scene
classification or retrieval. LLaNA [3] extends this idea by aligning those embeddings with
language representations for LLM processing, enabling open-ended questions over 3D
scenes. More recently, weight2space [9] produces spatial tokens from NeRF weights to be
interpreted by a fine-tuned LLaNA. However, all these hypernetworks methods remain
limited to Vanilla NeRF processing, thus operating on single-object or very small-scale
scenes, and limiting fine-grained spatial reasoning.
A second family of approaches instead learns generalizable semantic fields, where
the goal is to produce novel segmentation view synthesis from a pre-trained RGB NeRF
without any scene-specific ground-truth segmentation. Rather than relying on scenespecific optimization, the methods learn their models on annotated NeRF datasets to infer
consistent semantic segmentation on novel environments. Those methods are quite varied,
using various tricks to obtain this scene segmentation. Notably, NeSF [172] learns to predict
a semantic field from an explicit regular density grid, thus needing an expensive grid
decoding of the NeRF to interpret the 3D geometry. GNeSF [22] and SemanticRay [100]
on the other hand use an explicit generalization mechanism that computes 2D multiview features from the observed images, fuses those into per-point features, decodes the
features into semantics and projects those into the novel view.
However, no methods truly interpret scene-level NeRF models with fine-grained
spatial understanding, e.g. directly from the grid features. This is because implicit neural
representations are inherently scene-specific: each NeRF defines its own latent space,
making specific regions of the representations non-reproducible and difficult to generalize
across models. This necessitates either limiting the scope to a holistic understanding of
object-NeRFs (as in hypernetworks) or learning semantic fields from the geometry or
the multi-view images rather than the NeRF models themselves. Furthermore, existing
approaches are often tailored to narrow downstream tasks such as classification or retrieval,
limiting their versatility for broader semantic understanding.
A successful alternative builds on the dual 2D–3D nature of NeRFs, lifting semantically
rich 2D features (usually from pre-trained foundation models) into the 3D domain, forming
the basis of most recent advances in NeRF 3D scene understanding.
2.4.2
From 2D to 3D : Uplifting 2D Semantics into 3D Feature Fields
Neural Radiance Fields are not limited to learning RGB colors; in principle, they can learn
any quantity that can be projected and supervised from 2D observations. Building on this
idea, feature fields generalize the NeRF formulation to predict arbitrary per-point feature
signals rather than only color. Formally, the mapping
34
2.4. 3D NERF INTERPRETATION
𝐹Θ (x, d) −→ (c, 𝜎),
(2.16)
𝐹Θ (x, d) −→ (f, c, 𝜎),
(2.17)
is extended to
where f ∈ R𝑑 represents one (or multiple) vector that may encode any type of data,
whether it is color, semantics (e.g. per-point semantic class or instance), physical properties
(e.g. temperature, sound, magnetic waves, ...), or any other latent descriptor (e.g. 2D
Foundation Models embeddings). Supervision is achieved by reprojecting the predicted
features onto the image plane and comparing them to their corresponding 2D feature
maps, mirroring the color reconstruction process used in the original NeRF formulation.
Although NeRF can learn fields of infinite different natures, we focus in this thesis on
semantic-related fields. We can separate semantic fields into two types.
A first line of research focuses on learning explicit scene semantics.
Semantic-
NeRF [207], notably, was the first to introduce semantic fields within the NeRF framework.
Using ground-truth semantic segmentation maps, it augments the radiance field with
per-point semantic logits, enabling dense 3D semantic understanding. The extension to
instance-level fields [108] follows naturally when appropriate 2D annotations are available,
allowing each point to encode instance-specific information. These early approaches laid
the foundation for integrating semantics directly into NeRF representations, inspiring
subsequent works [49, 85] that explore richer and more general forms of 3D semantic
reasoning. However, the need for scene-specific 2D segmentation maps to interpret the
scene is very limiting for most real-life applications.
Another direction for enriching NeRF representations with semantics leverages 2D feature encoders, transferring their knowledge into the 3D domain by learning feature fields
from pre-trained foundation models. This strategy provides richer, more general, and
implicitly structured semantic information, while benefiting from the robustness and scalability of mature 2D encoders. As 2D vision models evolved from self-supervised visual
encoders to multimodal vision–language systems, feature fields inherited their capabilities,
progressively enabling more expressive and interactive forms of 3D interpretation.
A wide variety of approaches [82, 76, 40, 197, 170, 46, 109, 83] follow this paradigm,
each distilling different types of encoders depending on the target downstream task. For
instance, DINO features have been distilled for semantic segmentation [82], while CLIP and
related multimodal encoders such as OpenSeg provide open-vocabulary understanding in
methods like LeRF [76] and OpenNeRF [40]. SAM features enable instance segmentation
and mask propagation across viewpoints, as demonstrated in SAM-NeRF [46] and related
frameworks [109]. More recently, RelationField [83] learns a Bert [33] (i.e. language-only
encoder) feature field from LLM-generated textual descriptions of the objects relationships.
Together, these methods illustrate the versatility of feature distillation for aligning NeRFs
with a growing ecosystem of powerful 2D foundation models.
35
CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND
Figure 2.9: 2D CLIP vs LERF. The left visualizes similarity interpolated over patchwise
CLIP embeddings, and the right rendered from LERF. Because volumetric language
rendering incorporates information from multiple views, 3D relevancy activation maps
have better alignment with the underlying scene geometry. Figure is from [76].
Beyond distillation of 2D encoders, several approaches learn self-supervised feature
fields directly within the NeRF framework notably using contrastive learning: a paradigm
that trains models by pulling representations of similar items together while pushing dissimilar ones apart. For example, Garfield [78] applies this principle over multi-view
SAM-derived masks to build hierarchical 3D groupings, while NeRF-SOS [43] leverages
DINO and density with contrastive objectives to segment objects without explicit labels.
These methods treat per-point embeddings analogously to 2D backbone features, promoting 3D semantic consistency through these similarity signals.
Using feature fields to interpret 3D scenes represents a major shift from direct NeRF
interpretation methods. Unlike those approaches, feature fields augment the radiance
field with semantic information learned from 2D features. This means that geometry,
appearance, and semantics are co-encoded within the same 3D representation, allowing
each point in space to carry both physical and conceptual meaning. For embodied AI, this
joint representation enables agents not only to perceive the structure of their surroundings
but also to understand it inside one single unified map of their environment.
Additionally, a key advantage of this formulation lies in its multi-view consistency. By
aggregating 2D features extracted from multiple viewpoints, the field reconciles per-image
embeddings into a unified coherent 3D space. Because individual 2D feature maps are
often low-resolution, this aggregation effectively increases spatial density, enabling the
rendering of fine-grained feature maps, as illustrated with open-vocabulary segmentation
in Figure 2.9. The fusion process also enhances robustness to changes in viewpoint,
illumination, and occlusion, yielding spatially consistent and invariant semantics that
surpass their 2D counterparts.
Nevertheless, training and exploiting feature fields introduces notable challenges.
The high dimensionality of feature embeddings substantially increases memory usage
and computational cost. Moreover, the process of lifting 2D features into 3D remains
fundamentally 2D-driven and lacks the intrinsic geometric reasoning that explicit 3D
encoders could provide. Finally, unlike direct NeRF interpretation methods that infer
semantics in a single forward pass, feature fields require per-scene optimization before
36
2.4. 3D NERF INTERPRETATION
their semantics can be exploited, limiting scalability for large datasets or real-time use.
Despite these limitations, feature fields constitute a powerful middle ground between
reconstruction and interpretation. They transform NeRFs into a unified representation
where meaning and structure coexist. By leveraging the dual 2D–3D nature of NeRFs
and the expressive capacity of pre-trained 2D foundation models, they enable embodied
agents to operate with spatially grounded semantics, bridging the gap between perception
and scene understanding.
2.4.3
Towards Embodied AI with Neural Radiance Fields
Building semantically enriched 3D representations marks a key milestone toward embodied artificial intelligence. For embodied agents, perception must not only capture an
explicit understanding of the scene’s structure, i.e. where objects are, how they relate,
and what interactions they afford, but also support reasoning and action planning based
on user-given tasks. Neural Radiance Fields (NeRFs), when augmented with semantic
feature fields, are adapted for all these requirements: they encode a coherent 3D scene
model that can be interpreted by reasoning systems to plan and act, while maintaining
the geometric and semantic consistency required for spatial understanding.
In modern embodied systems, large language models (LLMs) [1, 4, 165, 112] and
Vision-Language models (VLMs) [103, 101, 102, 89] often serve as the cognitive core of the
agent: interpreting user queries, reasoning over contextual information, and producing
actionable plans [44]. Connecting NeRF-based scene models to such LLMs, however,
remains a challenging problem. Early approaches such as LLaNA [3] and Spatial LLaNA [9]
demonstrated that Vanilla NeRF embeddings and spatial tokens can be aligned with
language representations through fine-tuning, allowing LLMs to interpret object-NeRF
scenes. Yet, enabling LLMs to process full, complex 3D NeRF environments as direct
inputs remains unexplored.
However, beyond NeRFs, related research has successfully connected LLMs with other
3D modalities such as point clouds (e.g., LEO [63, 64], Scene-LLM [48], ...) and Gaussian
splatting (e.g., ChatSplat [21], SplatTalk [167]), highlighting a growing trend toward
unifying 3D perception and language-driven reasoning. These advances (especially the
methods from the differentiable Gaussian splatting representation) suggest that NeRFs
could similarly serve as a foundation for integrating 3D understanding into multimodal
reasoning systems.
While direct 3D-LLM connections allow holistic scene interpretation and high-level
reasoning, they often lack fine-grained spatial control. The reasoning model can describe or
analyze the global scene but struggles to selectively focus on specific objects or subregions
of the scene. Recent approaches [195] address this limitation by coupling LLM-based
reasoning with local perception tools that provide detailed visual or semantic grounding
which can be called upon by the LLM. For instance, Chat-With-NeRF [192] combines
the LLM with a LeRF [76] model, enabling the agent to query local semantics and focus
37
CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND
its attention across the scene. This framework reflects a natural evolution of embodied
intelligence: high-level cognition guided by adaptable, localized sensory queries.
Together, these advances outline a clear trajectory toward agents capable of spatially
grounded reasoning. By coupling NeRF-based 3D representations with language-driven
reasoning and adaptive perception modules, embodied systems gain both a global understanding of their environment and the ability to act locally with precision. In this sense,
neural feature fields not only enrich 3D reconstruction with semantics but also lay the
conceptual and technical foundations for embodied AI, where seeing, understanding, and
acting merge into a unified process.
38
3
A Foundation Architecture for Robust
and Extensible NeRF Map Reconstruction
in Real-World Settings
Establishing a reliable 3D map is the prerequisite for any downstream understanding task. However,
standard Neural Fields often struggle with the unconstrained nature of in-the-wild robotic data, such
as varying observation distances and sparse viewpoints. This chapter introduces RING-NeRF, an
architecture designed to bridge these robustness gaps through two structural inductive biases: a
continuous multi-scale representation of the scene and a strictly enforced invariance of the latent
space across spatial and scale domains. Leveraging these priors, we design a unified reconstruction
process that achieves state-of-the-art quality on challenging tasks including anti-aliasing, few-view
reconstruction, and initialization-free SDF reconstruction without requiring complex task-specific
supervision. Furthermore, RING-NeRF allows for a fully scalable reconstruction process: it supports
both dynamic adaptive resolution and spatial increments, paving the way for truly extensible
reconstruction in unbounded environments.
Project page can be found at : https://cea-list.github.io/RING-NeRF
3.1
Context and Motivation
For embodied AI agents to operate effectively in real-world environments, they must be
able to build and maintain an accurate 3D model of their surroundings as part of their
Functional Core activities. Such a representation forms the foundation for perception,
navigation, and interaction, enabling the agent to reason about its environment and execute
user-specified tasks. Neural Radiance Fields have rapidly become a central paradigm for
high-fidelity scene reconstruction. Their ability to reconstruct detailed and consistent 3D
representations directly from a simple RGB camera makes them particularly suitable for
robotics-based environment 3D mapping.
However, current NeRF formulations still face important limitations that restrict their
deployment in real-world scenarios, especially regarding embodied AI. Most recent
associated research therefore concentrates on addressing challenges related to:
39
CHAPTER 3. A FOUNDATION ARCHITECTURE FOR ROBUST AND EXTENSIBLE
NERF MAP RECONSTRUCTION IN REAL-WORLD SETTINGS
• Nature of scene - by transitioning from object-centric scenes to large open unbounded
ones, using mostly space contraction [10];
• Robustness - notably by managing free motion trajectories with variations of the
observation distance while avoiding aliasing artefacts (mostly through the integration of Level of Detail (LOD) in the model [11, 12]); or by reducing drastically the
number of supervised views through different kinds of regularization [193, 65, 129];
• Extensibility - by shifting from a holistic and fixed reconstruction process to an
incremental (extensibility in the number of views) and adaptive (extensibility in
resolution) process, mainly through the use of a frozen decoder [212, 106].
Much of the recent research in NeRF has focused on overcoming only one of these
challenges at a time and introduce specialized and often complex mechanisms that increase
computational cost and reduce composability. In practice, comprehensive mapping
systems for embodied AI require all three properties to coexist within a single, efficient
framework. Achieving this joint capability is crucial for deploying NeRF-based perception
models in dynamic, unbounded, and uncertain environments.
Rather than introducing yet another heavy and task-specific solution, we propose in
this chapter RING-NeRF, a versatile and simple NeRF architecture by rethinking usual
grid-based models to introduce two inductive biases. We first represent a 3D scene as
a continuous multi-scale representation. Secondly, we also make the decoder’s latent
space invariant in position and scale. Together, these two priors enable the production
of intrinsic continuous LOD of the scene without explicit supervision. We demonstrate
experimentally that, when combined with adapted cone casting and coarse-to-fine optimization, the resulting architecture is able to compete on several tasks with on-par
quality performances with dedicated state-of-the-art solutions while improving speed,
robustness and extensibility. The overall process is also simple, easy to implement and
generic enough to be coupled with other specific solutions. Our contributions in this
chapter can be summarized as follows:
1. an architecture that, by construction, represents the scene with a continuous and
unbounded level of detail without the need for LOD-specific supervision and which
permits extensible reconstruction in both resolution and space;
2. a distance-aware forward mapping compatible with scene contraction, that takes
benefits of the continuous multi-scale representation with an adapted cone casting
process to avoid aliasing artefacts when varying the observation distance.
3. a continuous coarse-to-fine reconstruction process that improves the convergence
and stability (especially in challenging setups such as supervision with few viewpoints or no scene-specific initialization for SDF reconstruction).
40
3.2. RELATED WORKS
Figure 3.1: RING-NeRF Overview Examples. RING-NeRF is a simple and versatile
architecture which tackles many NeRF common issues such as robustness to distance of
observation, few view supervision and lack of scene-specific initialization for SDF-based
reconstruction. It provides on-par performances in terms of quality with SotA dedicated
solutions [12, 193, 95] and and in terms of efficiency with fast methods [164, 200].
3.2
Related Works
From its original iteration [120], a majority of current research focuses on overtaking
limitations of NeRF-based reconstruction in terms of adaptability to various natures of
scene, robustness (to varying observation distances or limited amount of viewpoints) and
reconstruction extensibility.
Adaptability to various natures of the scene. The ability of Neural Fields to reconstruct
various natures of scene depends on three factors. The first one is related to its architecture itself. Tri-plane architectures [62, 213, 20] are mostly designed for object-centric
reconstruction (as they provide a higher density of information in the center of the scene)
while vanilla and 3D grid-based NeRF are able to cover a wider variety of scenes, though
they initially were still constrained to a limited volume. A second factor is related to the
representation of the 3D space of the scene, especially to represent distant elements in
open scenes. As mentioned before, some approaches use two different NeRF models to
reconstruct separately the foreground and the background [204], while some apply space
contraction to the 3D scene coordinates [204, 10, 12] to map the infinite scene volume into a
bounded one. The last factor is related to the initialization of the Neural Fields. While the
random initialization of density-based NeRF can adjust to almost any nature of scenes, the
convergence of SDF-based (Signed Distance Function) Neural Fields is extremely sensitive
to their parameters’ initialization, as stated in [6]. Current solutions rely on a scene-specific
initialization (using an SDF field representing a sphere for outdoor scenes or an inverted
sphere for indoor scenes), making them unable to adapt automatically to any scene. We
41
CHAPTER 3. A FOUNDATION ARCHITECTURE FOR ROBUST AND EXTENSIBLE
NERF MAP RECONSTRUCTION IN REAL-WORLD SETTINGS
built RING-NeRF on top of a grid-based NeRF with space contraction to support large,
unbounded scene reconstruction. The method is also designed to remain robust in practice,
mitigating initialization sensitivity and avoiding any need for scene-specific heuristics.
Robustness to observation distance variations. The initial NeRF model, as well as most
of the subsequent works, relies implicitly on the hypothesis of a constant distance of
observation to the scene.
Indeed, the NeRF model provides a per-3D-point scene density representation and a
rendering process which does not take into account the increasing volume covered by a
pixel with respect to the distance to the cameras. As underlined in Mip-NeRF [11], this
discrepancy induces artefacts such as over-contrasted images or aliasing phenomenon
when the distance of observation differs from the ones used at reconstruction time. To
avoid these artefacts, the rendering process needs to assess the density and color for a
volume instead of a point. In the current state-of-the-art, two main approaches are used (or
their combination, as in Zip-NeRF [12]). The first solution consists of representing the scene
with different levels of detail (LOD), in order to vary the precision of the reconstruction
based on the observation distance. This is usually done by using a LOD-aware latent
space [36, 161, 171, 11, 12, 188], meaning that the LOD information is already encoded in
the inputs of the MLP. This can be achieved by using a per-LOD decoder as in PyNeRF
[171] or by incorporating LOD information in the latent feature as done in Zip-NeRF [12]
and VR-NeRF [188]. One main flaw of these solutions is that they require the supervision
of every used LOD which makes it impossible to have different observation distances
between the train views and novel synthesised views.
A second approach consists of defining the latent representation of a volume as the
mean of the latent features of the points included in the volume. It requires integrating
the features over the volume, which can be achieved through convolutions for tri-plan
representation [62, 213], or through super-sampling of the latent space for 3D grids as also
done in Zip-NeRF [12]. However, these approaches lengthen the training and rendering
processes as they increase the number of computations.
In contrast to these approaches, RING-NeRF decouples LOD from the decoder through
an LOD-invariant latent space, allowing the model to naturally adapt to varying observation distances without supervising multiple LODs or incurring the heavy computation
typical of volume-integrating methods.
Robustness to limited amount of viewpoints. As already stated, NeRF is subject by
construction to the shape-radiance ambiguity [204]. If not enough supervision viewpoints
are available, the optimization might overfit them while not providing consistent 3D
reconstruction nor generalization to non-supervised viewpoints. A first family of solutions
to overcome this limitation consists of regularizing the reconstruction process through
additional losses, whether via geometric [129, 199, 185] or semantic [65] regularization.
42
3.3. METHOD
The second family of solutions relies on a progressive reconstruction of the details of
the scene, as introduced in FreeNeRF [193] and Nerfies [135]. Restraining the ability of
the model to reconstruct a complex scene at early stages enforces the consistency of the
reconstruction over the different supervision viewpoints. This presents the advantage of
bringing stability while keeping a fairly simple training process. However, these latter
solutions mostly rely on Vanilla models for stability purposes and require long training
duration.
RING-NeRF follows this progressive philosophy through a continuous coarse-to-fine
scheme, extending it to grid-based neural fields to provide the same stabilization benefits
without relying on vanilla architectures or incurring long training times.
Reconstruction extensibility. Two kinds of extensibility should be distinguished: extensibility with respect to the number of views or the resolution. The first one is related
to reconstructing new scene areas while keeping the previously reconstructed ones unchanged, notably by avoiding catastrophic forgetting of previous areas. The usual solution
consists of using grid-based approaches with a pre-trained position-invariant decoder
that is frozen during the reconstruction[212].
On the other side, the extensibility of the resolution consists in dynamically increasing
the level of detail of a previously reconstructed scene. This problem is intractable for classic
architectures such as vanilla NeRF and I-NGP since the number of layers (respectively
grids) of those approaches cannot increase during the reconstruction. Some rare solutions,
such as Neural Sparse Voxel Fields [106], combine a pre-trained decoder with a data
structure allowing to dynamically allocate voxels to increase the reconstruction resolution.
This is however a crucial stake, notably for embodied AI, as being able to adapt the
precision of the representation based on the complexity of the scene permits to optimize
the computational efficiency (both in speed and memory requirements) with minimal loss
in quality.
With its scale- and position-invariant decoder, RING-NeRF is perfectly adapted to be
the pre-trained backbone of both view and resolution extensibility.
3.3
Method
3.3.1
Overview
RING-NeRF relies on the classic NeRF [120] inverse rendering pipeline which is used
to reconstruct a 3D scene from a set of localized frames. The originality of RING-NeRF
relies on its neural architecture, illustrated in figure 3.2, which is designed to represent the
scene with a continuous and unbounded level of detail without the need for LOD-specific
supervision (see section 3.3.2). We then use this LOD inductive bias to adjust the LOD
of the samples with respect to the distance to the camera in contracted space for more
accurate renderings (see section 3.3.3). Finally, combined with a continuous coarse-to-fine

3.3.2
NeRF Representation with Inductive Biases
Classic grid-based representations, such as the one introduced by I-NGP[124], rely on
two key elements: a unique MLP decoder which transforms a latent space into an output
space (color, density or SDF), and a 3D (hierarchical) grid of latent features that implicitly
defines a unique mapping function from the scene space onto the decoder latent space.
We can distinguish two different approaches used to extend the mono-scale representation to a multi-scale representation: conditioning with scale information the decoding
process [12, 171] and/or defining separate per-LOD mapping functions [171].
In both cases, the training becomes more complex, leading to potential convergence
issues as it relies solely on additional specific supervision.
Instead, we propose to condition the mapping function itself with scale information
and introduce inductive biases to guide the convergence.
Scene representation as multi-scale mapping function. As illustrated in Figure 3.2, the
mapping function at level 𝑁 is controlled by a grid 𝐺 𝑁 that is implicitly defined through
a recursive refinement process over scale:


 𝐺0 ← 𝛿 0


 𝐺 𝑁 ← 𝒮(𝐺 𝑁−1 ) + 𝛿 𝑁

(3.1)
where 𝛿 𝑖 is the deviation defined by the i-th latent feature grid and 𝒮 is the subdivision
scheme consisting in increasing the resolution of 𝐺 𝑁−1 up to the 𝑁-th grid resolution
(with tri-linear interpolation). In practice, the latent vector associated with a point in
44
3.3. METHOD
the scene for a level of detail 𝑁 is simply obtained by interpolating linearly the point
in the cell for each level inferior or equal to 𝑁, and summing the results. Since linear
interpolation is differentiable, our mapping function is also optimizable. The summing
of the interpolation is comparable to a residual connection and gives the name of the
architecture RING-NeRF for Residual Implicit Neural Grids.
Such representation provides several advantages. First, since the mapping function
is constructed in a top-down manner, refining recursively one level of detail from the
previous one, its number of LOD is unbounded and adding a finer level of detail keeps
the coarser ones valid. Secondly, because each 𝛿 𝑖 only represents a deviation of the
coarser mapping value 𝐺 𝑖−1 , a continuous LOD representation can be easily obtained by
incorporating a weighting factor 𝛼 𝐿𝑂𝐷 ∈ [0, 1] in the recursive process:
𝐺 𝐿 ← 𝒮(𝐺 𝑁−1 ) + 𝛼𝛿 𝑁
(3.2)
with 𝐿 ∈ [𝑁 − 1, 𝑁] and 𝛼 𝐿𝑂𝐷 = 𝐿 − (𝑁 − 1).
Spatial and scale invariance of decoder. Since our architecture does not rely on a decoder
conditioned on position (unlike [161]) nor scale (unlike [11, 10, 12, 171]), the decoder latent
space is invariant to translations and scale changes in the scene coordinate. This property
makes RING-NeRF adapted for incremental reconstruction in both spatial and scale space,
since it ensures that local updates in the spatial and scale domain of the scene are achieved
through the hierarchical grid, making the decoder scene-agnostic in terms of spatial and
scale variances.
The decoder architecture is illustrated in figure 3.2. The final feature after multiresolution combination is passed into a normalization layer to improve convergence
stability. Because a summation replaces the concatenation usually seen in grid-based
architectures, the feature is shorter and could face problems for expressing high frequencies.
It is thus projected into a higher dimension space with a Random Fourier Feature mapping.
This consists in a learnable frequency filter, for which we chose a sinus filter : y = 𝑠𝑖𝑛(𝑊x)
with 𝑊 a linear matrix (without bias). A linear layer transforms the filter output into both
density (or SDF) and one color feature. This feature, concatenated with the direction of
observation encoded into Spherical Harmonics, is fed to a MLP of 3 layers that predicts
the radiance.
LOD inductive bias. During the reconstruction process, the gradients of the ray samples
are backpropagated through the residual connections and aggregated for each grid level.
Due to the pyramidal resolution of the hierarchical grid, a grid code at coarse levels
influences a large scene volume and is thus supervised by more ray samples. Therefore,
the gradient of a grid code increases as the level’s resolution decreases, and as long
as the associated samples’ gradients are uniform. However, once the backpropagation
through the residual connections reach a level whose associated samples have divergent
enough gradients (meaning the error is finer than this level’s resolution), the result of
45
CHAPTER 3. A FOUNDATION ARCHITECTURE FOR ROBUST AND EXTENSIBLE
NERF MAP RECONSTRUCTION IN REAL-WORLD SETTINGS
Figure 3.3: LOD Inductive Bias on objects. We demonstrate it by training our model
with a hierarchy of 𝑁 = 7 levels where only the last level of the mapping function 𝐺 𝑁 is
supervised. We then compute renders at different levels 𝐿 ≤ 𝑁. The scenes come from
the DTU dataset.
Figure 3.4: LOD Inductive Bias on real-world scenes. Different LODs outputs of the
model when only trained on the last level 𝐿 = 7 on the Mip-NeRF dataset.
Figure 3.5: Comparison of LOD. When supervising (a) every level of detail or (b) solely
the finest level of detail.
46
3.3. METHOD
the aggregation will be mitigated. Hence, the level with the maximum correction is
always the coarser level where the samples’ gradients are still uniform. This property
naturally induces corrections at the proper grid level and LOD. In figures 3.3 and 3.4, we
illustrate this inductive bias by displaying different continuous LOD of various scenes
(both object-centric and complex unbounded) while the reconstruction is only supervised
for its finest LOD. By supervising all the LOD, we observe however a correlation between
the LOD used to compute the image and the level of details in the images (as shown in
figure 3.5).
Not only this is a useful property when an unsupervised multi-scale representation
is needed (as demonstrated in section 3.4.1 with anti-aliasing for instance), this also
guarantees a more robust convergence (as illustrated in section 3.4.2.1 and section 3.4.2.2).
3.3.3
Distance-Aware Forward Mapping
Cone Casting in contracted space. In order to accommodate the scene rendering process
to the variation of observation distances, we introduce a distance-aware forward mapping
mechanism. Similarly to the cone casting of [62], it relies on assigning to each sample
a latent feature whose LOD is inversely proportional to the sample-camera distance.
However, unlike [62, 171], our model relies on the use of space contraction to allow the
reconstruction of unbounded scenes.
In this thesis, we use the scene contraction function defined originally in Mip-NeRF
[11] :


(3.3)
Additionally, we use ℒ∞ norm rather than ℒ2 , as it bounds the position to a cube
rather than a sphere, which is convenient with grid-based representations.
To define the LOD of a sample at distance 𝑑 of the camera, we first compute the
associated volume of the cone cast pixel cube in the world space coordinates (see fig. 3.2).
Assuming the pixel is a square of size 𝑐 at distance 1 of the camera (𝑐 depends on the
image resolution and camera’s FOV), the volume is thus 𝑉 = (𝑑𝑐)3 . To take into account
the space contraction, we then proceed to contract the volume. Denoting 𝐽 the Jacobian of
the contraction function at the sample’s location x, 𝑉𝑐𝑜𝑛𝑡𝑟 𝑎𝑐𝑡 = 𝑉 𝑑𝑒𝑡(𝐽(x)). In practice, we
compute the analytical derivation of the contraction function depending on x.
Assuming the 𝑁-th feature grid’s resolution in our hierarchy can be written as 𝑔 𝑁 𝑏
with 𝑏 the resolution of the grid 𝛿0 and 𝑔 the growth factor, finding the appropriate LOD
𝐿 ∈ R+ means finding a virtual grid1 of resolution 𝑔 𝐿 𝑏 whose cell’s volume is equal to
the previously computed volume (in contracted space). Because we are working in the
contracted space of size 1, the volume of a cell of the virtual grid of LOD 𝐿 is ( 𝑔1𝐿 𝑏 )3 . The

With this formula, we can first notice that, in the non-contracted space (inside the cube
of size 1), the LOD 𝐿 does not depend on the contraction function, and its value decreases
with the distance as expected.
However, further in the scene, inside the contracted space, we notice that the LOD
L might increase with the distance (due to 𝑙𝑜 𝑔(||x||4 )), as illustrated in figure 3.6. While
this can seem pretty non-intuitive at first, this has a logical explanation. Because of the
contraction, the fixed volume of a grid cell will represent an increasing volume of the
scene as the distance to the scene’s center increases, up to an infinite volume. This clashes
with the idea that the further our sample, the lower our chosen LOD needs to be. This
duality is represented in the equation by the 𝑙𝑜 𝑔(||x||4 ) − 𝑙𝑜 𝑔(𝑑) part, and it shows that, at
some point, the contraction function will always take the advantage on the distance term
as our sample grow further from the center. Hence, whatever the position of the camera,
if a ray continues far enough, the chosen LOD will increase at some point because of the
contraction function. This also implies that the more resolute grids are used both for near
objects and further areas, which would not have been the case if we did not consider the
contraction function and which provides a more optimal use of the grid hierarchy with
less unused parameters.
We can note that our contracted cone casting process is similar to ZipNeRF [12] and
VR-NeRF [188] propositions. However, the first one rather derives a contracted scale factor
from its Gaussian samples while the latter directly computes the LOD in the contracted
space, which can be considered an approximation of our computation.
48
3.4. EXPERIMENTS
Forward mapping. As illustrated in Figure 3.2, we use the determined LOD to compute
a distance-dependent weighted sum of the features, which is fed to our decoder and
transformed into density (or SDF) and radiance.
3.3.4
Continuous Coarse-To-Fine and Resolution Extensibility
Recent works proposed to use coarse-to-fine optimization to improve the stability of NeRF
models, especially when facing more challenging setups, including with few images [193]
and surface-based models [95]. It consists of optimizing progressively the different LOD of
the representation, from coarse levels to the most precise ones. The goal of this progressive
optimization is to avoid the shape-radiance ambiguity [204] by introducing a strong
regularization through LOD restriction, then relaxing progressively this regularization
once the coarse geometry of the scene is reconstructed to recover the details of the scene.
The coarse-to-fine reconstruction process of RING-NeRF consists of estimating progressively the LOD of the mapping function from the coarsest to the finest ones. In practice,
it implies, during the cone casting, to clamp the samples’ LOD up to a maximal LOD 𝐿,
the grids of level 𝐿 + 1 and above being set to zero and not optimized. Moreover, since our
architecture provides continuous LOD, the coarse-to-fine optimization can be achieved
continuously in the LOD space by using a linear scheduler 𝐿 = (𝐿0 + 𝑛 𝑐𝑡𝑛 𝑓 ) ∈ R+ with 𝑛 the
current epoch, 𝑛 𝑐𝑡 𝑓 a hyperparameter describing the speed of the process and 𝐿0 defining
the number of used grids at initialization; up to a specified maximum resolution.
Furthermore, the RING-NeRF architecture is more adapted than I-NGP-based architectures [95] for coarse-to-fine training. Indeed, for solutions based on the concatenation
of features, keeping grid values to zero implies that some dimensions of the decoder’s
latent space are not supervised. When a grid starts to be optimized, those unsupervised
dimensions are suddenly used. The weights of the decoder thus need to be refined, with
a global effect on the whole scene. On the opposite, our solution keeps supervising all
the dimensions of the decoder latent space, and when a new grid gets optimized, it only
implies more degrees of freedom to define the mapping function between the scene space
and the decoder latent space. This also means that our scene reconstruction can be refined
by adding dynamically new grid levels without modifying the decoder’s weights or previously trained grids, as we demonstrate experimentally in section 3.4.3. This resolution
extensibility property opens the path to adaptive resolution models, where the precision
used to describe an area depends on the details needed, to optimize efficiency both in
memory consumption and training duration.
3.4
Experiments
In these experiments, we intend to highlight the versatility of RING-NeRF by evaluating
it on several tasks. We first evaluate our model on novel view synthesis with changes in
observation distances (sec. 3.4.1). Then, we explore how robust is RING-NeRF first with
49
CHAPTER 3. A FOUNDATION ARCHITECTURE FOR ROBUST AND EXTENSIBLE
NERF MAP RECONSTRUCTION IN REAL-WORLD SETTINGS
few view reconstruction (sec. 3.4.2.1) and then without scene-specific initialization for
SDF reconstruction (sec. 3.4.2.2). Finally, we demonstrate the extensibility capacity of our
architecture first with LOD extensibility, as a first step towards adaptive reconstruction (sec.
3.4.3.1) and then with spatial extensibility, by introducing an incremental reconstruction
baseline with RING-NeRF in section 3.4.3.2.
As stated before, we build upon Nerfstudio’s core method Nerfacto. All of these
models are trained on one Nvidia-A100 GPU. The reported times correspond to the
approximated training duration of the models.
3.4.1
Novel View Synthesis and Anti-Aliasing
This experiment aims to evaluate the reconstruction quality through the ability to synthesize viewpoints that are not supervised during the reconstruction. These new viewpoints
differ from the angle of observation, but also from the distance of observation. The latter
is particularly important since a reconstruction or a rendering process that does not take
correctly into account the distance of observation leads to artefacts, from over-contrasted
rendering to aliasing phenomenon. The challenge is to avoid these artefacts while keeping
the reconstruction and rendering process as fast as possible.
Dataset. The evaluation relies on the dataset introduced by Mip-NeRF-360 [10]. This
dataset is composed of 9 scenes, each containing both a central area and complex background in both inside and outside setups. Since the trajectory keeps a constant distance
to the central part, each viewpoint is represented with a pyramid of 4 different image
resolutions to simulate a variation of the distance of observation combined with a change
of the camera FOV, following the Mip-NeRF [11] original anti-aliasing evaluation pipeline.
Algorithms. We compare our solution against several grid-based NeRF baselines, both
with (PyNeRF [171] and Zip-NeRF [12]) and without anti-aliasing processing (Nerfacto),
using their Nerfstudio implementations.
Hyperparameters Our Novel View Synthesis and Anti-Aliasing experiments on the
Mip-NeRF 360 dataset are done on one unique configuration for all our baselines and
setups. We mostly use the configuration provided in the article of ZipNeRF [12] with
few differences. We trained each baselines for 25k iterations with a batch size of 216 rays.
We use the RAdam Optimizer with an initial learning rate of 10−2 , and a cosine-decayed
scheduler over the whole training to 10−3 .
Regarding the model itself, we use a hash grid pyramid of 10 grids with a growth factor
of 2, with grid resolutions ranging from 16 to 8192. Contrary to the original Zip-NeRF,
we use 8 features per level rather than 4, but with a similar hashmap size of 1283 = 221
(however note that our experiments with ZipNeRF were done also with a feature size of
8). We chose this parameter to maximize the quality of every baseline, as it is compatible
with our GPU.

Following Nerfacto’s architecture, we also used scene contraction (as described previously), as well as the distortion loss in these experiments, as implemented in NerfStudio
for Nerfacto, PyNeRF and RING-NeRF, and their adapted improved versions for ZipNeRF
as presented in their article. We also did not use appearance embedding for any method
as we also noticed a decrease in metrics when using it, as observed in Mip-NeRF 360
and ZipNeRF. Finally, for Nerfacto, PyNeRF and RING-NeRF, we did not use any additional mechanism such as the ones introduced in ZipNeRF (no novel interlevel loss, nor
scale featurization, nor weight decay loss, nor Affine Generative Latent Optimization as
appearance embedding, ...).
Protocol. For each scene, we train the models with two different setups: the mono-scale
setup that uses only the image with the highest resolution for each viewpoint (i.e. training
the model only with the original dataset), and the multi-scale setup which uses the whole
pyramid of images for each viewpoint.
Mono-Scale Setup Results. First of all, regarding novel view synthesis quality using
the mono-scale setup for both training and testing, as referred to in table 3.1, we notice
that our architecture provides on par performances with Zip-NeRF, slightly better results
than PyNeRF, and a more important gap with Nerfacto. Regarding the reconstruction
processing time, since RING-NeRF does not rely on the multiplication of either sample or
decoder, it processes as quickly as the fastest Nerfacto. On the other hand, the other antialiasing methods, PyNeRF with its per-LOD MLP and Zip-NeRF with its super-sampling,
are approximately 2.5 times slower.
As expected, those PyNeRF, ZipNeRF and RING-NeRF baselines also all outperform
every Vanilla architectures, both in terms of quality and speed. Mip-NeRF 360 is the only
vanilla method which performs close to our baselines, and also outperforms Nerfacto
(although by being 50 times slower!). While simple, RING-NeRF succeeds in performing
state-of-the-art performances on a real single-scale dataset.
In order to further analyze the separate impact of our architecture and of the associated
mechanisms (distance-aware forward mapping and continuous coarse-to-fine), we run
ablative mono-scale experiments. Our model is thus evaluated on 4 configurations with
varying setups and the corresponding results can be found in table 3.2. First of all, we

notice a gap between the results of Nerfacto and our method without the distance-aware

Moreover, the addition of the distance-aware mechanism still increases the performances significantly. While this idea was initially developed as an anti-aliasing process,
this experiment demonstrates that it also benefits Novel View Synthesis in situations with
little variations of distances of observation.
Finally, while our continuous coarse-to-fine brings slightly better results than when
using the usual discrete coarse-to-fine, it still presents slightly lower results than when not
using coarse-to-fine. While the difference is not prohibitive (especially in SSIM and LPIPS)
and mainly due to randomness in the training process, this observation demonstrates
that, even though using coarse-to-fine on harder setups is necessary to avoid catastrophic
failure or improve consistency, as will be explained in section 3.4.2, it does not improve
results in already stable setups for Novel View Synthesis.

observations against the Nerfacto baseline. On one side, the aliased rendering of Nerfacto
presents different types of artifacts at different resolutions. This is due to the NeRF’s model
property to "average" the distances of observations between the trained views. This thus
results in under-contrasted image at full resolution, over-contrasted image at resolution
1/4 and aliasing artifacts at resolution 1/8. On the other hand, RING-NeRF takes into
account the distance in the computation of the feature and succeeds in adapting to the
different resolutions.
We can also note that computation times are equivalent to the mono-scale setup, with
RING-NeRF once again the fastest anti-aliasing method, in addition to the state-of-the-art
quantitative results.
Multi-Scale Results from Mono-Scale Training. Next, we evaluate the capacity of the
models to generalize over new resolutions by training them on full-resolution images and
then evaluate them on smaller resolutions. Qualitative results are shown in figure 3.8
while quantitative results can be found in the 1/2-th, 1/4-th and 1/8-th res. columns of
table 3.4. Because most anti-aliasing methods need LOD-specific supervision to correctly
function (including Zip-NeRF and PyNeRF), they cannot render coherent anti-aliased
images in this setup. However, PyNeRF behaves better than ZipNeRF with coherent
although very aliased renderings, as it decides to limit the LOD used for rendering inside
the range of LOD seen during training. RING-NeRF, with his LOD inductive bias, is the
only architecture capable of producing anti-aliased renderings from novel observation
distances and thus outperforms every other method.
While this experiment can seem somewhat esoteric as training on a multi-resolution
images pyramid is rather easy, this increases GPU memory and total training time. Moreover, depending on the resolution and the trajectory, it is not trivial to choose the accurate
number of scales in the image pyramid to supervise correctly every grid in the hierarchy
53

NERF MAP RECONSTRUCTION IN REAL-WORLD SETTINGS
Figure 3.8: Image Renderings. At 1/8th resolution from models solely trained with the
full resolution images. RING-NeRF is the only method capable of producing coherent
aliasing-free renderings thanks to its LOD inductive bias.
and especially the coarsest grids.
3.4.2
Robustness Experiments
3.4.2.1
Few Viewpoints Supervision
This experiment aims to evaluate the influence of the RING-NERF architecture and
pipeline on the reconstruction robustness to limited supervision viewpoints.
Dataset. We evaluate our contribution on the object-centric real dataset DTU [66], often
used in few-viewpoints evaluations. We follow usual protocols and use 15 scans among
the 124 existing of the DTU [66] dataset, with each scene the same training and evaluation
ID images (with thus the same camera poses).
Algorithms. We compare our architecture against several baselines: Mip-NeRF [11],
and FreeNerf [193] (a state-of-the-art method for this task), for vanilla architectures and
Nerfacto for grid-based architectures. To better demonstrate the intrinsic stability brought
by RING-NeRF, we also developed the Nerfacto+ architecture, which corresponds to
a Nerfacto architecture coupled with a coarse-to-fine training based on a progressive
activation of the grids (the rest of the decoder’s input being filled with zeros). For
Nerfacto+ and RING-NeRF, we also add FreeNeRF’s loss that penalizes the density of
the first 𝑀 = 10 samples of each ray to reduce as much as possible artefacts in front of
the cameras. As an ablative experiment, we also evaluate RING-NeRF using discrete
coarse-to-fine (fixed LOD increment of 1 rather than the proposed continuous increase).
Hyperparameters Regarding the model, we follow FreeNeRF’s choices as much as
possible and, because there are no grid-based methods to compare ourselves to, the
rest of the hyperparameters (mainly regarding the hashgrids) were derived from the
configuration used in our Novel View Synthesis experiments. Additionally, we followed
the number of samples used in FreeNeRF (128 samples) and also disable the mechanisms
of nerfacto dedicated to unbounded scenes : scene contraction and distortion loss. Our
experiments on Nerfacto/Nerfacto+ and RING-NeRF are all done using a pyramid of
9 grids of growth factor 2, a configuration similar to our NVS experiments where we

removed the last level of the highest resolution as we consider the object-centric DTU

of size 8.
Finally, we follow FreeNeRF’s density loss implementation and use the introduced
black and white prior, where we minimize the density of the 𝑀 + 10 (5 more samples than
in FreeNeRF’s implementation) first samples rather than the 𝑀 first when the value of the
pixel is either black or white, in order to not be penalized by the particular form of the
DTU dataset with uniform backgrounds.
Protocol. We follow FreeNeRF’s evaluation pipeline, including the number of supervision viewpoints (3 to 9), the choice of these views among the dataset and the evaluations
using masks of the object. Since we are using the same protocol, the results of FreeNeRF
and Mip-NeRF were taken out of FreeNeRF’s article.
We used the same training and evaluation protocols as FreeNeRF and its predecessors
PixelNeRF and Reg-NeRF. We also downsample each training and testing view by a factor
4, resulting in 300 x 400 pixels for each image. The masks used to evaluate the results are
the ones used by FreeNeRF.
Results. Evaluation results are reported in table 3.5. We first notice an important
difference between vanilla and grid-based baselines. While Mip-NeRF faces troubles in
reconstructing the scene with 3 views, the method seems to find coherency when adding
more images. However, Nerfacto struggles much more to form a consistent 3D scene even
when using 9 images (see figure 3.1). Even though the grid-based baseline is way faster to
train, its design implies more instability when facing a small number of images.
This does not mean however that few viewpoints are incompatible with grid-based
methods. Using progressive training coupled with a very simple and generalizable
regularization, both Nerfacto+ and our architecture succeed in creating coherent geometry.
Nonetheless, RING-NeRF considerably outperforms Nerfacto+, as shown in figure 3.9,
and with a PSNR difference varying from 3 to 4, demonstrating the stability increase
brought by our architecture, and also outperforms Mip-NeRF for the configuration with
3 and 6 supervision images. The discrete coarse-to-fine version of RING-NeRF performs
in-between Nerfacto+ and the complete RING-NeRF. This showcases both the intrinsic

56
3.4. EXPERIMENTS
Ablative Experiments. In order to better estimate the increase of stability brought by our
architecture, we further developed the ablative experiments on the complex few images
setup. Table 3.6 shows the results of different RING-NeRF versions on respectively 3, 6
and 9 images.
First of all, the results without both coarse-to-fine and density loss are, as expected,
quite low. However, we notice that its results are in average better than the basic Nerfacto
architecture, especially when training with 6 and 9 views. While both are low results, we
see qualitatively (see figure 3.10) that the sole RING-NeRF architecture mostly succeeds
in creating a coherent 3D geometry, although not perfect and most of all very affected by
floaters artifacts. This proves that our RING-NeRF architecture is more stable than the
concatenation architecture by design, even though it is still lacking of a way to get rid of
artefacts-inducing ambiguities.
We also evaluate the model both without coarse-to-fine and without the density loss
separately. Using solely the density loss surprisingly results in correct metrics. However,
the visualization demonstrates that the 3D reconstruction in itself is slightly less precise
than when using only coarse-to-fine, although the metrics are better as they are more
impacted by the artefacts. Moreover, xe observe that the reconstruction without coarseto-fine is less stable, with worse coherency in very hard setups, such as when observing
the scene with only 3 views and with novel views very far from the training views. Note
that, to further demonstrate that the density loss is not the sole explanation of the results,
figure 3.10 (b) shows the results of the nerfacto architecture coupled with the density
loss, and we notice that, while a beginning of 3D consistency appears, it is still way more
approximate than its RING-NeRF counterpart (e).
These experiments thus demonstrate that our model, coupled with continuous coarseto-fine is able to create a coherent and qualitative 3D reconstruction, although surrounded
by a huge amount of background misplaces, which have a huge impact on the metrics. The
simple density loss is an efficient solution to counter this issue, but without continuous
coarse-to-fine, it will in return slightly degrade the reconstruction, as well as loose in
stability. The combination of both is thus the best trade off between 3D reconstruction
and NVS quality.
3.4.2.2
SDF Reconstruction without Initialization
SDF reconstruction is known to be a more unstable process than density-based NeRF [6],
requiring a scene-specific initialization to converge. This initialization becomes an issue in
complex environments and incremental setups, where several types of scenes can co-exist
and are not necessarily known beforehand. Therefore, in this experiment, we evaluate the
ability of RING-NeRF and other SotA architectures to achieve SDF reconstruction without
scene-specific initialization.

Dataset. The evaluation is achieved on a subset of 7 scenes of the Replica[160] synthetic
indoor dataset. We also provide a qualitative real scene example (Ballroom) from the
Tanks & Temples [81] dataset. The estimated depths are provided by SDFStudio and
predicted via a pre-trained OmniData [38] model, they serve as an indicator rather than
true ground truths.
Algorithms.
We compare our architecture to two SDF methods, all of them implemented
in the same SDFStudio [200] branch of the Nerfstudio framework for fairer comparisons:
NeuS-facto, an adaptation of NeuS [177] for grid-based methods with Nerfacto modules,
and an implementation of NeuralAngelo[95]. The first one is considered a fast and efficient
Python SDF-based implementation and the latter is considered state-of-the-art for surface
reconstruction methods.
For these experiments, our model is built upon the NeuS-facto baseline, using in
particular the same NeuS-based SDF-to-density transformation[177].
Hyperparameters.
We mostly follow the basic Neuralangelo configuration implemented
by SDFStudio. This results in 16 levels of resolutions 32 to 4096 with features of size 8
and a hashmap size of 219 . The main differences regards the scheduling of the training,
initially implemented to last 500k epochs. However, we noticed a similar convergence
when scaling down the scheduling parameters for the training to last 100k epochs. Hence,
we decided to evaluate all three methods (RING-NeRF, NeuS-facto and Neuralangelo)
on 100K epochs (with accordingly scaled down schedulers and coarse-to-fine duration).
Because the Replica dataset is a synthetic indoor dataset, we remove background MLPs,
appearance embedding and scene contraction from all evaluated baselines.
Protocol. To evaluate the impact of the architecture and pipeline over the convergence
and stability, we suppress the inverted sphere SDF initialization scheme and use a random
initialization for the model.
Results without Initialization. Evaluation results are shown in table 3.4.2.2 and examples in Figures 3.11 and 3.1. First of all, NeuS-facto faces low rendering and reconstruction
metrics, due to catastrophic failure in most of the tested scenes (see figure 3.11) since the
model tends to re-draw the 2D images in front of the camera. Regarding NeuralAngelo,
its relatively high PSNR demonstrates its ability to synthesize satisfying RGB. However,
as illustrated in figure 3.1 and 3.11 and highlighted by the reconstruction metrics, the
58

values of the evaluation images of the entire scene.
Figure 3.12: Resulting Meshes for models without initialization. Chamfer Distances in

underlying geometry of the scene is poorly reconstructed, without any fine details. Finally,
our method RING-NeRF is by far the best performer, with much higher PSNR and a
better geometry including fine details although a bit noisy. The simple architecture of
RING-NeRF also permits faster epochs, thus faster training. Finally, we display an example
on the more complex real Tanks and Temples "Meeting Room" scene, as shown in Figure
3.13, whose results are coherent with the Replica dataset.
Figure 3.12 displays 3D reconstructions as meshed extracted using marching cubes
from the models with random initialization. As expected, the NeuS-facto mesh is completely useless, with the cloudy artefacts from the model resulting in opaque matter. On
the other hand, both NeuralAngelo and RING-NeRF produces coherent meshes. However, the NeuralAngelo mesh is very blurry and contains very little details, while ours is
