way more precise, although rather noisy. These qualitative results are coherent with the
previously described results.
Results with initialization. While not the subject of the initial experiment, it is interesting
to compare those results with their correctly initialized counterparts. Figure 3.14 gives an
example on one scene of the dataset.
First of all, our approach presents similar results whether we initialize the model or not.
This further proves that RING-NeRF is robust to the initialization process. We can notice
a slight difference in the PSNR metric which can be considered within the randomness
interval of two reconstruction reruns of the same model and scene.
NeuS-facto presents an expected behavior as it does not face catastrophic failure
anymore and succeeds in obtaining coherent RGB renders. However, the depth remains
a bit blurrier than RING-NeRF and the final PSNR results on the associated RGB images
are thus affected and remains slightly lower than RING-NeRF.
Regarding Neuralangelo, initializing the model improves both the RGB metrics and the
depth estimation. However, the depth is still surprisingly blurry, which in turns prevents
the RGB renders to overcome RING-NeRF results. This may be caused by their curvature
loss contribution, which is supposed to smooth the reconstruction. Because RING-NeRF
introduces a bit of noise in the reconstruction, combining thoses two contributions could
be an interesting research focus for robust and smooth SDF reconstruction.
3.4.3
Extensibility Experiments
3.4.3.1
LOD Extensibility: Adaptive Reconstruction
While resolution extensibility has not been heavily studied in previous works, it actually
is a crucial property in several different use cases and applications, notably for embodied
AI. This can be useful for better compression of NeRF models. An important issue in
grid-based NeRFs is the choice of hyper-parameters, and more especially the maximum
resolution of the grid pyramid. Being able to dynamically change the resolution of your
model gives the possibility to dynamically choose the optimal local maximum resolution
during training while exploring depending on the scene’s complexity, therefore discarding
60
3.4. EXPERIMENTS
Figure 3.14: Example of SDF reconstructions when using scene-specific initialization.
any useless parameters for a more optimal model. Moreover, for embedded systems with
important resources limitations, the usefulness becomes obligation. We can for instance
imagine two types of limitations in SLAM environment mapping situations, where the
agent is moving without interruption:
• Time limitation : Because it is constantly discovering new environment to reconstruct,
it does not have enough time to reconstruct with precision the previously seen areas.
However, RING-NeRF will let the robot optimizes with higher resolution previous
areas later on when it will have more resources available.
• Memory Limitation : As the scene to reconstruct is of unknown size and the system
possesses limited memory, it can not afford to reconstruct the entirety of the scene
with maximum precision. A more viable strategy would be to reconstruct coarsely
the majority of the scene and only reconstruct finer details on important areas
(whether the most looked at places or those with the most objects for instance).
The following experiment aims to demonstrate RING-NeRF’s unique ability to increase
dynamically the level of detail of the scene representation.
Dataset. We evaluate this property on the scan 114 of the DTU dataset and further
display it on the real-world Garden scene of the Mip-NeRF 360 dataset.
Algorithms.
Because I-NGP architectures [124, 12, 95] cannot perform LOD extensibility
(due to the fixed decoder’s input size), only RING-NeRF is evaluated.
Protocol. For the quantitative analysis, we train our model using two different configurations : one low resolution with a 3 levels grid hierarchy and one high resolution with
5 levels (the grid resolutions are 16, 32, 64, 128 and 256). For these two configurations
named "3 levels" and "5 levels", every grid and the decoder are trained simultaneously.
We proceed to showcase the extensibility of RING-NeRF by adding grids to the "3 levels"
61
CHAPTER 3. A FOUNDATION ARCHITECTURE FOR ROBUST AND EXTENSIBLE
NERF MAP RECONSTRUCTION IN REAL-WORLD SETTINGS
Figure 3.15: Resolution Extensibility. Learning curves and final renderings of RINGNeRF models with different grid configurations trained either jointly or incrementally.
Figure 3.16: Resolution Extensibility on the Garden scene of the 360 dataset. Apart
from the first grid, each novel grid is trained alone, with both the previous grids and the
decoder frozen.
configuration that is previously trained. We first train one grid of resolutions 128 ("3+1
levels" in Figure 3.15) with the three initial grids and the decoder frozen and then train
another grid of resolutions 256 ("3+1+1 levels" in Figures 3.15) with the four grids and the
decoder frozen.
Results. Figure 3.15 shows that the configuration "3+1+1 levels" results in the same
rendering quality than the "5 levels" one. This demonstrates the ability of our model to
dynamically change the resolution of the grid hierarchy. This is an important step towards
the development of an adaptive architecture which locally chooses the resolution of the
representation based on the scene’s content. This allows to drastically reduces the number
of parameters, helping in improving the memory footprint, the training duration and the
model’s robustness.
We then provide in Figure 3.16 a qualitative example on a more complex unbounded
scene. We follow the same experimental protocol with an increased hierarchy (6 levels
from 16 to 512 max resolution). We begin the reconstruction with only the first three levels,
train both the grid and the decoder to convergence and then freeze both of them. We
then proceed to train to convergence one novel grid at a time, freezing the previous one
at convergence. We notice that adding a grid always improves the reconstruction (with
an increasing PSNR). This showcases both the capacity of our model to reconstruct finer
62
3.4. EXPERIMENTS
Figure 3.17: Illustration of the catastrophic forgetting phenomenom. In (a), the pictures
are an evlolution across time of the reconstruction, as the camera moves from right to
left of the scene ("T" refers to the trajectory proportion). For each image, the camera
respectively focuses on the red, blue and green regions, thus gradually forgetting the
previous regions. In (b), we use a frozen pre-trained decoder and remove catastrophic
forgetting.
details after the decoder’s training and its ability to keep the coarser LOD valid.
3.4.3.2
Spatial Extensibility: Incremental Reconstruction
While achieving robust reconstruction is a major step toward deployable neural scene
representations, real-world embodied agents must also be able to build their environment
model incrementally as they explore it. Unlike conventional NeRF settings where all
training views are known in advance, an embodied agent perceives the world sequentially
through a continuous stream of observations. This calls for an incremental reconstruction
framework capable of integrating new views on the fly, without revisiting past data or
degrading already reconstructed regions.
One of the main obstacle to incremental NeRF training lies in catastrophic forgetting:
when fine-tuning a model on new data, previously learned content is often overwritten,
leading to degradation in earlier parts of the scene. This problem is somewhat alleviated
in grid-based NeRF variants, as their local feature grids allow for region-specific updates
and can even be dynamically expanded as the region of interest grows. However, this
benefit only holds if the decoder remains frozen and scene-agnostic, that is, able to decode
any feature vector into color and density independently of the scene it originated from.
In basic setups where the decoder is learnt jointly with the feature grids, this remains the
critical source of interference, since its shared parameters are globally updated across all
regions.
RING-NeRF, by design, naturally mitigates this issue. Its position- and scale-invariant
decoder learns to interpret latent features consistently across spatial and scale domains,
making it more robust to incremental fine-tuning. This invariance suggests that, if
pretrained, the decoder can serve as a transferable mapping function across scenes, an
essential property for continual and incremental reconstruction. In the following, we
present preliminary experiments conducted with Dumitru Pavel during an internship
partially under the supervision of this PhD, demonstrating that RING-NeRF, when pretrained, can be used for incremental reconstruction.
63
CHAPTER 3. A FOUNDATION ARCHITECTURE FOR ROBUST AND EXTENSIBLE
NERF MAP RECONSTRUCTION IN REAL-WORLD SETTINGS
Protocol. We explored this direction by implementing an incremental reconstruction
pipeline inside Nerfstudio, where a stream of images progressively sampled from a prerecorded video simulates a mobile robot navigating and continuously reconstructing
its environment. This implementation remains close to the standard NeRF training
procedure; the main modification lies in the data loader. Instead of providing all images
at the beginning of training, we modify the codebase so that images are introduced
sequentially throughout the optimization. To better emulate an autonomous robot, we
include an image memory bank that limits the number of frames the agent can store at
any given time, causing older observations to be gradually discarded.
Regarding the RING-NeRF decoder, it was pretrained offline across multiple scenes to
enforce a shared latent interpretation. This was achieved by jointly training several NeRFs
on different scenes, where each scene maintains its own feature grids while all share a
common decoder. Further details will be given in the next chapter.
Results. In the incremental setting, using a decoder trained as the agent discovers the
scene indeed results in catastrophic forgetting, as illustrated in Figure 3.17 (a). Objects
that were accurately reconstructed at earlier stages (e.g., the red post) gradually disappear
as the camera moves forward and these regions become less frequently observed.
In contrast, using the pretrained decoder within the same online strategy (Figure 3.17
(b)) effectively eliminates catastrophic forgetting, as expected. In this configuration,
the final reconstruction quality becomes comparable to that of an offline reconstruction,
demonstrating that RING-NeRF’s decoder architecture is well suited for use as a pretrained,
scene-agnostic component in an online mapping pipeline.
Although these preliminary experiments demonstrate successful incremental reconstructions without catastrophic forgetting, all subsequent NeRF models in this thesis are
trained in an offline setup, as online reconstruction falls outside the scope of this work.
These early results are presented solely to highlight RING-NeRF’s capacity to serve as the
geometric backbone of our conceptual embodied AI system, even when the system must
reconstruct incrementally its environment.
3.5
Conclusion
RING-NeRF provides a robust and versatile foundation for 3D scene reconstruction in
complex and unbounded environments. By design, it combines continuous multi-scale
modeling with position- and scale-invariant decoding, enabling aliasing-free and reliable
rendering without requiring additional supervision. Its efficiency and resilience to scenespecific conditions make it particularly suitable for deployment in embodied systems,
where reconstruction must remain stable under varying viewpoints, motion trajectories,
or incomplete observations. Moreover, its resolution and spatial extensibility form a
crucial basis for developing a fully functional embodied AI system where the model would
incrementally map its environment as new images arrive, while adapting reconstruction
64
3.5. CONCLUSION
precision to available resources and scene complexity, ultimately supporting integration
into a full SLAM framework. Such capabilities are essential for real-world applications,
where a robot must not only perceive but also maintain and update a consistent spatial
representation while navigating its environment.
However, while geometry provides the structural backbone of perception, it alone
is insufficient for an agent to effectively self-function, e.g. to navigate safely within its
surroundings. An embodied agent must also infer semantic understanding, i.e. distinguishing traversable surfaces from obstacles, recognizing manipulable objects, and
identifying the functional organization of the scene. In the following chapter, we therefore transition from pure reconstruction to semantic enrichment of NeRFs, focusing on
closed-set semantic segmentation as a step toward linking geometric representation and
environment understanding.
65
4
Enhancing the Functional Core via
Direct NeRF Semantic Segmentation
While robust geometry is essential for navigation, an embodied agent must also understand the
semantic content of its environment to interact with it effectively. This chapter addresses the challenge
of equipping Neural Radiance Fields with a functional semantic layer. We first introduce architectural
enhancements for scene-specific segmentation, proposing a direct interpretation pipeline that leverages
multi-scale features, spatial derivatives, and high-order interpolation to achieve robust segmentation
even under sparse or noisy supervision. Second, we tackle the problem of generalized semantic
segmentation, introducing a foundational framework based on vector quantization and foundation
model regularization to enforce feature consistency across scenes. Together, these contributions
transform the NeRF from a purely visual map into a semantically grounded representation capable
of supporting the agent’s self-functioning tasks.
4.1
Context and Motivation
In the previous chapter, we introduced RING-NeRF, a method designed to ensure robust
and scalable 3D reconstruction. This geometric backbone provides the agent with a stable
map of its surroundings, capable of handling real-world constraints like sparse views or
varying resolutions while being able to incrementally reconstruct as the agent discovers its
environment. However, for an embodied agent to be truly autonomous, robust geometry
is merely a prerequisite, not a solution. A robot possessing a perfect geometric map but
lacking any understanding of its content is essentially navigating a world of meaningless
shapes.
This capability is critical for nearly all downstream robotic tasks. In the context of
navigation, for instance, the agent must distinguish traversable surfaces (e.g., curtains)
from obstacles (e.g., walls, furniture) or hazards. Similarly, for fine-grained tasks like
manipulation or grasping, a semantic understanding is required to identify precise object boundaries and separate a target object from the background or clutter. Therefore,
grounding our Neural Field with a reliable semantic segmentation layer is the logical next
step toward a comprehensive scene model which can truly be used by autonomous agents.
66
4.1. CONTEXT AND MOTIVATION
As discussed in the Background chapter, numerous methods exist for the semantic segmentation of Neural Fields. However, the specific constraints of Embodied AI necessitate
a solution that satisfies two key properties:
• Direct Grid Interpretation: We seek to interpret the grid-based NeRF representations
themselves (specifically their feature grids) rather than relying on external pipelines
or additional modules which may require heavy decoding computation. While some
methods have explored direct interpretation for vanilla MLP-based NeRFs [207, 108,
49, 85], to the best of our knowledge, no equivalent solution exists for modern gridbased architectures where geometric grid features are interpreted by a semantic
decoder. Bridging this gap is crucial for two reasons. First, efficiency: direct
interpretation avoids the computational overhead of rendering and re-processing
views and also ensures that semantics are tightly coupled with the 3D geometry.
Second, necessity: as established in the previous chapters, grid-based architectures
are required for Embodied AI due to their speed and scalability compared to vanilla
NeRFs; therefore, enabling their direct semantic interpretation is a prerequisite for
their effective deployment in robotic systems.
• Generalizability: Most standard NeRF approaches require scene-specific training
with ground-truth semantic masks. However, a robot exploring a new environment
cannot be expected to possess such annotations for a room it has never seen. A truly
robust system must therefore be able to interpret geometric cues generally, without
needing supervision for a novel scene. While generalizable semantic methods exist [172, 100, 22], none achieve this via direct grid-based interpretation; they typically
rely on interpreting 2D semantic distilled priors or require excessive decoding and
rendering steps. Technically, this property translates to the requirement of a universal semantic decoder: a module capable of taking as input the feature grids from any
NeRF scene and producing segmentation, regardless of the specific environment.
These two requirements present distinct technical challenges. To address them systematically, we divide this chapter into two sequential studies.
First, we focus on scene-specific semantic segmentation within a grid-based NeRF.
In this setup, we assume access to 2D semantic maps during training. We investigate how
to best interpret the geometric feature codes stored in the grid and identify fundamental
limitations in naively adapting Vanilla-based interpretation methodes. Specifically, we
highlight the loss of implicit context inherent to local grid-based interpolation. We
thus propose contributions to address these issues, and evaluate how they improve
interpretation quality and robustness, notably against noisy or sparse annotations.
Second, we tackle the significantly more complex challenge of generalized NeRF
semantic segmentation. Here, the goal is to train a model capable of interpreting NeRF
geometric codes on a novel scene without having access to any semantic annotation for
that specific environment. After defining the specific hurdles imposed by grid-based
67
CHAPTER 4. ENHANCING THE FUNCTIONAL CORE VIA DIRECT NERF SEMANTIC
SEGMENTATION
NeRF representations in this context, namely training the semantic model on multiple
NeRF scenes at once and enforcing feature space consistency amongst different NeRF
optimizations, we propose architectural solutions, including a multi-scene pre-training
algorithm for the first issue; and the use of vector quantization and DINO-based feature
regularization regarding the second. It is important to note that this generalized approach
represents foundational work. While our experiments demonstrate the validity of our
proposed mechanisms, it remains a proof-of-concept that requires further scaling to reach
the performance levels of scene-specific methods.
To summarize, our contributions in this chapter are:
• We adapt semantic segmentation methodologies from MLP-based NeRFs to gridbased architectures, introducing architectural enhancements, specifically multi-scale
inputs, contextual derivatives, and higher-order interpolation, that overcome inherent limitations and significantly improve segmentation quality, particularly under
sparse and noisy supervision.
• We address the challenge of generalized semantic segmentation by identifying the
critical issues of generalizable NeRF training and latent space inconsistency, and
proposing a novel pipeline leveraging multi-scene pre-training, vector quantization, and foundation model distillation. We experimentally demonstrate that these
contributions successfully solve the identified issues, establishing a validated framework that, when leveraged at scale, paves the way for robust feed-forward NeRF
interpretation.
4.2
Direct Scene-Specific NeRF Semantic Segmentation
4.2.1
Method
We propose to advance the semantic segmentation of Neural Fields by developing a
framework tailored for the direct interpretation of feature grids. In the following sections,
we first establish a baseline by adapting the principles of Vanilla Semantic-NeRF to a gridbased architecture. We then introduce three specific architectural contributions designed
to augment this baseline and resolve its inherent limitations: first, providing multi-scale
information to the semantic decoder; second, explicitly encoding local shape and texture
cues via spatial derivatives; and finally, expanding the spatial receptive field by modifying
the underlying interpolation scheme.
Semantic Segmentation for NeRF. Building on the Vanilla Semantic-NeRF [207, 49, 85]
framework established in Chapter 2, we extend toward grid-based NeRF by attaching
a dedicated semantic segmentation head directly to the concatenated multi-resolution
feature grids. Formally, for any queried 3D point x, the feature grids produce a feature
vector f encoding local geometry and appearance. We feed this vector into a lightweight
68
4.2. DIRECT SCENE-SPECIFIC NERF SEMANTIC SEGMENTATION
Figure 4.1: Semantic Segmentation Pipeline Architecture. This architecture uses notably
the multi-scale contribution. Here, we simplify the model by using a grid hierarchy of
size 3.
Multi-Layer Perceptron (MLP) decoder Φ𝑠𝑒𝑚 , augmented with a softmax, which maps the
high-dimensional feature to a vector of class logits p ∈ R𝑁𝐶 , where 𝑁𝐶 is the number of
predictable semantic classes. We supervise the decoder using ground-truth 2D semantic
maps. The semantic logits are accumulated along rays via volume rendering to produce
2D semantic estimates, which are optimized using a standard multi-class Cross-Entropy
loss:
ℒ𝑠𝑒𝑚 = −
𝑁𝐶
Õ
𝑦 𝑐 log(𝑝 𝑐 )
(4.1)
𝑐=1
where 𝑦 𝑐 denotes the ground-truth class indicator and 𝑝 𝑐 is the c-th component of p, i.e.
the predicted probability for class c.
Crucially, we employ a stop-gradient operation between the semantic decoder and the
feature grid. Our goal is for the decoder to interpret the existing geometric features (densities and colors) rather than alter them. Backpropagating semantic gradients could degrade
geometric reconstruction or introduce artifacts to satisfy semantic constraints. More importantly, our final goal is to have a model which can directly interpret NeRF models
without any scene-specific ground-truth segmentation, meaning that our interpretation
model should infer semantics from the 3D NeRF geometry and radiance only.
However, we identify that relying solely on raw, point-wise concatenated grid features
f presents two fundamental limitations. First, grid-based representations are sensible
to scale. Ideally, interpretation should be scale-invariant (a miniature kid chair and a
normal chair should both have the same class) but raw grid features are sensitive to the
physical size and grid resolution of the object. Second and more importantly, grid-based
point-wise interpretation is inherently ill-posed; a specific density-color tuple (and by
extension, its generating feature code) is ambiguous and could belong to many different
semantic classes. Solving those ambiguities requires additional local information such
as texture or shape or simply wider context. These challenges motivate our proposed
enhancements: enforcing scale invariance for efficient multi-scale information, providing
explicit shapes and textures, and enabling inherent broader spatial context.
69
CHAPTER 4. ENHANCING THE FUNCTIONAL CORE VIA DIRECT NERF SEMANTIC
SEGMENTATION
Figure 4.2: Feature Derivatives (a) and Spline-NeRF (b) Contributions. We represent
those contributions on a simpler 2D grid setup.
Scale-Invariant Feature Space and Multi-Scale Information. A critical limitation of
standard grid-based representations lies in how they construct the input feature vector.
Typically, feature vectors interpolated from multiple grid resolutions are simply concatenated before being fed to the density and color MLPs for geometric reconstruction.
Because the optimization is driven by this composite input, each grid level naturally
learns a distinct, resolution-specific feature distribution. Consequently, when we feed
this same concatenated vector to a semantic decoder, it receives a composite input where
each segment resides in a disjoint latent space. Given that the semantic decoder is a
lightweight fully-connected MLP (i.e. each feature of each resolution passes by all the
network’s neurons), partitioning its limited capacity to learn separate interpretation rules
for each resolution is highly inefficient. Ideally, we seek a single, scale-invariant latent
space, where the feature distribution remains consistent regardless of the resolution level.
To achieve this, we leverage the RING-NeRF architecture introduced in the previous
chapter. By design, RING-NeRF enforces a level-of-detail (LOD) inductive bias, training a
single geometric decoder to interpret features interpolated from any continuous resolution.
This process implicitly aligns the latent spaces of all grid levels into a unified, scale-invariant
manifold, which motivates the use of RING-NeRF inside our semantic segmentation
framework.
However, we diverge slightly from the geometric strategy regarding input aggregation. While geometric reconstruction of each sample relies on the features of a specific
continuous LOD, semantic understanding benefits significantly from analyzing the scene
at multiple scales simultaneously, grasping both the coarse context of an object and its
fine-grained details. Therefore, we provide the semantic decoder with a concatenation
of features from all grid resolutions. Crucially, unlike the baseline approach, each of
these concatenated features now reside in the shared, scale-invariant latent space established by RING-NeRF. This allows the decoder to effectively aggregate multi-scale context
without the burden of adapting to multiple disjoint feature distributions, focusing its
capacity entirely toward semantic interpretation. The resulting architecture is illustrated
in Figure 4.1.
70
4.2. DIRECT SCENE-SPECIFIC NERF SEMANTIC SEGMENTATION
Context Aggregation for Shapes and Textures. As previously discussed, feature vectors
extracted at a single point provide only punctual density and appearance information.
Resolving semantic ambiguity from such strictly local data is fundamentally ill-posed,
as any class may share identical point-wise properties. To resolve this, the decoder
requires access for each sample to additional contextual information describing its local
surroundings. This can be done in multiple ways. Here, we propose to provide per-sample
information regarding shape (geometric variation) and texture (appearance variation).
These properties are implicitly encoded as spatial variations of the latent code: a change in
the feature vector across space correlates to a change in the underlying physical properties
of the scene.
To explicitly provide this information to the decoder, we propose computing the spatial
analytical derivative of the feature vector at the sampled point, as illustrated in Figure 4.2
(a). We measure how this code varies along the canonical axes 𝑥, 𝑦, and 𝑧. We formulate
this aggregate variation f′ (𝑥, 𝑦, 𝑧) using a central difference approximation:
′
f (𝑥, 𝑦, 𝑧) =
f(𝑥 + Δ𝑥, 𝑦, 𝑧) − f(𝑥 − Δ𝑥, 𝑦, 𝑧)
,
2Δ𝑥
f(𝑥, 𝑦 + Δ𝑦, 𝑧) − f(𝑥, 𝑦 − Δ𝑦, 𝑧)
,

(4.2)
where f(𝑥, 𝑦, 𝑧) represents the interpolated feature vector at the query coordinates. The
resulting variation vector f′ is concatenated with the original feature f before being passed
to the semantic decoder.
Crucially, the step size Δ is not an arbitrary hyperparameter. Although we use
the analytical derivative formulas, we intentionally avoid taking the limit Δ → 0, as an
instantaneous gradient would restrict the receptive field to the current voxel, preventing the
decoder from aggregating context from neighboring voxels. To maximize the contextual
utility, we set Δ equal to the voxel size of the specific grid resolution being queried. This
forces the derivative calculation to work as a finite difference computation and simply
sample the neighboring voxels. In our multi-resolution hierarchy, this means each grid
level contributes a derivative calculated at its own native scale, providing the decoder
with nested information about feature variations from coarse to fine detail.
High-Order Interpolation: Spline-NeRF. Standard grid-based NeRFs typically utilize
trilinear interpolation to reconstruct a grid feature f from discrete grid vertices. While
this interpolation scheme is computationally efficient for rendering, it introduces two
fundamental limitations that directly hinder the performance of our semantic pipeline:
1. Limited Spatial Context. Standard trilinear interpolation reconstructs a point using
only its 23 = 8 nearest neighbors. This strictly local support limits the semantic
71
CHAPTER 4. ENHANCING THE FUNCTIONAL CORE VIA DIRECT NERF SEMANTIC
SEGMENTATION
context available to the feature code, effectively creating a "tunnel vision" effect
where the decoder struggles to integrate broader regional cues.
2. Gradient Aliasing and Grid Artifacts. In a linear grid, the field of codes is 𝒞 0 ,
meaning that its gradient is discontinuous at every voxel boundary. Consequently,
derivative calculations, even via finite differences like we perform, are sensitive to
grid alignment. As a sampling ray traverses the volume, the computed derivative
can fluctuate abruptly purely due to the grid discretization, rather than actual scene
geometry, especially at the vertices borders. This introduces high-frequency noise
into the semantic decoder, forcing the network to waste capacity learning to filter
out these artifacts.
To address this, we introduce Spline-NeRF, a RING-NeRF framework where we
replace the standard linear kernel with high-order B-Spline interpolation. Specifically, we
propose Quadratic and Cubic interpolations.
To implement this, we build directly upon the continuous field formulation established in RING-NeRF (Chapter 3). Recall that we defined the multi-resolution feature
grid encoding not as a discrete array of voxels, but as a continuous mapping function
parameterized by a lattice of control points. While Chapter 3 utilized standard linear
interpolation (effectively a Degree-1 B-Spline) for efficiency, this framework naturally
extends to higher-order bases. In this section, we simply generalize this mapping: we
retain the exact same lattice of control points (the grid indices), but we replace the linear
kernel with B-Spline basis functions of degree 𝑛, specifically Quadratic (𝑛 = 2) and Cubic
(𝑛 = 3). Formally, the feature vector f(x) at any continuous position x is computed by
convolving the local neighborhood of control points with these wider, smoother kernels.
In practise, as illustrated in Figure 4.2, this involves summing the contributions of the
local control points 𝜃i , weighted by the B-Spline kernel 𝛽 𝑛 :
Õ

of the chosen kernel degree 𝑛.
For efficient implementation, we exploit the separability of the B-Spline kernel. The
3D interpolation is decomposed into sequential 1D operations along each axis. In the
Quadratic (𝑛 = 2) case, which enables 𝒞 1 continuity, the interpolated value f(𝑡) at a

This matrix formulation remains constant regardless of the grid resolution, enabling us to
implement optimized CUDA kernels for both quadratic and cubic interpolation.
The primary benefit of B-Splines for semantic interpretation is the drastic increase
in the interpolation support (receptive field). Compared to the 8 neighbors used in trilinear interpolation, Quadratic and Cubic splines aggregate information from 33 = 27 and
43 = 64 neighbors respectively. This expanded support ensures that every single queried
feature code inherently integrates context from a wider spatial neighborhood, effectively
acting as an intrinsic 3D convolution during the feature extraction step. This provides
the semantic decoder with a wider view of the local geometry, significantly improving its
ability to resolve semantic ambiguities without increasing the network depth.
Additionally, the shift to B-Splines imposes a strong smoothness prior on the representation. By enforcing 𝒞 1 (Quadratic) or 𝒞 2 (Cubic) continuity, Spline-NeRF naturally
filters out the high-frequency discontinuities inherent to linear grids, enabling stabler
differences as input to the decoder.
Beyond our specific semantic segmentation task, replacing linear interpolation with
higher-order splines improves NeRF in other ways. Notably, several NeRF reconstruction
techniques rely on higher-order differential properties that will benefit from the improved
𝒞 1 or 𝒞 2 properties. Notably, Signed Distance Field (SDF) reconstruction requires satisfying the Eikonal equation (|∇𝑠| = 1), which necessitates a valid and ideally continuous
gradient field, even on vertices. Similarly, methods like Neuralangelo utilize curvature
constraints (requiring the Hessian, or 2nd-order derivatives) to regularize surface normals.
Spline-NeRF makes the grid-based representation compatible with these advanced loss
functions by providing analytically valid 1st and 2nd-order derivatives everywhere in the
volume, paving the way for high-fidelity grid-based reconstruction.
4.2.2
Experiments
In this section, we evaluate the effectiveness of our contributions for the task of scenespecific NeRF semantic segmentation. We begin by detailing the implementation and
hyperparameter settings used throughout our experiments. Next, we present a comprehensive evaluation of our method, demonstrating how our proposed enhancements
significantly improve segmentation quality compared to standard baselines. We then
73
CHAPTER 4. ENHANCING THE FUNCTIONAL CORE VIA DIRECT NERF SEMANTIC
SEGMENTATION
conduct ablation studies to justify our design choices. Finally, to validate the robustness of
our approach for challenging real-world setups, we assess performance under imperfect
supervision, specifically evaluating the model’s resilience to noisy and low-resolution
ground-truth annotations.
Implementation Details and Baselines.
We conduct our evaluations on the Replica
dataset. To rigorously assess the isolated impact of our proposed interpretation modules,
we first establish a baseline based on the standard Nerfacto architecture equipped with a
semantic head, as first presented without the additional contributions. Due to the lack of
direct competitors performing scene-specific interpretation of feature grids, our analysis
primarily focuses on an ablation study: we incrementally introduce our contributions to
demonstrate their respective improvements over this baseline.
Regarding the Spline-NeRF contribution, ensuring a fair computational comparison
is critical. To this end, we implemented the Quadratic and Cubic interpolation kernels
directly within the original feature grids codebase, inside the optimized tiny-cuda-nn
C++/CUDA library. This ensures that any observed performance differences are due to
the interpolations themselves rather than implementation inefficiencies compared to the
default linear kernel.
We report the mean Intersection over Union (mIoU) and total pixel accuracy (Acc)
averaged across the 8 Replica scenes. Additionally, specifically for the Spline-NeRF
contributions, to assess the computational impact of our methods, we report the inference
speed in Frames Per Second (FPS).
Hyperparameters. To ensure a fair evaluation, we maintain a consistent geometric
backbone across all experiments, varying only the semantic interpretation modules. For
the underlying scene representation, we utilize a multi-resolution hash encoding consisting
of 16 grid levels, with resolutions spanning from 16 to 2048 and a feature dimension of 8
per level. All models are trained for 5000 steps.
Importantly, our proposed semantic contributions are agnostic to these specific hyperparameter choices. The architectural enhancements do not depend on a specific grid size
or feature dimension; they are compatible with any grid-based configuration, provided
that the underlying geometric reconstruction converges effectively.
Comparative Analysis. We present the quantitative results of our semantic segmentation
contributions in Table 4.1 and also display qualitative examples in Figure 4.3.
The baseline Nerfacto method achieves a relatively low mIoU of 37.88%. While this
shows that our adaptation of Vanilla NeRF semantic segmentation works, this also confirms
that relying on raw, point-wise feature interpolation is insufficient for resolving semantic
ambiguities in complex scenes.
74
4.2. DIRECT SCENE-SPECIFIC NERF SEMANTIC SEGMENTATION
Figure 4.3: Scene-Specific Contributions. We qualitatively evaluate the impact of adding
all our contributions to the naive Nerfacto baselines. All scenes were trained under
identical conditions.
• RING-NeRF: As mentioned earlier, replacing feature concatenation with the RINGNeRF architecture enables latent space unification across the resolutions, which
improves the segmentation results by +3.91% mIoU and +2.12% mAcc.
• Multi-Scale Input (+MS): Concatenating features from all resolutions yields a very
significant performance boost (+18.59% mIoU!). This validates our hypothesis that
the decoder greatly benefits from simultaneous access to coarse and fine geometric
context.
• Semantic Derivatives (+D): The addition of spatial derivatives also improves considerably the segmentation quality, pushing the performance to +5.51% mIoU. By
explicitly providing local shape and texture variation cues to the decoder, we enable
it to smooth predictions in consistent regions while sharpening boundaries between
distinct objects. While this is true independently of the multi-scale input, the performance boost is all the more important when we provide multi-scale shapes and
textures.
Finally, we evaluate the impact of replacing the standard linear interpolation with
high-order Quadratic and Cubic B-Splines (rows +Spline). Mathematically, higher-order
interpolation provides a continuous gradient field, which theoretically aids the optimization landscape. Our results confirm this: spline interpolation consistently yields
a slight improvement in both reconstruction quality (in average +0.22 and +0.15 PSNR
respectively for Nerfacto and RING-NeRF) and semantic segmentation (in average +0.71,
+0.56 and +1.12 mIoU respectively for Nerfacto, RING-NeRF and RING-NeRF with our
contributions) compared to the linear baseline.
However, this slight performance gain comes at a severe computational cost. Despite
our optimized CUDA implementation, the high-order interpolations result in an important reduction in inference speed (respectively −41% and −50% for quadrative and cubic
interpolations in RING-NeRF with contributions). While Spline-NeRF offers a valid mathematical path to smoother and more contextual fields, the marginal gain in segmentation

quality does not justify the prohibitive computational overhead for embodied applications. Consequently, for the remainder of this thesis, we retain the Linear + Multi-Scale +
Derivatives configuration as our optimal operating point.

Robustness to Imperfect Supervision. In real-world robotic deployments, obtaining
pixel-perfect semantic annotations is often infeasible. Human and automatic annotations
are prone to errors (noise) and dense annotation is prohibitively expensive (necessitating
sparse or low-resolution supervision). To validate the practical utility of our method, we
evaluate its resilience under these two regimes of imperfect supervision.
Resilience to Label Noise. First, we simulate annotation errors by applying random noise
to the ground-truth masks during training. Specifically, we randomly corrupt a fixed
percentage of pixels in the segmentation maps by reassigning their semantic class to
another random class present in the scene. An example of this corruption is shown in
Figure 4.4. Table 4.2 reports the segmentation performance retention compared to clean

supervision.
We observe that all our baselines maintain high segmentation accuracy even when
a significant portion of the training pixels are randomly corrupted. This robustness
stems primarily from our core paradigm of interpreting geometry. A random pixel flip
in the segmentation map introduces high-frequency noise that is inconsistent with the
underlying, continuous geometric features which represent density and color and remain
smooth and coherent. By stopping gradients and forcing the decoder to interpret these
consistent structural codes, the geometry acts as a strong regularizer that filters out
incoherent label noise. Furthermore, our specific contributions also enhance this stability.
By providing broader spatial context, they allow the decoder to base its prediction on a
robust combination of features rather than a single ambiguous point, making the model
less susceptible to isolated label errors compared to the baseline.
Efficiency with Low-Resolution Supervision. Second, we evaluate the data efficiency of
our approach by drastically reducing the spatial resolution of the ground-truth supervision.
Specifically, we generate downscaled supervision masks by applying majority-vote pooling
across patches of varying sizes (specifically 2 × 2, 4 × 4, and 16 × 16 pixels). This results in
extremely coarse segmentation maps where fine details and precise object boundaries are
lost, as shown in Figure 4.4.
Remarkably, as demonstrated in Table 4.3, despite the blocky and coarse nature of the
supervision, our method successfully recovers sharp object boundaries at inference time.
77
CHAPTER 4. ENHANCING THE FUNCTIONAL CORE VIA DIRECT NERF SEMANTIC
SEGMENTATION
This is fundamentally possible because the feature grid operates at a higher resolution than
the semantic supervision: the decoder learns to associate the coarse semantic labels with
the fine-grained geometric features provided by the RGB-supervised grid. This capability
is further amplified by our contextual derivatives contribution. Since the derivatives
explicitly encode geometric boundaries and texture changes, they provide the decoder
with the precise edge information that is missing from the downscaled ground truth,
effectively guiding the segmentation to follow the physical object borders even within
coarse supervision blocks.
4.3
Generalized NeRF Semantic Segmentation
4.3.1
Problematics
Although the semantic segmentation methods presented in the previous sections yield
qualitative results, they suffer from a significant limitation: the necessity to have access to
ground-truth 2D annotations of the scene during training. This reliance on supervision
is a critical issue for real-world applications, particularly in the context of embodied AI.
An agent incrementally discovering a novel environment cannot reasonably rely on the
existence of dense segmentation maps for every new room it explores. To address this, we
aimed to adapt our previously introduced framework, which directly interprets feature
grids using a semantic decoder enriched with multi-scale and contextual information, into
a generalized system. The goal is to obtain a semantic decoder that can be trained once
on a set of scenes and then applied to any novel NeRF reconstruction without requiring
any further fine-tuning.
To our knowledge, no existing method has successfully tackled this problem of segmenting grid-based NeRFs without scene-specific maps by directly interpreting the feature grid.
While some solutions for generalized NeRF segmentation exist, they typically operate on
the outputs of the model rather than its internal structure, requiring either computationally expensive multi-view 2D renderings [22, 100] or global 3D scene decoding [172]. Our
objective is to bypass these intermediate steps and interpretate the geometric neural field
directly from a single shared semantic decoder. However, achieving this generalization
presents two major challenges.
The first challenge concerns the training paradigm itself. Standard NeRF pipelines
are inherently designed to optimize a single representation for a single scene. To learn a
generalized semantic decoder, we must devise a multi-scene training strategy which can
optimize a shared interpretation module simultaneously across a diverse set of scenes.
The second challenge is less obvious but no less critical: it stems from the inherently
scene-specific nature of NeRF representations. A standard NeRF optimization offers no
guarantee that the learned feature space will be consistent across different training runs,
even for the same scene and even with a pre-trained frozen decoder. To illustrate this
lack of stochasticity, we conducted a motivating experiment shown in Figure 4.5. We first
78
4.3. GENERALIZED NERF SEMANTIC SEGMENTATION
Figure 4.5: Lack of Constraints in NeRF Training - Re-run Setup. We first train our
semantic decoder to interpret Scene 1. We then evaluate its capacity to segment Scene 2,
which corresponds to a second, independent training of the same scene (grid + geometric
decoder) using RGB supervision only.
trained a basic semantic NeRF until convergence. We then froze the learned semantic
and geometric decoders and re-trained only the feature grids on the exact same scene
using only the RGB reconstruction loss. Logic suggests that if the grid represents the same
geometry, the semantic decoder should still work. However, as shown in the figure, the
result is a catastrophic collapse of the segmentation. The second training run produced
a feature grid that was geometrically accurate but mostly unintelligible to the frozen
semantic decoder, resulting in large artifacts and class changes.
This experiment highlights that without specific constraints, the latent codes in a gridbased NeRF are arbitrary: there is no shared "language" or feature alignment between
different trainings. To solve this, one must develop a reconstruction process where the
learned codes remain coherent and interpretable from one training to another. In the
following, we describe the solutions proposed during this thesis in collaborative work
with Jalil Benhiba during a research internship directed by myself.
We introduce three technical contributions to address these challenges: first, we
propose a strategy to train the semantic decoder across multiple scenes simultaneously;
second, we introduce a feature codebook inspired by vector-quantization techniques to
strictly constrain the latent space; and finally, we leverage foundation models to serve as
latent space regularizers. We demonstrate how these solutions enforce feature alignment,
paving the way for efficient grid-based generalized semantic segmentation. It is important
to note that while these contributions successfully resolve the theoretical inconsistencies,
the current quantitative results represent a foundational proof-of-concept; achieving
performance competitive with fully supervised, scene-specific approaches will require
further scaling in terms of compute, data, and architecture. Consequently, we dedicate
the final section of this chapter to outlining the specific perspectives and scaling strategies
required to bridge this gap.
79
CHAPTER 4. ENHANCING THE FUNCTIONAL CORE VIA DIRECT NERF SEMANTIC
SEGMENTATION
4.3.2
Proposed Method
In the following, we describe our contributions developed for consistent latent spaces
for generalizable semantic segmentation, first regarding the multi-scene NeRF decoder
training, and then about constraining the latent space by using Vector Quantization
techniques and foundation models as regularizers.
Decoder Pre-Training Strategy. To enable the pre-training of a generalized semantic
decoder across a dataset of NeRF scenes, we require a training strategy where each scene
contributes effectively to the learning of shared weights. We investigated two distinct
paradigms to address this multi-scene optimization challenge.
The first approach is a sequential training strategy inspired by meta-learning techniques, specifically the Reptile algorithm [128]. Originally designed to learn optimal
weight initializations across multiple tasks, this method involves training the decoder
on a single NeRF scene until convergence, and then repeating this process in a loop
over the dataset, sampling scenes randomly with repetition. Between each scene-specific
optimization, the decoder weights are updated using an Exponential Moving Average
(EMA) of the weights before and after the optimization step. However, we observed a
critical flaw in this paradigm: catastrophic forgetting. By the end of the global training
loop, the decoder tends to lose the ability to interpret the scenes (and semantic concepts)
encountered at the beginning of the process, making it unsuitable for our goal.
The second paradigm follows a joint training approach, mirroring standard batchbased training in computer vision. Here, the objective is to optimize the entire dataset of
NeRFs simultaneously while sharing a single semantic decoder. This prevents the model
from overfitting to any single scene’s specific geometry and ensures that the decoder does
not forget previous scenes. However, because loading dozens of full NeRF representations
into GPU memory simultaneously is infeasible, we implement this via an interleaved
batching strategy. Every 𝑁𝑠𝑤𝑎𝑝 steps, we swap the active subset of scenes being optimized,
ensuring that all scenes in the dataset progress toward convergence in parallel. The
rationale behind this simultaneous optimization is to force the decoder to learn robust,
common features across the entire dataset, which should thereby reduce the scene-specific
bias of the representation. This is also the pre-training algorithm we use in the previous
RING-NeRF chapter for incremental reconstruction.
Furthermore, we extend this pre-training strategy to include the geometric decoder
(density and color). As discussed in the RING-NeRF chapter, employing a shared geometric
decoder across diverse scenes forces the optimization to store all scene-specific data within
the feature grid rather than in the decoder weights. This separation is equally critical
for direct generalizable semantic interpretation. Moreover, it is also a first step towards
having a shared latent space across the feature grids from all scenes, thus facilitating
interpretation of novel scenes.
80
4.3. GENERALIZED NERF SEMANTIC SEGMENTATION
Latent Space Constraining via Vector Quantization. However, the multi-scene training strategy described above is, by itself, insufficient to guarantee generalization. As
highlighted in Section 4.3.1, training a NeRF on a specific scene, even with a pre-trained
geometric decoder, does not guarantee that the feature grid will converge to a consistent
latent representation. Since the mapping between geometry/color and the feature space
is ill-posed, there exists an infinite number of potential latent codes capable of representing the same density and radiance. Without explicit constraints, the stochasticity of the
optimization of a new scene is likely to create a drift into a region of the feature space that
the pre-trained decoder has never encountered, rendering interpretation impossible.
To address this, we first propose to strictly constrain the usable feature values by
introducing a quantization mechanism. Inspired by Vector Quantized Variational AutoEncoders (VQ-VAE) [131], we force each of the feature grids to take values from a finite,
discrete and shared set of learned codes, referred to as a codebook. This codebook acts as
a shared "vocabulary" learned across the varied dataset: just as the decoder learns to interpret semantics, the codebook learns a discrete basis of features capable of reconstructing
any density, color, and semantic class found in the training set.
By discretizing the latent space, we drastically reduce the degrees of freedom available
to the feature grid optimization. The NeRF is forced to reconstruct the scene using only
the interpretable codes available in the dictionary. Consequently, during the inference of a
new scene, the grid optimization is guided toward this shared, semantic-aware manifold,
ensuring compatibility with the frozen semantic decoder.
Technically, we implement this by inserting a quantization layer between the interpolated feature grid and both decoders. Let 𝑓 (x) be the continuous feature vector interpolated
from the grid at position x, and let ℬ = {𝑒 𝑘 }𝐾𝑘=1 be the codebook containing 𝐾 discrete
vectors. The quantization process replaces 𝑓 (x) with its nearest neighbor 𝑓𝑞 (x) in the
codebook:
𝑓𝑞 (x) = arg min∥ 𝑓 (x) − 𝑒 𝑘 ∥2
𝑒 𝑘 ∈ℬ
(4.6)
We also note that when we provide multi-scale information to the semantic decoder, as
done in the previous section, we quantize the information of each scale with one single
codebook shared across resolutions, because the features are scale-invariant.
In this setup, the feature grids, the semantic decoder, and the codebook vectors
are trained jointly on multiple scenes using the interleaved batching strategy described
previously. The global objective function is defined as:
ℒ𝑡𝑟 𝑎𝑖𝑛 = ℒ𝑟 𝑔𝑏 + ℒ𝑠𝑒𝑚 + ℒ𝑐𝑜𝑑𝑒𝑏𝑜𝑜 𝑘 + 𝜆ℒ𝑐𝑜𝑚𝑚𝑖𝑡
(4.7)
where:
• ℒ𝑟 𝑔𝑏 is the standard Mean Squared Error (MSE) between the rendered color and the
ground truth pixel color.
• ℒ𝑠𝑒𝑚 is the standard cross-entropy loss between the predicted semantic class and
the ground truth label.
81
CHAPTER 4. ENHANCING THE FUNCTIONAL CORE VIA DIRECT NERF SEMANTIC
SEGMENTATION
• ℒ𝑐𝑜𝑑𝑒𝑏𝑜𝑜 𝑘 = ∥sg[ 𝑓 ] − 𝑓𝑞 ∥22 optimizes the codebook vectors. It pulls the chosen code 𝑓𝑞
towards the continuous feature 𝑓 produced by the grid. The operator sg[·] denotes
the stop-gradient operation.
• ℒ𝑐𝑜𝑚𝑚𝑖𝑡 = ∥ 𝑓 − sg[ 𝑓𝑞 ]∥22 is the commitment loss. Conversely to the codebook loss, it
pulls the continuous grid feature 𝑓 towards the chosen discrete code 𝑓𝑞 . This term is
crucial as it forces the grid to commit to a specific code in the dictionary, preventing
the output from fluctuating between codes.
We employ a weighting factor 𝜆 = 0.25, determined empirically, to balance the commitment
constraint.
Regarding the inference phase, where we reconstruct a semantic NeRF on a novel,
unseen scene using only RGB images (no semantic annotations), both the pre-trained
decoders and the learned codebook are frozen. Only the feature grid of the new scene is
optimized.
The objective function for this semantic inference is:
ℒ𝑖𝑛 𝑓 𝑒𝑟𝑒𝑛𝑐𝑒 = ℒ𝑟 𝑔𝑏 + ℒ𝑐𝑜𝑚𝑚𝑖𝑡
(4.8)
Crucially, we remove ℒ𝑠𝑒𝑚 (as no ground truth is available) and ℒ𝑐𝑜𝑑𝑒𝑏𝑜𝑜 𝑘 (as the codebook
is frozen). The supervision relies entirely on ℒ𝑟 𝑔𝑏 for geometry and appearance, and
ℒ𝑐𝑜𝑚𝑚𝑖𝑡 for feature regularization. The commitment loss forces the grid to organize its
features such that they map to valid codes in the frozen codebook. By doing so, the
grid naturally aligns itself with the latent space the decoder understands, enabling the
generation of semantic segmentations despite the absence of direct supervision.
Regularizing the Latent Space via 2D Foundation Models. A final research direction
to improve generalization is to inject semantic priors directly into the feature grids.
Specifically, we investigate the use of self-supervised 2D foundation models, such as
DINO [19], to influence the organization of the grid latent space.
It is first important to acknowledge that this approach seemingly contradicts our
primary objective: to semantically segment a scene by interpreting only its geometric
and radiance codes. Indeed, if we were to fully distill DINO features into the grid, we
arguably shift paradigms from "interpreting geometry" to "building a standard semantic
feature field" (like LeRF). Such fields often require heavier decoding heads and additional
pre-processing memory, which we aimed to avoid.
However, we propose to use DINO here not as a target representation to be perfectly
replicated, but as a semantic regularizer. The rationale is that DINO features possess powerful invariance properties: they cluster visually similar elements and separate dissimilar
ones, regardless of viewpoint, deformation, or noise. For instance, patches of a same
person yield similar DINO descriptors across different images and scenes. By forcing our
geometric feature grid to be predictive of these descriptors, we implicitly encourage the
latent space to adopt this semantic structure. This structural alignment is particularly
82
4.3. GENERALIZED NERF SEMANTIC SEGMENTATION
valuable for our generalized task, as it could drastically reduce the number of scenes
required to train the generalized decoder by providing a consistent, "universal" target
latent space.
We implement this as a usual feature field learning problem. We attach a lightweight
auxiliary MLP head ΦDINO to our feature grid (specifically after the codebook quantization
step when used). This head is tasked with predicting the respective DINO features
extracted from the training images by a frozen, pre-trained DINO encoder D.
Crucially, contrary to our semantic decoder, we allow gradients from this DINO head
to backpropagate into the feature grid and codebook. This modifies the grid’s latent
values to be not only geometrically accurate (via ℒ𝑟 𝑔𝑏 ) but also semantically structured.
The additional supervision term is defined as:
ℒDINO = ∥ΦDINO (f) − D(r)∥22
(4.9)
where ΦDINO (f𝑖 ) is the predicted feature from the grid code, and D(r) is the ground-truth
DINO descriptor extracted from the corresponding image patch.
4.3.3
Experimental Validation
In this section, we present experimental results aiming to validate that our proposed
architectural solutions effectively resolve the feature space inconsistency issues. All
evaluations are performed on the same Replica dataset.
Our experimental analysis is structured in three progressive steps. First, we evaluate
the introduction of the codebook in a standard, scene-specific setting. This serves as a
sanity check to ensure that discretizing the feature space does not degrade the quality
of geometric reconstruction or semantic interpretation compared to continuous feature
grids. Second, we evaluate whether our codebook and DINO contributions force the
feature space to align across independent training runs of a single same scene, as this is
a prerequisite for any generalized decoder. Finally, we use the multi-scene pre-training
algorithm to deploy the full pipeline and address the target task of segmenting novel,
unseen scenes without any semantic fine-tuning.
Protocol and Hyperparameters. Unless otherwise stated, we utilize the same hyperparameters established in the previous experimental section. We perform ablations on
the codebook size to determine the optimal vocabulary capacity. Regarding the dataset
protocols: for the scene-specific and consistency experiments, we report results averaged
over all 8 Replica scenes. For the generalized segmentation experiments, we adopt a
cross-validation protocol, training on a split of 6 scenes and evaluating on the remaining
2 held-out scenes to test zero-shot transfer capabilities.
Impact of our Contributions on Scene-Specific Performance. Before evaluating the
generalization capabilities of our framework, we must first verify that our proposed
83

SEGMENTATION

number of available vectors inside the codebook and evaluate the geometric reconstruction.
architectural constraints, specifically the codebook and DINO distillation, do not fundamentally compromise the network’s ability to reconstruct and interpret a scene in a
standard, scene-specific setting.
First, we evaluate the isolated impact of the codebook. We integrate the quantization
layer into a basic RING-NeRF pipeline (excluding the multi-scale and derivative enhancements) and train it end-to-end on single scenes. Table 4.4 presents the reconstruction and
segmentation metrics with various codebook sizes, and Figure 4.6 illustrates it.
As anticipated, discretizing the feature space introduces a representational bottleneck.
We observe a quantitative decrease in both geometric and semantic performance compared
to the unconstrained, continuous baseline ("No codebook"). However, the method does not
collapse; it remains capable of valid reconstruction and segmentation. Crucially, there is a
clear trend: increasing the vocabulary size of the codebook reduces the quantization error,
progressively bridging the gap toward the continuous baseline. This confirms that with
sufficient capacity, a discrete feature manifold can effectively approximate the continuous
scene representation. In the following, we’ll use a codebook size of 100 vectors.

Next, we re-introduce the semantic enhancements developed in Section 4.2.1 (Multiscale input and Contextual Derivatives) to assess if they can compensate for the quantization loss. These results are reported in Table 4.5.
The results demonstrate the synergy between our contributions. While the Codebook
alone lags behind the baselines (respectively −10.50 mIoU and −11.80 mIoU for Nerfacto
and RING-NeRF), adding Multi-scale information and Derivatives enables the quantized
method to not only recover but outperform the standard unquantized Nerfacto baseline
in segmentation (+13.57 mIoU). This suggests that while quantization limits expressivity,
the structured context provided by our contributions greatly aids interpretation, even
when using a codebook. It is however important to note that we do not fully recover
the best unquantized performance (50.17 mIoU vs 60.70 mIoU for the RING-NeRF with
multi-scale and derivatives). Finally, as expected, the addition of DINO distillation yields
a boost in semantic quality (+3.65 mIoU), although with a trade-off in geometric fidelity
(−1.10 PSNR) due to the multi-task optimization balance.

In summary, these experiments confirm that while vector quantization inherently
imposes a constraint on the representation, it does not forbid high-quality reconstruction
or segmentation. When supported by a sufficient codebook size and context-aware inputs,
the quantized pipeline serves as a robust baseline. Moreover, the usage of DINO distillation
does not sacrifice the RGB quality reconstruction for semantic segmentation. This validates
our architectural choices and justifies moving forward to the generalized setting, where
these constraints become the key to cross-scene consistency.
Evaluation of Feature Consistency (Re-Run Experiment). Having established the scenespecific baseline performance, we now address the core challenge identified: the stochasticity of the NeRF latent space. To verify that our contributions enforce feature alignment,
we replicate the motivating experiment described earlier. The protocol is as follows:
1. We train the full pipeline (Geometry + Semantics + Codebook/DINO) on a scene
until convergence.
2. We freeze the learned semantic and geometric decoders (and codebook and DINO
head, if used).
85
CHAPTER 4. ENHANCING THE FUNCTIONAL CORE VIA DIRECT NERF SEMANTIC
SEGMENTATION
3. We re-train solely the geometric feature grid on the same scene from scratch, without
the semantic loss and with pre-trained frozen decoders.
4. We evaluate the semantic segmentation performance of this new grid with the former
semantic decoder.
If our method successfully constrains the latent space, the new grid should naturally converge to a representation compatible with the frozen decoder, yielding close segmentation
scores to the scene-specific paradigm.
Table 4.6 presents the results. For each metric, we report the performance of the retrained model, with the value in parentheses indicating the drop in performance compared
to the original scene-specific run (Δ = Re-run − Original).
The results offer compelling validation of our hypothesis. The naive method (without
codebook) exhibits a catastrophic collapse in segmentation performance, confirming
that standard NeRFs do not learn reproducible features. Introducing the codebook
significantly mitigates this issue; by restricting the grid to a discrete vocabulary, we force
the optimization into a more repeatable manifold, resulting in a substantial gain in mIoU
compared to the naive baseline, catching up to +21.94 mIoU. However, a noticeable gap
remains compared to the original run, suggesting that the codebook alone provides a
necessary but not fully sufficient constraint for perfect alignment. As expected, augmenting
the model with our previous contributions reduces this gap progressively, although not
fully (50.17 mIoU vs 43.18 mIoU respectively before and after re-run with all contributions
except DINO).
Crucially, the addition of DINO distillation bridges this remaining gap. By acting as a
constant, view-invariant semantic anchor during optimization, DINO forces the grid to
converge to an explicit semantic structure. This results in very high consistency, where
the re-trained grid is almost entirely compatible with the frozen decoder.
These quantitative findings are visually corroborated in Figure 4.7, where the naive rerun produces incoherent segmentation, while our contributions globally preserves object
boundaries and identities. When comparing those with the scene-specific segmentation
in Figure 4.3, we also notice the increase in segmentation consistency.
In summary, this experiment proves that our architectural constraints, specifically
Vector Quantization and DINO regularization, successfully solve the problem of feature
space stochasticity. They transform the NeRF optimization from an arbitrary geometric
fitting process into a constrained, reproducible feature learning task, clearing the path for
generalized inference.

our contributions enforce feature consistency across training runs, we now deploy the full
pipeline in the target scenario of Generalized Semantic Segmentation. In this setup, we
train the semantic decoder (and codebook) on a subset of 6 Replica scenes. We then freeze

Qualitative results are presented in Figure 4.8. First of all, we can notice that the
naive baseline (Nerfacto trained on multi-scene) without feature consistency contributions
output segmentation results with very low quality. Only some very specific objects (e.g.
the table) are well labelled. When adding the contributions, while the segmentations are
rather noisy and with some errors, they demonstrate better spatial coherence, with much
better object classification which proves that the feature grid seems to be converging to
the expected latent space. Furthermore, identifying the distinct failure modes allows us
to decouple the performance of our mechanism from the limitations stemming from the
data.
The configuration leveraging DINO priors yields the most promising results. The
segmentation successfully captures major structural elements (walls, floors) and distinct
objects. However, errors occur primarily on objects present in the test scene that were
either absent or visually distinct in the small training set. This highlights a vocabulary
gap: while the decoder effectively generalizes concepts it has seen, it cannot infer the class
