CHAPTER 4. ENHANCING THE FUNCTIONAL CORE VIA DIRECT NERF SEMANTIC
SEGMENTATION
Figure 4.8: Multi-Scene Results. We qualitatively evaluate the impact of adding our
contributions to the naive method. All scenes were trained under identical conditions.
of an out-of-distribution object (e.g., a specific vase or furniture style unique to the test
scene). Since DINO features are inherently robust to appearance changes, this suggests
that the primary bottleneck is the limited diversity of the training set (6 scenes) rather
than the method itself.
When relying solely on the codebook for feature consistency, the performance drop is
more significant, manifesting primarily as increased high-frequency segmentation noise.
This degradation is attributable to the absence of DINO regularization, which otherwise
acts to harmonize feature codes spatially. Apart from this type of regularization, solutions
could rely on giving more context to the semantic decoder, notably by scaling the decoder’s
architecture so that it takes as input many neighbor codes at once. However, regarding the
classification errors, we notice better object classification compared to the DINO version.
This means that incorporating DINO indeed regularizes the latent space, at the cost of
regrouping the codes of similar (but distinct) classes.
In summary, these experiments confirm that our contributions are on the right path
to solve the fundamental mechanical issues of NeRF generalization (feature alignment
and multi-scene training). The remaining performance gap is attributable to the scale
of the data and model capacity. As discussed in the next perspectives section, we are
confident that scaling this pipeline would reduce these vocabulary gaps, as the probability
of encountering novel objects would diminish significantly.
4.3.4
Perspectives and Future Work
Scaling Data and Architecture. As discussed, while our technical contributions successfully resolve the theoretical hurdles of feature alignment, multi-scene training, and
segmentation generalizability, our current experimental results represent a foundational
88
4.3. GENERALIZED NERF SEMANTIC SEGMENTATION
Figure 4.9: Segmentation Example in ScanNet++. The segmentations were done with all
our contributions including the codebook and DINO.
proof-of-concept. To bridge the gap between this prototype and a truly deployable generalized semantic NeRF, several distinct improvement axes must be explored.
The primary bottleneck identified in our experiments is the scale of the learning
process.
• Data Diversity. Our reliance on the Replica dataset (8 scenes) provides a training
set that is orders of magnitude too small to capture the semantic diversity of the
real world. Generalization requires that the model encounters a vast vocabulary
of objects and varying geometries during pre-training. Although the use of DINO
distillation mitigates this issue by integrating generalizable semantic priors into the
grids, a novel scene containing an object never seen in the training set will inevitably
lead to segmentation failure due to the untrained semantic decoder. To address this,
future work should leverage large-scale, real-world datasets such as ScanNet++ (for
which we initiated preliminary re-run experiments, as shown in Figure 4.9), forcing
the codebook to learn a truly universal set of geometric-semantic primitives.
• Architectural Capacity. While our lightweight MLP decoder proved sufficient when
guided by strong DINO priors, it appears to be a limiting factor when relying solely
on the codebook as the discretization of the feature space is a harsh constraint. Future
iterations might benefit from more expressive decoding architectures (e.g., small
Transformers or Attention-based heads) capable of reasoning about the relationships
between multi-scale features and larger contexts more effectively than a simple
perceptron.
• Optimized Implementations. Finally, a powerful generalized model requires an
optimization regimen that matches its ambition. This includes extended training
horizons and, crucially, a highly optimized codebook implementation. Our current
PyTorch-based implementation entails significant memory overhead and computational latency, thus limiting greatly its maximum size; it would benefit substantially
from being implemented directly in C++/CUDA within the tiny-cuda-nn library.
Beyond Segmentation. Beyond the immediate goal of semantic segmentation, the mechanisms we have introduced, specifically multi-scene pre-training and grid quantization,
open exciting perspectives for various sub-domains of Neural Fields.
89
CHAPTER 4. ENHANCING THE FUNCTIONAL CORE VIA DIRECT NERF SEMANTIC
SEGMENTATION
Figure 4.10: NeRF Editing Example. By forcing all scene-specific information to be stored
in the feature grids, we can edit the NeRF by manipulating those grids as if we were
manipulating voxel grids.
First, as demonstrated in the previous chapter, pre-training the decoder serves purposes
beyond simple interpretation. Notably, our method could inherently prevent catastrophic
forgetting in incremental learning scenarios. Furthermore, we believe a frozen, pre-trained
decoder can act as a strong geometric regularizer, potentially aiding reconstruction in
complex or constrained settings by forcing the model to generate "plausible" features
consistent with the distribution of correctly reconstructed NeRFs.
Second, pre-training the decoder enables NeRF editing. By removing the scene-specific
dependency of the decoder, we force the feature grids to fully encode the scene’s geometry
and appearance, much like a voxel grid. By manipulating these feature grids directly,
similarly to techniques used in Control-NeRF [87], we can alter the scene content: e.g.
performing 6-DoF displacement, duplication, and removal of objects. Figure 4.10 demonstrates preliminary examples of object editing achieved by manipulating our feature grids
with a pre-trained decoder. It is important to note, however, that this introduces secondary
challenges we did not fully pursue, particularly regarding the update of proposal samplers
to reflect the modified density field.
Finally, regarding the codebook contribution, vector quantization offers a natural path
toward extreme NeRF compression. Moreover, this points toward a vision of a "generalizable codebook": a standardized dictionary of neural codes shared across thousands
of scenes. Such harmonization would not only drastically reduce training times for new
environments but could also enable cross-scene analytics and content search at a scale
currently impossible with isolated, per-scene NeRFs, effectively alleviating some of the
constraints of per-scene optimization.
In summary, the contributions presented here extend far beyond a single segmentation
task. They lay the groundwork for a wide array of applications and could offer novel
methodological solutions to important challenges in the field.
4.4
Conclusion
In this chapter, we tackled the challenge of fixed-set pre-defined interpretation, aiming
to transform a purely geometric Neural Field into a semantically aware representation.
90
4.4. CONCLUSION
We first proposed a scene-specific grid-based NeRF segmentation baseline and then
introduced several architectural enhancements such as adding context to decoders and
feature grids, and multi-scale inputs, that improve the quality of the segmentations even
when trained with sparse or noisy ground-truth annotations. We then addressed the
significantly harder problem of generalized semantic segmentation. We identified the core
obstacles preventing standard NeRFs from generalizing (namely, latent space inconsistency,
multi-scene training, and dataset scaling) and proposed novel solutions relying on multiscene pre-training, vector quantization, and foundation model distillation. While fully
deployable generalization will require scaling these contributions to larger datasets and
architectures and use optimized implementations, our preliminary results, particularly
those leveraging DINO priors, are highly encouraging and prove that grid-based NeRFs
can indeed be interpreted in a feed-forward manner.
This chapter constitutes the second pillar of our unified scene representation. By
combining the robust, incremental reconstruction of RING-NeRF with the fine-grained,
reliable semantic understanding, we have established a solid Functional Core for our
embodied agent. This semantic grounding is essential for the agent’s basic self-functioning,
providing for instance the traversability cues necessary for safe navigation and the dense
geometric definition required for object manipulation and grasping. However, perception
alone is not action. For a robot to truly be useful, it must go beyond autonomous
operation to understand and solve user-defined tasks. "Knowing there is a chair" is
distinct from "Understanding which chair the user refers to." To bridge this gap, we must
augment the agent’s cognitive core, its "brain", with modules to both understand the
NeRF representation and react to user queries. The next chapter focuses on this critical
evolution: we augment our representation with an open-vocabulary, task-driven module
that empowers the agent to focus on specific regions based on the user requirements.
91
5
Task-Driven Discovery as a Perceptual
Focusing Tool for the Cognitive Core
While fixed semantic categories are sufficient for navigation, a truly interactive agent must be able to
adapt its perception to open-ended user requests. Standard methods typically force a trade-off: they
either adapt strictly to the user’s query (Open-Vocabulary Segmentation) or strictly to the scene’s
geometry (Unsupervised Segmentation). This chapter introduces DiSCO-3D, the first framework
to bridge this gap by addressing the novel task of 3D Open-Vocabulary Sub-concepts Discovery.
Built upon our Neural Field representation, DiSCO-3D combines unsupervised scene decomposition
with weak open-vocabulary guidance, allowing the agent to discover and segment in the scene
specific sub-concepts relevant to a text query. We demonstrate that this approach not only enables
fine-grained, focus-driven sub-concepts discovery but also achieves state-of-the-art performance on
the generalized tasks of both open-vocabulary and unsupervised segmentation.
Project page can be found at : https://cea-list.github.io/DiSCO-3D/
5.1
Context and Motivation
In the preceding chapters, we have progressively enriched the NeRF representation to
serve as a robust perceptual foundation for Embodied AI. With RING-NeRF (Chapter 3),
we ensured the geometric stability required for incremental mapping in the wild. With our
Semantic Segmentation contributions (Chapter 4), we provided the agent with a reliable
understanding of fixed semantic categories. This establishes the agent’s Functional Core:
it enables "self-functioning" capabilities such as collision-free navigation, traversability
analysis, and basic object manipulation.
However, for a robot to be truly useful, it must go beyond these pre-defined routines.
It must be able to solve tasks that are not known beforehand. Specifically, it must react to
live, open-ended user requests. "Navigate to the kitchen" requires a fixed map while "Find
me something to fix this loose screw" requires a flexible, query-driven understanding of
the scene.
To achieve this, the system must not only understand the scene but also adapt this
understanding to the specific task at hand. In practice, there is no single "correct" semantic
92
5.1. CONTEXT AND MOTIVATION
Figure 5.1: We introduce the 3D Open-Vocabulary Sub-concepts Discovery (OV-SD)
paradigm, which aims to provide a 3D semantic segmentation adapted to both the scene
(semantic classes are discovered from the scene content) and user queries (semantic classes
should be semantically related to the queries, i.e. sub-concepts of queries). DiSCO-3D is
the first solution to address this challenge and stands out for its versatility, as it covers
3D OV-SD’s edge cases: Open-Vocabulary Segmentation and Unsupervised Semantic
Segmentation.
segmentation for a given environment. The suitability of a particular decomposition
depends entirely on how well it preserves relevant information for the user’s current goal.
This means that the semantic segmentation should be adapted to both the content of the
scene and the task’s requirements.
Adaptation to the downstream task should involve not only providing the relevant
semantic classes for the task, but also excluding irrelevant ones since, as underlined by
[39], they would behave as distractors. Scene adaptation, on the other hand, should require
that the output semantic classes provide the most fine-grained semantic description of
the scene, while also excluding classes absent in the scene. To illustrate the difference, if
a scene contains a television, a hammer and a screwdriver, and the task requires the use
of tools, task adaptation implies to ignore the "television" class while adaptation to the
scene implies to provide the two classes "hammer" and "screwdriver" instead of a single
class "tools" or a list of all existing tools whether or not they actually are in the scene.
However, supervised approaches [210] as well as more recent Open-Vocabulary methods
(OV-Seg) [136, 76, 105, 158] focus on adapting segmentation to a specific downstream
task by requiring users to specify task-relevant classes, whereas recent works on 3D
Unsupervised Semantic Segmentation (USS) [205, 104] focus exclusively on adapting the
semantic classes to the scene through label-free decomposition. To our knowledge, no
solution provides both adaptations.
We introduce 3D Open-Vocabulary Sub-concepts Discovery (3D OV-SD), which involves
providing the most relevant segmentation of a 3D scene regarding its content and a
downstream task defined through a user query. We propose DiSCO-3D as the first
solution, consisting in plugging into a Neural Field [120] representation an USS module
93
CHAPTER 5. TASK-DRIVEN DISCOVERY AS A PERCEPTUAL FOCUSING TOOL FOR
THE COGNITIVE CORE
partially supervised by an OV-Seg. As illustrated in Figure 5.1, DiSCO-3D not only
addresses OV-SD but also generalizes to its edge cases: 3D OV-Seg (when queries target a
single sub-concept) and 3D USS (when no query is provided).
In thischapter, our main contributions are:
1. We introduce 3D OV-SD, a new 3D semantic segmentation task providing adaptive
segmentations based on scene context and user-defined queries. We also propose
a quantitative benchmark by extending Replica’s semantic classes and providing a
suitable evaluation protocol.
2. We present DiSCO-3D, the first method designed to solve the 3D OV-SD problem,
combining Unsupervised Semantic Segmentation with Open-Vocabulary Segmentation guidance to serve as a direct plug-in to NeRF.
3. We evaluate DiSCO-3D on both real and synthetic data, demonstrating better performance than hand-designed naive baselines on the proposed OV-SD task and
experimentally show that our solution produces state-of-the-art performances on
the OV-SD edge cases of NeRF Open-Vocabulary Segmentation and Unsupervised
Semantic Segmentation, highlighting its versatility.
4. We propose a framework where DiSCO-3D is provided as an interpretation tool to
an LLM and show that it successfully uses DiSCO-3D to answer open-ended user
questions on the scene, demonstrating the effective integration inside an embodied
agent’s cognitive core.
5.2
Related Works
Unsupervised Semantic Segmentation. Due to the difficulty of obtaining large annotated datasets, the unsupervised paradigm has attracted attention for image semantic
segmentation. Recently, 2D USS approaches have adopted self-supervised pre-trained
models, such as DINO [19], as input for deep clustering modules. In particular, STEGO
[55] inspired a range of techniques by revealing the correlation between unsupervised
network features and true semantic labels. This line of research has recently expanded
with methods like ACSeg [93], EAGLE [77], and SmooSeg [86], which focus on online clustering of pixel-level features, typically by contrasting these features into easily classifiable
groups. Apart from 2D USS ideas, some recent methods [205, 104] focus on performing unsupervised semantic segmentation directly on 3D point clouds. These methods
demonstrate an increasing interest in transferring label-free semantic segmentation to 3D.
While USS methods adjust segmentation to the scene’s content, they are independent
of downstream tasks, making the output classes poorly suited for follow-up applications.
3D Open-Vocabulary Segmentation. 2D Open-Vocabulary segmentation methods rely
on the use of pre-trained vision-language models such as CLIP [142], sharing a feature

Figure 5.2: Overview of DiSCO-3D for a LeRF Feature Field. DiSCO-3D inputs pairs of
features from 3D samples into a projector network learnt to accentuate semantic disparities.
Those projected features are then classified by comparing them to class-specific prototypes
(subsection 5.3.2). Thoses prototypes are updated each epoch using an EMA with the
projected features. A user query can be used to supervise the projector by encouraging the
prototypes to be divided into either relevant or irrelevant classes, enabling the semantic
segmentation of only task-relevant sub-concepts (subsection 5.3.3).
space for both image and text encodings. Although the original models produce perimage embeddings, several solutions focus on computing pixel-wise features for precise
segmentation [90, 50, 206, 186]. Due to the high cost of 3D data acquisition and annotation,
developing 3D foundation models is challenging, which has led to extensive research
on applying 2D Vision-Language models for 3D open-vocabulary segmentation. Many
approaches have indeed proposed to distill various 2D foundation models [142, 19, 132,
80, 214, 50] into various 3D representations ranging from point clouds [136] to more recent
Neural Radiance Fields [120] and Gaussian Splatting [75]. More specifically, NeRF-based
distillation into so-called feature fields are particularly well-known as the ray-based nature
of NeRF is highly compatible with feature distillation. Notably, many works distill various
image encoders into their models for different semantic applications [82, 23, 170, 157, 109,
78], ranging from semantic segmentation [82, 170] to open-vocabulary segmentation [76,
105]. Some methods, such as LeRF [76] or LEGaussians [158], combine different feature
fields into a single model to leverage the strengths of each encoder.
Although 3D open-vocabulary segmentation methods decompose scenes based on
user queries, their semantic labels are limited to these concepts. Our method rather
automatically discovers sub-concepts, offering a richer scene description and flexibility
for real-world applications.
95
CHAPTER 5. TASK-DRIVEN DISCOVERY AS A PERCEPTUAL FOCUSING TOOL FOR
THE COGNITIVE CORE
5.3
Method
5.3.1
Problem Statement and Overview
The objective of our method is to provide a solution to the previously introduced 3D
Open-Vocabulary Sub-concepts Discovery problem, specialized to the case of Neural Field
[120] representations. As illustrated in Figure 5.1, it consists in providing a 3D semantic
segmentation of the scene related to one or several user queries without explicit nomination of each of the semantic classes. This task requires both to understand what’s
relevant in a scene relative to user queries and being able to semantically cluster these
relevant objects without any additional information on the user requirements. Natural
solutions would include the successive works of both open-vocabulary understanding
of the scene to decipher query relevancy across the scene and unsupervised semantic
segmentation to propose scene-adapted semantic decomposition (in any order). However, performing these two sub-tasks successively gives sub-optimal results, as will be
demonstrated in subsection 5.4.2 and this is why we build DiSCO-3D to perform them
simultaneously: an Unsupervised Semantic Segmentation module based on prototypes
that automatically discovers semantic classes in the scene, alongside a parallel mechanism
leveraging open-vocabulary segmentation to guide the USS toward sub-concepts related
to one or more user queries. Those modules are connected together through the use of a
shared architecture, as illustrated in Figure 5.2.
To reach this objective, our solution relies on a Neural Field representation of the 3D
scene augmented with feature fields of both a queryable representation (eg. an OpenVocabulary field [142, 50]) and a spatially precise semantic representation [19, 132]. For
ease of understanding, we first consider the specific case of a pre-trained frozen LeRF [76]
which includes jointly a multi-scale CLIP [142] pyramid and a DINO [19] feature field.
However, other feature fields can be used, as discussed in subsection 5.3.4. Although we
assume a frozen representation here, our method is fully compatible with online scenarios
where the feature fields are reconstructed incrementally in parallel, notably for embodied
AI applications.
In the following, we present our method in three parts. We first introduce a method
of 3D Unsupervised Semantic segmentation designed for Neural Fields (subsection 5.3.2).
We then extend this approach to perform open-vocabulary sub-concepts discovery by
incorporating open-vocabulary guidance into the USS process (subsection 5.3.3). Finally,
we introduce several extensions to handle more complex queries and diverse semantic
representations (subsection 5.3.4).
5.3.2
Unsupervised Semantic Segmentation for LeRF
To the best of our knowledge, USS approaches have never been adapted to the continuous
NeRF representation. Hence, to perform 3D unsupervised semantic segmentation from a
LeRF representation, we draw inspiration from well-known prototypes-based 2D methods
96
5.3. METHOD
[55, 93, 77, 86] which focus on clustering semantic features from pre-trained vision models
like DINO. Similarly to these approaches, our solution relies on learning a non-linear
projector that maps the scene DINO features obtained from LeRF onto a new latent space
where the projected features are agglomerated around cluster centroids (also named
prototypes) which each describe a semantic class.
Given a 3D sample 𝑘, its probability distribution of class assignment 𝐷 𝑘 is computed
as the softmax (augmented with an additional 𝛽 sharpness hyperparameter) of the cosine

Regarding the projector architecture, we follow SmooSeg [86] and combine a non-linear
MLP (with SiLU activations) with a linear layer serving as a residual connection. It is
however to be noted that the impact (in both quality and time) is minimal, as evaluated
later on in the ablative experiments.
The training process is achieved per batch of ℬ DINO features which we obtain by
casting rays from randomly sampled pixels across all available posed images, and then
sampling the scene along these rays, following usual NeRF pipelines. While the projector
is optimized through standard back-propagation, the prototypes are updated using a
different strategy. At a given epoch 𝑛, each prototype 𝒫𝑖 is updated using the weighted

Moreover, unlike image or point cloud representations, the relevance of the DINO feature
field varies depending on the 3D position within the scene. Specifically, if a sample’s 3D
location is in free space or inside an object, the corresponding DINO feature value becomes
meaningless and should not significantly contribute to the training process. Because the
relevance of 3D samples along a given ray is determined by their density weights 𝑤 𝑖

The projector’s supervision presents two main challenges. The first is ensuring the
projection maintains the semantic consistency of the DINO features, preserving the
distance relationships between them (i.e., features close in DINO space should remain
close in the output space, and vice versa). The second challenge is achieving well-separated
clusters with sharp probability distributions.
To address the first challenge, we incorporate a loss, denoted ℒ𝑝𝑟𝑜 𝑗 , designed to
maintain the relationships between DINO features and their projected counterparts. This
loss, commonly known as correlation loss [55], smoothness loss [86] or correspondence
distillation loss [77], uses pairs of samples to supervise the projector (using a stopgrad
operation on the prototypes) by encouraging pairs that are close in DINO space (i.e. closer
than a fixed hyperparameter 𝑏) to have similar probability distributions while encouraging
pairs that are distant to exhibit more divergence in their distributions:
ℬ
ℒ𝑝𝑟𝑜 𝑗 =
1 Õ 𝑓 𝑘 · 𝑓𝑙
(
− 𝑏)(1 − 𝐷 𝑘 · 𝐷𝑙 )
ℬ
|| 𝑓 𝑘 |||| 𝑓𝑙 ||
(5.3)
𝑘,𝑙
Although this loss maintains semantic consistency, it does not prevent the probability
distribution of a projected feature of the scene from being uniform or relatively smooth
over the semantic classes. To solve this issue, unlike other USS methods relying on additional losses [86], we propose to enforce a progressive sharp agglomeration of the samples
around their associated prototypes by introducing a scheduled linear decaying of the 𝛽
parameter (defined in Equation 5.1) to progressively separate the clusters.
5.3.3
Open-vocabulary Guidance for Sub-concepts Discovery
We build on the previously introduced LeRF USS segmentation approach to address the
core challenge of our paper: 3D Open-Vocabulary Sub-concepts Discovery. Let’s first
consider the scenario of a unique query.
Discovering Query-Relevant Sub-concepts. We aim in the following to discover and segment 𝑁𝑞 sub-concepts of a scene related to a user query represented as a CLIP embedding
𝑞. Since these sub-concepts are not specified by the user and depend on the scene, it is
not possible to provide a supervision for each of them. On the other hand, the irrelevant
semantic parts of the scene are implicitly defined by the query. We thus propose, as
illustrated in Figure 5.2, to extend the previously presented NeRF USS method by adding
an additional irrelevant semantic class with 𝑁𝑖𝑟𝑟 associated prototypes (corresponding to
index 𝑁𝑞 + 1 to 𝑁𝑞 + 𝑁𝑖𝑟𝑟 ). The projector is then supervised by an additional loss function

is the one-hot vector associated with the relevant semantic class. This loss uses the CLIP
field to supervise the DINO projector by maximizing the probabilities of irrelevant classes
for samples unrelated to the query, and minimizing them for other samples. Combined
with ℒ𝑝𝑟𝑜 𝑗 applied on relevant classes, it enables the model to focus on discovering and
segmenting relevant sub-concepts.
Semantic Recovery and CLIP-Guided Regularization. Because the projector is specific
to each training process, the prototypes are defined on an arbitrary feature space rather
than a scene-agnostic foundation model’s embedding space. This limits the ability to fully
leverage the results, allowing only the use of the sub-concepts segmentation maps but
without the ability to understand them.
To handle this problem, we propose to associate a 𝒫𝑖𝐶𝐿𝐼𝑃 embedding to each prototype
𝒫𝑖 . These are initialized as zero embeddings and are updated using the same EMA process
as the base prototypes, with the weighted mean of the CLIP embeddings of the batch
samples associated with the corresponding class, as illustrated in Figure 5.2.
These characteristic embeddings can be used for various applications, notably a posteriori concept retrieval as illustrated in subsection 5.4.2. Moreover, by design, this generic
prototype definition can be applied to any available feature field (e.g., DINO in LeRF)
using the same approach, enabling further semantic understanding.
These CLIP prototypes can also be leveraged during optimization to enhance semantic
segmentation performance. Since the projector uses DINO features as input which tends
to produce over-segmented features (i.e. describing object parts rather than entire objects),
we regularize the model using CLIP semantics (which are more object-consistent) by
introducing a final loss term based on the CLIP prototypes. This loss, denoted as ℒ𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑡𝑜 ,
drives the projected DINO features closer to the prototype whose CLIP embedding is
most similar to the sample’s CLIP embedding:
ℬ
1 Õ
ℒ𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑡𝑜 =
(1 − 𝐷 𝑘 · 𝐻 𝑘𝑇 )
ℬ
(5.5)
𝑘
where 𝐻 𝑘 is a one-hot tensor defined such that the one is at argmax ( 𝑓 𝑘𝐶𝐿𝐼𝑃 .𝒫𝑖𝐶𝐿𝐼𝑃 ) for
𝑖∈[1,𝑁]
every sample of the batch.
5.3.4
Method extensions
Multiple and complex queries. So far, we have described the method with a single query
for clarity. However, DiSCO-3D can process multiple simultaneous queries 𝑄 = {𝑞 𝑖 , 𝑖 ∈
[1, 𝐾]}, as long as we define a priori which prototypes are relevant for each query (although
not studied in this thesis, a well-defined cognitive core/LLM should be able to perform
the prototypes definition too). While the losses ℒ𝑝𝑟𝑜 𝑗 and ℒ𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑡𝑜 remain unchanged, a loss
99
CHAPTER 5. TASK-DRIVEN DISCOVERY AS A PERCEPTUAL FOCUSING TOOL FOR
THE COGNITIVE CORE
Figure 5.3: DiSCO-3D Qualitative Evaluation for OV-SD. We present results for various
queries, scenes (which originate from [76, 99, 160]) and feature fields (LeRF in orange
and OpenNeRF in blue). (b), (e) and (f) illustrate multiple queries, respectively disjoint,
overlapping and nested, (g) a visual query encoded with CLIP and (h) a CLIP feature
obtained by a user click as query. Finally, (i) and (j) are OV-SD edge cases, where (i) has 1
sub-concept (OV-Seg) and (j) has no user query (USS).
𝑞
ℒ𝑖𝑟𝑟𝑖 is added for each query 𝑞 𝑖 following Equation 5.4. Each of these losses is guided by a
unique one-hot vector 𝐻𝑞 𝑖 that defines the relevant prototypes for each query. Notably, it is
filled with ones for the defined relevant prototypes and filled with zeros elsewhere (both
irrelevant and non-overlapping prototypes from other queries). This formulation allows
full flexibility, supporting overlapping, disjoint, or nested queries without additional
constraints.
Extending to other Features Fields. Although we present our method using a pretrained LeRF as input, DiSCO-3D is compatible with a wide range of feature fields (and
their combinations) as long as two conditions are met. First, the projector requires at
least one spatially precise feature field to perform segmentation (e.g., dense encoders).
Second, the scene must be represented by at least one feature type that can be compared
to a query. Given these conditions, the input 3D representations and query modalities
can vary widely, from a single feature field satisfying both requirements (e.g. OpenSeg)
to alternative inputs such as user clicks, as demonstrated in Figure 5.3.
5.4
Experiments
After introducing some implementation and evaluation details in subsection 5.4.1, we
first present evaluations on the novel Open-Vocabulary Sub-concepts Discovery problem
with a dedicated benchmark in subsection 5.4.2. Finally, we successively propose experiments for the edge cases of Open-Vocabulary Segmentation and Unsupervised Semantic
Segmentation respectively in subsection 5.4.3 and subsection 5.4.4.
5.4.1
Implementation and evaluation details
We use the same Nerfacto model for every evaluation for the radiance field and regarding
the feature fields, we follow LeRF’s implementation and use an additional set of grids
100
5.4. EXPERIMENTS
shared amongst features with a dedicated MLP decoder for each field.
All quantitative experiments, including DiSCO-3D and the comparative baselines, use
the same pre-trained Nerfacto models and feature fields as input. All our experiments
were run on the same single RTX 4090 GPU. They run for 100 epochs each, at approximately 20ms per epoch (resulting in ∼2s optimization per query, which can be considered
fast enough for most practical applications, including in embodied AI).
Hyperparameters. In the following, we describe the used hyperparameters in the following experiments. However, it should be noted that most of those parameters, have
minimal impact on the performances.
• Pre-trained Feature Fields. The configurations for the feature fields follow standard
setups defined by LeRF. We use a set of hashgrids disjoint from the Nerfacto grids
of 24 levels (219 dict size) with 8 feature size and resolutions ranging from 16 to
512. Following both LeRF and OpenNeRF, we use respectively an OpenCLIP base
(ViT-B/16) and a CLIP large (ViT-L/14). The DINO used for LeRF is a ViT-S/8.
• DiSCO-3D Hyperparameters. Unlike 2D USS methods that cluster DINO features,
which are notoriously sensitive to hyperparameter tuning and prone to failures
on diverse datasets, DiSCO-3D benefits from NeRF’s scene-specificity, making it
more robust. However, while DiSCO-3D has relatively few hyperparameters, certain
parameters still require careful adjustments.
– Number of Prototypes. We showed in subsection 5.3.3 that the chosen number
of relevant prototypes is not crucial as long as there are enough to describe
each sub-concept. Regarding the number of irrelevant prototypes, we use
three irrelevant prototypes in all experiments. However, this is not a sensitive
hyperparameter, only requiring sufficient expressivity to encompass diverse
irrelevant objects.
– Projector. The projector follows the introduced architecture and uses linear
layers which both have as hidden dimension and output dimension the input
dimension. A dropout of probability 0.2 is also applied on it.
– 𝛽 Scheduling. The 𝛽 hyperparameter and its linear scheduling configuration,
which affect the sharpness of the probability distributions, also exhibit minimal impact across scenes as long as we keep a sound configuration. In the
experiments, we use an initial value of 0.5, linearly decreasing to 0.1 over the
training.
– Thresholds. The threshold for ℒ𝑝𝑟𝑜 𝑗 has little impact and is fixed at 0.5, but
ℒ𝑖𝑟𝑟 ’s threshold is more crucial and depends on the feature field. Indeed, OpenNeRF with its OpenSeg encoder generally outputs higher relevancy scores than
101
CHAPTER 5. TASK-DRIVEN DISCOVERY AS A PERCEPTUAL FOCUSING TOOL FOR
THE COGNITIVE CORE
LeRF with CLIP. To accommodate this difference, we use distinct thresholds:
thresholds are set at 0.55 for OpenNeRF and 0.5 for LeRF.
– Loss Weights. We balance the three proposed losses to optimize the trainings
and obtain ℒ = 𝜆 𝑝𝑟𝑜 𝑗 ℒ𝑝𝑟𝑜 𝑗 + 𝜆 𝑖𝑟𝑟 ℒ𝑖𝑟𝑟 + 𝜆 𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑡𝑜 ℒ𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑡𝑜 with 𝜆 𝑝𝑟𝑜 𝑗 = 20, 𝜆 𝑖𝑟𝑟 = 1
and 𝜆 𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑡𝑜 = 0.5.
• Finally, for all experiments, the model is trained for solely 200 epochs with an
EMA decay factor 𝛼 = 0.998 and an Adam optimizer of learning rate exponentially
decreasing from 1𝑒 − 2 to 1𝑒 − 4 across the optimization.
Dataset. We introduce an extension of the Replica [160] dataset for Open-Vocabulary
Sub-concepts Discovery. This extension consists of enriching the annotations of its 8
indoor synthetic scenes with 40 semantic concepts. Each concept represents a specific
grouping of Replica classes (called sub-concepts) and is designed with robotic perception
in mind. To ensure diversity, we use a large language model (LLM) to generate queries
spanning object categories (e.g., "furniture"), properties (e.g., "soft"), and actions (e.g.,
"eat").
Protocol. The following evaluation protocol is designed to be compatible with any future
OV-SD method and follows the standard approach of being performed on a segmented
point cloud, which can typically be derived from most types of 3D representations. It
assesses, for each scene and concept, the segmentation quality of discovered sub-concepts
against the query-related ground-truth sub-concepts. The evaluated method takes as input
the scene 𝑆, the textual query 𝑞 corresponding to a concept, and a collection of 3D points 𝑃.
It should output a set of embedding (e.g. CLIP) representing the discovered sub-concepts
of 𝑞 and should classify each point of 𝑃 with at most one of these embeddings (in DiSCO,
these relate to 𝒫𝐶𝐿𝐼𝑃 associated to each prototype). Segmentation quality is evaluated by
first matching discovered sub-concepts to the dataset-defined sub-concepts (we match
predictions with all of the scene’s classes) using embeddings distances. This enables
comparison with the ground-truth query segmentation to compute classic segmentation
metrics: Mean Accuracy (mAcc) and mean Intersection over Union (mIoU). Since these
metrics do not penalize the presence of unrelated predicted sub-concepts (i.e. false positive
classes not matched to any ground-truth sub-concepts), we also use the standard Panoptic
Quality [79] (PQ) metric, often used for instance segmentation, where we replace the
notion of instances with sub-concepts:
Í

(𝑝,𝑔)∈𝑇𝑃 IoU𝑝 𝑔
|𝑇𝑃|
|

|𝑇𝑃|
|𝑇𝑃| + 0.5|𝐹𝑃| + 0.5|𝐹𝑁|

3D Point Cloud Conversion. For NeRF-based methods such as DiSCO, in order to obtain
3D point cloud predictions, we follow OpenNeRF’s protocol and render the semantic class
distribution image (in DiSCO-3D, we compute 𝐷𝑖 for each pixel 𝑖) for each supervision
viewpoint and back-project it onto the 3D point cloud. Probabilities for each 3D point are
then aggregated across viewpoints, with the final class assigned via an argmax operation.
5.4.2
Open-Vocabulary Sub-concepts Discovery
Evaluated methods. Since no OV-SD baselines exist yet, we design and evaluate two naive
baselines alongside DiSCO. Both of these baselines share the DiSCO-3D architecture and
the input feature fields. The first one begins by performing open-vocabulary segmentation
to identify relevant regions (i.e. regions where the CLIP similarity with the query is above
a fixed threshold) and then does USS on those regions. The second baseline runs USS on
the full scene and then filters out irrelevant classes by thresholding the CLIP similarity of
each USS class to the query (using their CLIP prototypes). Notice that the only difference
between DiSCO-3D and those baselines relies on the fact that DiSCO-3D achieves USS and
OVSeg jointly whereas the latters achieve it successively. We also create two additional
naive baselines by replacing the USS part by a standard K-Means clustering algorithm. All
the methods use the same hyperparameters and especially, we fix the number of prototypes
𝑁 = 10 for all queries (as no concept query exceeds 9 ground-truth sub-concepts).
Results Quantitative results are reported in Table 5.1 (in 𝒫𝐶𝐿𝐼𝑃 columns). We notice
that using DiSCO-3D always overperforms the naive baselines, which demonstrates the
interest of performing jointly USS and OVSeg. More specifically, considering the PQ, mIoU
and mAcc averaged on both LeRF and OpenNeRF, we obtain respectively an increase of
+72%, +71% and +42% against USS→OVS, and +47%, +22% and +44% against OVS→USS.
However, the benchmark is still far from being saturated, showing the difficulty of the
task and the room for future improvements.
103
CHAPTER 5. TASK-DRIVEN DISCOVERY AS A PERCEPTUAL FOCUSING TOOL FOR
THE COGNITIVE CORE
We also display some qualitative examples in Figure 5.3 across various scenes (both
indoor and outdoor from various datasets [76, 99, 160]), feature fields (LeRF and OpenNeRF), types of queries (textual, visual and user clicks) and queries complexity (multiple
queries at once). Although the overall results are strong, some minor inaccuracies (e.g.,
armrests in (e)) or missed ambiguous detections (e.g., workout device at the back in (d))
can still be observed.
Accuracy of CLIP Prototypes. To evaluate the ability of the produced CLIP prototypes
to achieve semantic matching, we evaluate the OV-SD performance by replacing the
prototypes matching by a ground-truth aware matching (using the Hungarian algorithm
between ground-truth and predicted 3D segmentation masks). Results are reported
in Table 5.1 (𝐻𝑢𝑛 𝑔𝑎𝑟𝑖𝑎𝑛 columns).
Considering the PQ, mIoU and mAcc averaged on both LeRF and OpenNeRF, we
observe respectively an increase of 23%, 18% and 42% when the optimal GT-aware matching is used. In practice, we compute that approximately 76% of the matching remains
unchanged, while 24% is reassigned to a new ground-truth sub-concept. As illustrated
in Figure 5.4, this 24% is usually related to sub-concepts with close semantic (e.g. "blanket" and "comforter") or ambiguous annotations (e.g. the armchair is annnotated as
"chair" whereas DiSCO confidence better reflects the ambiguity with the "sofa" and "chair"
sub-concepts). It underlines that many discovered sub-concepts have descriptive CLIP
prototypes which may be sometimes ambiguous due to the nature of OV-SD.
We aslo provide another more complete example in Figure 5.5 where we query the
furniture and give the corresponding probability attributions of the top-10 semantic classes
(amongst the 51) of each sub-concept.
Finally, we observe that the difference of performances between DiSCO and the
baselines is not related to the use of CLIP prototypes. Indeed, averaged on both feature
fields on the GT matching, DiSCO provides an increase of +53%, +30% and +23% against
USS→OVS for PQ, mIoU and mAcc, and +45%, +19% and +49% against OVS→USS. We
also notice that replacing our USS with K-Means in the naive baselines outputs mostly
worse performances, highlighting the interest of our architecture choices.

left and right images are respectively "Sleep" and "Furniture". By comparing each CLIP
prototype to Replica’s semantic classes encoded with CLIP, DiSCO-3D is able to choose
the most relevant class to describe each sub-concept.

Sensitivity to Number of Prototypes and influence of ℒ𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑡𝑜 . Following 2D USS evaluations [93], we study the impact of the predefined number of prototypes on DiSCO’s
performance in Table 5.2. We vary 𝑁 from 𝑁𝐺𝑇 (the number of ground-truth sub-concepts
in a specific scene for a specific query) to 𝑁𝐺𝑇 + 20 and report both segmentation performance and the actual number of prototypes used in practice, with and without ℒ𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑡𝑜 . To
facilitate interpretation, we use ground-truth aware matching.
First, we observe that the complete model’s performance remains stable in both
segmentation accuracy and the number of prototypes used, as long as a sufficient number
of prototypes is available. Small performance drops for 𝑁 = 𝑁𝐺𝑇 and 𝑁 = 𝑁𝐺𝑇 + 2 likely
stem from the risk of missing GT classes, as the limited number of available prototypes
leaves no flexibility for some to remain unused. Adding ℒ𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑡𝑜 effectively regularizes the
number of prototypes used, leading to improved PQ and confirming its role in optimizing
prototype selection. Finally, the last column, corresponding to our main experiment with
a fixed 𝑁 = 10, shows that performance is maintained without requiring prior knowledge
of the number of GT sub-concepts.
Naive Baselines Visualization. In the following, we visualize and comment on the
proposed naive baselines, specifically focusing on the DiSCO-based baselines, K-means
105
CHAPTER 5. TASK-DRIVEN DISCOVERY AS A PERCEPTUAL FOCUSING TOOL FOR
THE COGNITIVE CORE
Figure 5.6: OV-SD Example Naive Baselines vs DiSCO-3D. The query is "light".
ones resulting in similar findings. These naive baselines thus have the same architecture
and configuration as DiSCO-3D but differ fundamentally in their segmentation process.
Instead of jointly performing OV-Seg and USS as in our method, they execute the two
tasks sequentially, each following a specific order.
We refer to these baselines as "naive" because a straightforward approach to solving
OV-SD might be to apply OV-Seg and USS successively. Figure 5.6 provides a visual
comparison using the query "light," which should correspond to the window, the bed-side
lamps and the ceiling lamps.
For the OVSeg-to-USS baseline, segmentation performance is significantly reduced
due to the spatial imprecision of open-vocabulary relevancy. This leads to two key issues:
(1) irrelevant objects may be partially segmented due to relevancy spilling (e.g., a large
part of the wall above the bed), and (2) relevant objects, such as the window, may be
incompletely segmented because the computed relevancy does not fully encompass the
object. In contrast, DiSCO-3D mitigates these issues by leveraging DINO features as input
to the projector, allowing it to refine spatial precision and avoid these relevancy errors.
For the USS-to-OVSeg baseline, while the segmentation aligns better with the scene’s
geometry, the main issue lies in classification. Since USS is performed without query
information, the resulting clusters are not structured according to the query. As a
consequence, after OV-Seg filtering, objects that should be distinguished with respect to
the query remain grouped together based on their overall similarity in the scene, leading
to incorrect decomposition. Here, the ceiling with its lamps are segmented together with
the window. Because the average CLIP embedding answers to the query, this grouping is
considered a sub-concept in this naive baseline.
5.4.3
Open-Vocabulary Segmentation
Protocol. We choose to evaluate OVSeg following two different paradigms. First, we
follow the one used in [158] where we successively evaluate the segmentation performance
of individual queries ("Per-query" paragraph), which is relevant in the embodied AI user
task situation. We also call this paradigm the multi-label paradigm as each 3D point can be
assigned zero or multiple labels based on independent query predictions. In this setup, if
𝑁 is the number of semantic classes in the scene, we train 𝑁 DiSCO models with each 1
query (and relevant prototype). We separate the evaluation in two and evaluate both the
semantic classes present in each Replica scene and the concepts introduced in our extended

We then evaluate OVSeg performance on a simultaneous multi-class paradigm ("PerScene" section). This setup, also called the mono-label paradigm as each point receives a
single label corresponding to the most probable class amongst all queries, translates into
training 1 models with 𝑁𝑞 simultaneous queries, meaning that this paradigm is a way
to evaluate DiSCO’s ability to handle multiple queries at once. Note that because this
paradigm needs the labels to be non-overlapping and needs to cover the whole scene, it
cannot be evaluated on the grouping dataset which has overlapping queries (eg. "furnitures" and "seating" have common sub-concepts). Within other methods and benchmarks,
the multi-label setup is considered more challenging as it requires segmenting each class
independently without relying on other class names as priors.
Evaluated methods.
We evaluate the impact of plugging DiSCO into both LeRF and
OpenNeRF and compare the results with the baselines without the DiSCO module.
Per-query Segmentation Results. We present quantitative outcomes in Table 5.3, first
analyzing results for classes, followed by concepts. Adding DiSCO improves segmentation
performance across both LeRF and OpenNeRF feature fields. For class-level segmentation,
LeRF benefits from a +3.63 increase in mIoU and +3.40 in mAcc, demonstrating that
DiSCO refines segmentation boundaries and mitigates feature noise. OpenNeRF, which
already provides strong segmentation, also sees slight improvements in mIoU and mAcc.
For concept-level segmentation, DiSCO leads to even greater improvements, particularly in
mAcc, with a +8.94 and +15.48 gain for LeRF and OpenNeRF respectively, highlighting its
ability to complete sparse relevancy heatmaps (as vague queries usually result in globally
low relevancy across a scene). Overall, DiSCO mitigates common open-vocabulary segmentation issues by reducing relevancy spilling for highly responsive queries, preventing
over-segmentation, and filling relevancy holes for more complex queries, ensuring a more
complete representation of semantic concepts.
We display some additional qualitative results in Figure 5.7, both with singular queries
(composed of precise classes and concepts) and multiple queries at once.
Per-Scene Segmentation Results. We report quantitative results of this paradigm in
Table 5.4. Regarding LeRF, we notice improvements for every metrics and paradigms when

illustrated in Figure 5.11): it directly improves mIoU and also increases mAcc because
reducing spilling in a paradigm where every point is labeled increases correct classification.
Regarding OpenNeRF, whose segmentation performances are already much better than
LeRF, integrating DiSCO slightly improves the mIoUs (resp. +1.68) at the expense of
mAcc. This trade-off arises because DiSCO segments directly from features rather than
relying on similarity maps like OpenNeRF. As a result, DiSCO provides better boundary
refinement by leveraging additional information but introduces minor misclassification,
particularly for small less frequent classes.
5.4.4
Unsupervised Semantic Segmentation
Protocol. For this experiment, the evaluated methods are requested to segment the scene
into 𝑁 semantic classes. We follow usual USS evaluation pipelines and use Hungarian
matching to link the semantic classes to ground-truth classes. Since Replica scenes contain
different sets of objects, we predict for every method 𝑁 = 10 semantic classes and compare
our results to the top-10 classes of each scene. CLIP prototypes are not used in this setting.
Evaluated methods. Since no NeRF-based unsupervised semantic segmentation (USS)
methods exist for direct comparison with DiSCO-3D, we construct a hand-crafted NeRF
baseline where we extract DINO features per point of the evaluated point cloud using the
feature field and apply K-Means clustering. Additionally, we evaluate two representative
USS methods: the 2D method SmooSeg [86] and the 3D point-cloud method GrowSP [205].
Since SmooSeg only produces 2D segmentations, we recover a 3D segmentation by training
a Semantic-NeRF [207] on its outputs.
Results.
Quantitative results can be found in Table 5.5.

Figure 5.8: Example of USS on real data.
DiSCO-3D achieves the best results across all evaluations. Firstly, 2D USS methods
such as SmooSeg do not assure multi-view consistency meaning that one object seen
from different viewpoints will have different semantic predictions. This impairs the
3D segmentation performances when training the Semantic-NeRF as NeRF predictions
are agnostic to the viewpoint by design. Regarding GrowSP, although it succeeds in
performing accurate segmentation, the global performances are lower, probably due to the
input data modalities, as the discrete nature of point clouds may limit their expressiveness
compared to the continuous representations of NeRF. Finally, K-Means on the feature field
yields slightly lower but comparable results. However, it remains restricted to USS, as it
cannot incorporate open-vocabulary queries for OV-SD. In contrast, DiSCO-3D effectively
handles both OV-SD and its edge cases, OV-Seg and USS, demonstrating its versatility.
Figure 5.8 shows an example on real 2D data (in particular the "Waldo Kitchen" scene
from LeRF). On this latter figure, the "SmooSeg" baseline refers to the 2D method being
trained on the multi-view images without injecting 3D inside the segmentation, while
the "SmooSeg + NeRF" image is a semantic render from a NeRF model trained using the
segmentation maps of the SmooSeg. As explained in subsection 5.4.4, 2D USS methods
such as SmooSeg do not have multi-view consistency. This makes the training of a
Semantic-NeRF hardly consistent, resulting in very noisy segmentations. Although multiview inconsistent, SmooSeg actually performs well when doing per-image segmentation
as illustrated in the figure. While some noise subsist, the results are semantically and
spatially coherent. However, DiSCO-3D still produces better segmentation as it profits
from multi-view information for more precise DINO features, thus better spatial precision
of the segmentation (e.g. the bottles on the top left of the image). Note that no GrowSP
results can be obtained as there is no available point cloud for these hand-captured images
of real data.

5.4.5
Ablative Experiments on the Architecture Choices of DiSCO-3D
In our method, in order to propose a solution for the OV-SD problem adapted to Neural
Fields, we began by proposing a novel USS NeRF-based method as, to the best of our
knowledge, no existing USS method exist for the NeRF representation. In this section, we
perform ablative experiments to evaluate the contributions of the different modules of our
USS branch and show the results in Table 5.6. We evaluate the full method on USS (i.e.
with no user query) and then either modify the projector (we test a simple linear MLP) or
disable separately various components: the linear scheduling of 𝛽 and the two different
ponderations of the prototypes update EMA process.
We note that the selected architecture used in DiSCO-3D indeed presents the best results
amongst the different versions. Each of the other versions outputs diminished results,
ranging from minimal loss of performances when changing the projector to maximal
degradation when foregoing both ponderations in the EMA process.
5.4.6
Discussions on the Input Feature Fields
Choices of Feature Fields. We introduced in subsection 5.3.4 the possibility to use different feature fields, as long as we have a queriable feature field to serve as the query
latent space and a spatially precise one to serve as input to the projector. In the previous
experiments, the query feature space has been tested only with a multi-scale CLIP and
the dense OpenSeg while the input to the projector has been respectively a DINO and the
same OpenSeg feature field.
Regarding the segmentation feature field, there exists a large range of precise image
110
5.4. EXPERIMENTS
Figure 5.10: Garfield Feature Field. We use Garfield (SAM Masks outputs) as the segmentation field and perform USS. Note that Garfield being an instance feature field, it cannot
be used as a replacement for DINO to perform OVSeg and OV-SD. USS also cannot be
entirely considered as semantic segmentation.
Figure 5.11: Relevancy Errors in CLIP Models. By querying "Eggs", the LeRF and OpenNeRF baselines makes different prediction, both regarding the responding objects and
their precision. While DiSCO-3D can "repair" segmentation imprecision via the DINO features, it is dependent of the open-vocabulary expressivity making the OpenNeRF+DiSCO
also segment the background dish even though it does not seem to contain eggs.
encoders that can be injected into a feature field. For instance, Figure 5.9 shows an example of DiSCO-3D applied on a modified LeRF where the DINO is replaced by the image
encoder of SAM (without the decoder). Because SAM is also quite spatially precise (as
shown in the PCA), it can successfully be used to perform any of the 3 proposed tasks
(OV-SD, OVSeg and USS). In Figure 5.10, we display another example of segmentation
feature field by using a Garfield feature field. Garfield is a method producing a multi-scale
feature field using SAM segmentation masks and contrastive learning. However, for better
understanding, we limit here the Garfield feature field to mono-scale segmentation. This
results in extremely precise (but over-segmented) scene decomposition as shown in the
PCA which can be used to perform unsupervised segmentation. However, it is important
to note that SAM produces instance segmentation masks that are unaware of the semantics. Hence, they cannot be used to perform true USS, nor OV-Seg and OV-SD, but rather
instance segmentation. Future works could focus on combining Garfield with previously
introduced semantic fields to perform both semantic and instance segmentation at once.
Regarding the query feature field, we evaluated two adaptations of the open-vocabulary
model CLIP (LeRF and OpenNeRF). However, we could broaden the range of feature
fields used for the query, using other open-vocabulary models for instance or change the
modality of the query with other feature spaces (e.g. image queries with DINO encodings
or user clicks with any feature, as shown in Figure 5.3 with CLIP).
111
CHAPTER 5. TASK-DRIVEN DISCOVERY AS A PERCEPTUAL FOCUSING TOOL FOR
THE COGNITIVE CORE
Figure 5.12: Difficulty of Detecting Concepts with CLIP Models. We query "Art", which
is incorrectly detected in LeRF, resulting in an irrelevant segmentation of parts of the
windows rather than the painting.
Feature Field Quality Dependency. We already mentioned the dependency of our
method to the pre-trained feature fields performances. Since these fields provide input
features for both segmentation and open-vocabulary queries, inaccuracies can negatively
impact the results, as illustrated in Figure 5.11 and Figure 5.12. Regarding the openvocabulary field, errors are common due to the limited quality of 2D open-vocabulary
models and inaccuracies in NeRF’s 3D projection. These errors can lead to unexpected
query results: either an excessive number of objects being labeled as relevant (e.g., the
"Eggs" example in Figure 5.11) or a failure to correctly interpret some queries, especially
when they regard abstract concepts, preventing DiSCO-3D from segmenting the intended
sub-concepts. An example of the latter issue is shown in Figure 5.12, where the query "Art"
fails to recognize the painting while incorrectly identifying seemingly random parts of the
scene. This confusion propagates through the model, leading to incorrect segmentations.
The projector feature field (e.g. DINO) can also suffer some issues which can have an
impact on DiSCO’s performances. Depending on the used encoder, some models like
DINO tend to produce features which describes the scene at object parts-level rather than
object-level. This can lead sometimes to over-segmentation of sub-concepts. Although this
may be useful in certain applications (eg. object decomposition), this phenomenom is not
wanted in OV-SD and this is why we proposed the ℒ𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑡𝑜 to reduce this over-segmentation.
5.5
DiSCO-3D as a perception tool for Large Language Models
Recent works have shown that connecting Large Language Models (LLMs) with callable
tools can enable multimodal agents to reason about 3D scenes. In Chat-With-NeRF [192],
the LLM queries a LeRF representation trained on a given scene to answer questions such
as the presence or location of specific objects. However, LeRF’s low spatial precision and
limited semantic granularity constrain this interaction to simple detection-based questions,
preventing more complex or fine-grained scene understanding.
Replacing LeRF with DiSCO-3D naturally extends this paradigm. DiSCO-3D provides
more accurate semantic segmentation and enables subconcept discovery within a NeRF,
offering a more versatile interface between language reasoning and 3D perception. In
112
5.5. DISCO-3D AS A PERCEPTION TOOL FOR LARGE LANGUAGE MODELS
Figure 5.13: 1st Example of Combining DiSCO-3D with LLMs Red lining highlights
relevant information on the screen based on the chosen question. (a) showcases the scene
with a list of available questions in the demonstration app. (b) displays the answer to
the query, (c) the action plan created by the LLM to solve the task and (d) the NeRF
reconstruction with the relevant segmentation.
collaboration with Gerald Bianchi, a lab colleague, we implemented this approach in a
demonstration software system where an LLM could directly query DiSCO-3D for scene
understanding.
Two versions of this implementation were developed. In the first, we used DiSCO-3D
on NeRF representations following our original method. In the second, we adapted
the framework to point-cloud inputs, thus enabling the processing of scenes captured
only from LiDAR, exporting features from DINO and CLIP encoders to form a featureenriched point representation. We also augmented both implementations with the ability
to separate each class or sub-concept into instances. In order to do that, we applied the
HDBScan clustering algorithm [117] to the 3D coordinates of each segmented sample
(either a NeRF sample or a point), allowing spatially distinct instances to be separated
within each semantic class. The resulting modules, namely open-vocabulary segmentation,
subconcept discovery, instance decomposition, and object localization, were wrapped as
callable tools within the LangChain framework and accessed by a Mistral LeChat LLM.
This setup significantly expands the range of questions the agent can answer. For
example, the model can now respond to queries such as “How many blue chairs are there?”
(counting objects from their properties), “Display and separate all metallic objects” (subconcept discovery and instance segmentation), or “Locate all tools in the workshop” (object
113
CHAPTER 5. TASK-DRIVEN DISCOVERY AS A PERCEPTUAL FOCUSING TOOL FOR
THE COGNITIVE CORE
Figure 5.14: 2nd Example of Combining DiSCO-3D with LLMs Red lining highlights
relevant information on the screen based on the chosen question. (a) showcases the scene
with a list of available questions in the demonstration app. (b) displays the answer to
the query, (c) the action plan created by the LLM to solve the task and (d) the NeRF
reconstruction with the relevant segmentation.
localization). Beyond improved accuracy, DiSCO-3D’s ability to reason over subconcepts
and spatial instances enables richer and more grounded interactions with 3D scenes than
LeRF.
In Figure 5.13 and Figure 5.14, we display some screenshots of the demonstration
application, spanning 2 questions across two different scenes, and using the point cloud
adaptation implementation. In Figure 5.13, we showcase object localization via a simple
example, while in Figure 5.14, the more complex query of counting the plates on the
dining table requires a more complex course of actions, first detecting the table and plates,
and then counting only the plates that are close to the table. The LLM successfully planned
and performed the thought process, notably discarding the fourth plate which is not on
the dining table.
5.6
Conclusion
In this chapter, we introduced the problem of 3D Open-Vocabulary Sub-Concept Discovery and presented DiSCO-3D, a solution tailored to Neural Field representations. By
combining a novel Unsupervised Semantic Segmentation module with open-vocabulary
guidance, our approach allows for the flexible decomposition of scenes into granular,
114
5.6. CONCLUSION
meaningful concepts and holds significant potential for extension to other representations
such as 3D Gaussian Splatting, as partially explored with point clouds. DiSCO-3D is
also especially useful for embodied AI applications, as the agent can use it as a powerful
perception tool to answer user queries.
However, a critical limitation remains: in this configuration, the agent’s Cognitive Core
lacks global awareness of the scene before invoking the tool. It must currently infer what
to query purely from the user’s textual prompt, which is often sub-optimal. This limitation
motivates the integration explored in the next chapter, where we position DiSCO-3D as
a specialized local perception tool within a broader reasoning framework that possesses
inherent global scene understanding. This progression paves the way toward agents
capable of combining holistic reasoning with localized perception for fully embodied task
execution.
115
6
Interfacing NeRF and Large Language
Models for Holistic Scene Understanding
While the previous chapter addressed localized, user-driven discovery, a complete agent’s Cognitive
Core must also possess global situational awareness to plan complex tasks. Developing such multimodal reasoning for 3D environments is notoriously challenging due to the scarcity of 3D training data
compared to the abundance of 2D resources. This chapter introduces LLaVA3 (LLaVA-Cube), a novel
framework that bridges the gap between NeRF 3D scene representations and large Vision-Language
Models (VLMs) without requiring any fine-tuning. Inspired by Cubist principles, we propose a
method to "translate" the 3D Neural Field into a structured format the VLM can understand: by
decomposing the scene into objects and generating omni-directional visual descriptions for each, we
enable the cognitive core to reason about spatial relationships, affordances, and global context directly
from the reconstructed map. Extensive experiments demonstrate that this approach outperforms
existing baselines on 3D Visual Question Answering and Language Grounding, completing the
agent’s perceptual stack.
Project page can be found at : https://cea-list.github.io/LLaVA-Cube/
6.1
Motivation and Context
In an embodied AI system, the agent must possess a cognitive core capable of understanding user tasks, planning its actions, and reacting to unexpected events. In the previous
chapter, we argued that such a core requires perceptual tools allowing it to focus on
specific regions or objects for fine-grained understanding. We introduced DiSCO-3D as
an example of such a focusing tool: it provides open-vocabulary access to elements of
the scene and allows to semantically clusterize them into sub-concepts, helping the agent
retrieve precise information relevant to its current objective.
However, while this local focus is essential, it is not sufficient. A capable embodied
agent also needs an holistic understanding of its environment. The cognitive core must be
able to use this global context to ground user queries, formulate high-level strategies, and
decide when and how to invoke specialized local tools. In other words, the agent must
perceive both the "trees" and the "forest."
116
6.1. MOTIVATION AND CONTEXT
Enabling this global, high-level reasoning is the central goal of 3D scene understanding,
with broad applications in areas such as robotics [127, 125] and autonomous navigation [53].
This field covers a wide range of downstream tasks, from spatial reasoning and object
decomposition to dense captioning and segmentation, covering both textual and pixel-level
outputs, as illustrated in Figure 6.1.
Recent Vision-Language Models (VLMs) such as LLaVA [103, 203] have demonstrated
impressive capabilities in interpreting 2D images through multi-modal understanding
and autoregressive language generation, enabling tasks like Visual Question Answering
(VQA). Motivated by their success, multiple works have explored extending these models
to 3D scene understanding.
Two main families of approaches have emerged. The first, 3D Large Multi-modal
Models (3D LMMs), are trained to directly process raw 3D data like point clouds. The
second extends existing 2D Vision Language Models (VLMs) to interpret multiple 2D
images of the same scene. Yet, despite encouraging results, 3D performance still falls short
of the rich reasoning capabilities achieved in 2D. For 3D MLLMs, this gap is often due
to the scarcity of high-quality 3D training data. For 2D VLMs, the primary challenge is
the inherent complexity of 3D reasoning, which requires integrating information across
disparate viewpoints, spatial scales, and occlusions.
This chapter proposes a new direction to bridge this gap. Instead of training a new
3D model, we leverage a pre-trained VLM (specifically LLaVA) which we connect to our
NeRF scene representation by constructing a 2D visual description that faithfully reflects
the 3D structure of the scene while remaining compatible with the frozen architecture of
existing VLM models.
Inspired by works from ChatSplat [21] and SplatTalk [167], our approach first augments
our NeRF reconstruction with a 3D feature field of high-dimensional LLaVA visual tokens.
This 3D field is then used to provide 2D visual descriptions of the scene. However,
we observe that those initial approaches sample tokens across the scene in a spatially
unstructured manner, leading to redundant and inconsistent representations, ultimately
limiting the VLM’s reasoning ability.
To address this, we introduce LLaVA³, an object-centric, structured scene description
inspired by the principles of Cubist art, which deconstructs 3D objects into collections of 2D
projections. From our NeRF representation, we first perform a hierarchical segmentation
of the reconstruction, yielding a discrete set of objects. For each object, we then compute
an omni-directional visual description by sampling from the 3D LLaVA token field. These
per-object token sets are semantically rich, spatially diverse, and context-aware, and can be
fed as a composite image to a frozen LLaVA model. Finally, objects are ordered according
to their spatial layout, providing a consistent and interpretable input for downstream
reasoning.
Our evaluations demonstrate that LLaVA3 outperforms other VLM-based solutions
on 3D Visual Question Understanding (3D VQA) and 3D grounding while allowing many
3D downstream tasks (Figure 6.1) without requiring any VLM fine-tuning.
117
CHAPTER 6. INTERFACING NERF AND LARGE LANGUAGE MODELS FOR HOLISTIC
SCENE UNDERSTANDING
Figure 6.1: LLaVA3 empowers 3D understanding ability of Vision Language Models
(VLM) through a new paradigm of 2D visual representations of 3D scenes. Our representation relies on an object-centric description of the scene, each object being visually
described from a multitude of viewpoints jointly. Reconstructed from multi-view images,
this representation permits VLMs to achieve various tasks such as 3D Visual Question
Answering, 3D Grounding or 3D Semantic Segmentation.
To summarize, our contributions in this chapter are:
1. We introduce the new concept of representing a 3D NeRF scene for a VLM as a
collection of omni-directional visual descriptions of each of the 3D objects it contains.
2. We design the first object hierarchy decomposition of a 3D Neural Field scene,
facilitating interpretation due to its discrete nature.
3. We propose to model jointly view-independent and view-dependent information
contained in the high-dimension LLaVA visual tokens to better capture both object
semantics and their spatial relationships.
4. We demonstrate that LLaVA³ outperforms other VLM-based solutions in terms of
3D VQA and 3D grounding.
6.1.1
Related Works
Large Multi-modal Models for 3D Scene Understanding. Large Language Models (LLMs)
such as GPT-4 [1], Claude [4], and LLaMA [169] have established powerful foundations
for natural language understanding, leading to the emergence of Large Multi-modal Models (LMMs) that extend language capabilities to visual domains. LLaVA [103] (and its
118
6.2. METHOD
subsequent variants [101, 102, 89]) represents a seminal work in this direction, demonstrating that LLMs can understand and reason about visual content through a simple vision
encoder-language model architecture connected by a projection layer.
Approaches to extends VLM abilities to 3D scene understanding can be grouped
into several categories. First, 2D VLMs already exhibit impressive multi-view reasoning
capabilities, often achieving surprisingly accurate 3D scene understanding from 2D inputs.
However, these methods face key limitations: the restricted context window of language
models and the mismatch between 2D image inputs and the inherently 3D nature of scenes.
While recent works have sought to extend these architectures to 3D point clouds [61, 54,
189], adapting VLMs to process point clouds remains difficult as point clouds are less
informative than multi-view images and are harder to collect at scale, particularly for
large-scale model training.
A growing class of hybrid methods [21, 167] addresses these issues by reconstructing
the 3D scene from multi-view images using NeRF or Gaussian Splatting, and augmenting
the reconstruction with a LLaVA feature field. The scene is then summarized for the
VLM via global sampling of the LLaVA field. This approach combines strengths of both
paradigms: it leverages 3D reconstruction for dense spatial coverage while avoiding the
need to train or fine-tune the VLM. Our method falls within this category, but we argue
that existing sampling strategies, which usually consist in unstructured sampling across
the scene, are sub-optimal. Instead, we decompose the scene into objects and construct
per-object feature sets tailored for VLM consumption.
Feature Fields. As stated before, recent works have distilled pretrained image encoders
into 3D feature fields to enable open-vocabulary understanding and semantic segmentation. Methods like LeRF [76], OpenNeRF [40] or Decomposing-NeRF [82] ground
open-vocabulary embeddings (respectively CLIP [142], OpenSeg [50] and LSeg [90]) inside NeRF models, allowing textual querying across the scene. Other approaches leverage
the Segment Anything Model (SAM) [80]: SAM-NeRF projects SAM features into the
scene for easy novel view segmentation, while Garfield [78] rather proposes a continuous
multi-scale scene decomposition by distilling masks using contrastive learning.
Inspired by these ideas, LLaVA3 learns two complementary 3D feature fields. The first
one is aligned with LLaVA for semantic reasoning. Unlike those previous works, it reconstructs the view-dependency of the LLaVA token to better capture the spatial relationships
among the scene elements. The second field is aligned with SAM masks and CLIP features
for scene decomposition. Unlike Garfield, our decomposition provides a discrete objects
hierarchy that is easier to exploit than continuous volume-based decomposition.
6.2
Method
We propose to improve the VLM ability to interpret 3D scenes from our NeRF representation without any VLM fine-tuning. Instead of directly providing the visual-description of

Figure 6.2: Overview of LLaVA3 . We first reconstruct the 3D scene as a NeRF from
multi-view images with an associated LLaVA feature field. We also derive a hierarchical
3D segmentation of our NeRF. For each object, we create an omni-directional visualdescription as a set of tokens. After object re-ordering, we can finally feed them to the
