the captured 2D views of the scene to the VLM, our solution provides an omni-directional
visual-description for each object of the scene. As illustrated in Figure 6.2, our process
uses a 3D grid-based NeRF reconstruction of the scene which we augment with a LLaVA
feature field (section 6.2.1). Using a SAM-based feature-field, the reconstruction is then hierarchically decomposed into objects/parts/sub-parts, resulting into an explicit 3D object
decomposition graph (section 6.2.2). An omnidirectional 2D visual-description of each
object is computed by sampling tokens from the LLaVA field equally amongst the object
sub-components. This visual description is also designed to capture the object semantics
while preserving the maximum of contextual information related to its relationships with
the rest of the environment. Those objects’ omnidirectional visual-descriptions are then
provided as image tokens to the VLM, following an order depending on the object position
in the 3D scene (section 6.2.3).
6.2.1
Reconstructing a LLaVA Feature Field
Our first objective is to reconstruct a dense LLaVa visual feature field of the scene. We
choose to use LLaVA-OV [89], which integrates a SigLip [202] image encoder Φsiglip with
an LLM 𝒢𝐿𝐿𝑀 , connected via an MLP projector 𝑝 𝜃 that maps visual feature maps into the
language embedding space. In the usual 2D setup, the projected visual tokens are flatten
and concatenated with textual tokens, and jointly processed by the language model to
produce textual outputs.
Following standard feature field practice, we extract LLaVA token maps from training
images and distill them into our NeRF representation. Despite LLaVA’s low-resolution feature maps (27 × 27), NeRF’s multi-view consistency enables recovery of higher-resolution
features, as illustrated in 2.9 and explained in LeRF [76]. Based on ChatSplat [21] and
SplatTalk [167] insights, we supervise the field with post-projector features (𝑝 𝜃 ◦ Φsiglip )
rather than SigLIP Φsiglip features. This choice stems from the projector’s high sensitivity
120
6.2. METHOD
to reconstruction noise in SigLIP features: using 𝑝 𝜃 on SigLIP features sampled from a feature field results in tokens unintelligible by the VLM. On the other hand, supervising with
post-projector outputs offers more stability by operating directly in the VLM’s token space.
Reconstruction of high-dimensional feature field. LLaVA tokens pose a unique challenge: their high dimensionality (3584D), sparsity, and lack of normalization make them
difficult to learn reliably within a compact feature field without losing their semantic integrity. SplatTalk, based on Gaussian Splatting, addresses this by training a scene-specific
auto-encoder that compresses tokens into a lower-dimensional, normalized space to stabilize training. This step is necessary in Gaussian Splatting, where high-dimensional tokens
cannot be directly stored or rendered due to memory limitations [141, 158]. However, it
introduces information loss through two stages of imperfect compression (first via the
auto-encoder, then during field learning) and adds additional pre-training overhead. In
contrast, because we operate within a NeRF representation, we are not bound by these
limitations and can learn full-resolution LLaVA features end-to-end using an auto-decoder
architecture, preserving their full expressivity. Specifically, the feature field is decoded
into normalized lower-dim feature 𝑓 before being mapped to the full token dimensionality
𝑡, as illustrated in Figure 6.3.
Reconstructing semantics and spatial relationships. Visuo-language features, such as
CLIP, SigLIP or LLaVA features, encode both the semantics of individual objects and their
spatial relationships. However, because each viewpoint offers only a partial observation of
the scene, the spatial information captured in these features is inherently view-dependent,
while the object-level semantics remain view-independent.
To our knowledge, existing multi-view 3D reconstruction methods for feature fields
always rely on view-independent modeling (i.e. assigning a single static embedding to
each 3D point, regardless of the viewpoint). This approach effectively captures the object’s
semantics while averaging out viewpoint-specific spatial relationships. Such filtering
is well-suited for tasks like semantic segmentation, where object identity alone suffices.
However, this becomes questionable in the context of vision-language models (VLMs)
used for 3D scene analysis, especially regarding embodied AI applications. In such cases,
averaging view-dependent features can erase critical view-specific cues like inter-object
relationships that are visible from very few input images.
On the other hand, view-independent features benefit from multi-view aggregation,
making them better at preserving inherent object semantics.
To jointly capture both semantics and spatial relationships, we jointly model in 𝑓 two
complementary feature fields: one view-invariant 𝑓𝑉 𝐼 and one view-dependent 𝑓𝑉 𝐷 , the
latter being modeled as a deviation 𝛿𝑉 𝐷 of 𝑓𝑉 𝐼 to ensure consistency between these two:
121
CHAPTER 6. INTERFACING NERF AND LARGE LANGUAGE MODELS FOR HOLISTIC
SCENE UNDERSTANDING
Figure 6.3: Architecture of the Feature Fields. 𝑁 stands for "Normalization".
𝑓𝑉 𝐷 (x, d) = 𝑓𝑉 𝐼 (x) + 𝛿𝑉 𝐷 (x, d)
with x the sample position in the scene and d the ray’s direction of observation. For
each training sample, the feature 𝑓 is first decoded into 𝑓𝑉 𝐷 and 𝑓𝑉 𝐼 (each one having
its own decoder), which are then decoded into full LLaVA tokens 𝑡𝑉 𝐷 and 𝑡𝑉 𝐼 using
the same shared decoder and supervised independently with an MSE against the same
ground-truth tokens (see Figure 6.3).
6.2.2
Decomposing a NeRF into Objects Hierarchy
To create an object-centric description of the scene, we first seek to decompose it into
individual objects and further propose a hierarchical structure for this decomposition.
Hierarchical NeRF Feature Field.
We draw inspiration from Garfield [78], which lever-
ages SAM [80] masks across views to model implicitly a consistent 3D instance segmentation via a contrastive segment embedding field. In this setting, embeddings associated
with rays falling within the same 2D mask are pulled together, while those from different
masks are pushed apart, encouraging cross-view consistency.
However, unlike Garfield’s continuous spatial scale-space, which encodes scale in
terms of spatial volume, we aim to construct a semantic scale-space that reflects discrete
hierarchical levels: objects, parts, and sub-parts. This discrete structure aligns better with
the goal of structured scene understanding and enables a clear representation of hierarchy.
We perform this by replacing Garfield’s single scale-conditioned decoder with three
separate decoders, all connected to a single set of feature grids and each dedicated to
one level of the hierarchy. The training process is adapted accordingly: using the SAM
discretization trick introduced in LangSplat [141], we separate the 2D SAM masks into
122
6.2. METHOD
three semantic levels (object, part, subpart). Each set of masks supervises the training of
the corresponding decoder and feature field, enforcing a level-specific representation of
the scene.
Finally, to both enhance the scene decomposition and enable additional scene understanding downstream tasks (Section 6.3.5), we further augment this field with a CLIP
output. For each scale, an additional decoder predicts CLIP features from intermediate
segment embeddings, trained using an MSE loss against CLIP embeddings computed
from rendered SAM masks. The architecture used for this SAM-CLIP feature field is
also provided in Figure 6.3, though its design choices are relatively straightforward and
primarily guided by implementation simplicity.
3D Objects Hierarchy Extraction. Now that we have learned a three-level hierarchical
feature field, the next step is to construct a true hierarchical scene graph, that is, a tree
structure in which any point sampled from the NeRF can be associated with a specific
node in the objects hierarchy.
First, to derive full-scene segmentation from our hierarchical feature field, we cluster
segment embbedings of a batch of randomly sampled rays using the clustering algorithm
HDBScan [117] independently at each semantic scale. To further encourage a scale-specific
segmentation, we vary HDBScan’s parameters per-scale (see Section 6.3.1 for hyperparameters values). Cluster centroids are computed as confidence-weighted embedding
averages, such that any 3D point is assigned to its nearest centroid at each level.
However, this approach is not guaranteed to produce clean hierarchical segmentation,
whether in terms of hierarchical misclassifications or reconstruction-based artifacts.
To improve consistency, we introduce a three-fold refinement step that leverages CLIP
features and the multi-scale hierarchy. For each HDBScan’s segment, we compute a CLIP
centroid and apply the following heuristics across scales: (i) discard noisy segments with
high intra-cluster variance or low cardinal; (ii) split under-segmented regions when coarse
clusters contain sub-segments with very dissimilar CLIP features, using the finer scale
segmentation to compute new centroids; and (iii) merge over-segmented ones if feature
centroids (either CLIP or SAM) are nearly identical.
To construct a hierarchical structure from our NeRF-based segmentation, we analyze
the relationships between segments in a bottom-up manner. For each finer-level segment,
we determine its parent by identifying the coarser-level segment it most frequently cooccurs with, using the HDBScan’s rays clustering statistics. This process ensures a
consistent and well-structured hierarchy in which each segment is uniquely assigned to a
parent, resulting in a coherent and comprehensive decomposition of the scene.
Independence of LLaVA and SAM Feature Fields. An important but non-mentioned
choice of our method is the complete independence between our two feature fields. LLaVA
field is learnt from encoded feature maps from the whole images and does not use the
segmentation field in any way and vice-versa. However, it would have been possible to
123
CHAPTER 6. INTERFACING NERF AND LARGE LANGUAGE MODELS FOR HOLISTIC
SCENE UNDERSTANDING
interlace both training processes to maybe improve the performances. One main idea
would be to follow our implementation of CLIP per-mask encoding in the LLaVA field
and compute a per-mask LLaVA feature map for LLaVA supervision. However, we chose
not to pursue this direction for the following reasons:
• Computational Intractability. Although the precise number varies, SAM model
easily produces more than 100 mask per image across all scales. Computing and
storing feature maps of dimension 400 × 100 × 27 × 27 × 3584 (≈ 105 ∗ 109 ) obviously
results in impossibly long processing time and most importantly > 100Gb per-scene
checkpoint, which is not really realistic for real-life applications.
• Loss of Contextual Information. Mask-level supervision for LLaVA features would
eliminate spatial context by encoding each object in isolation. This absence of
surrounding information would severely hinder the model’s capacity for spatial
reasoning and scene-level understanding.
• Modularity and Flexibility. Maintaining separate feature fields enables modular
use of each component depending on the target application. For instance, tasks that
do not require a language model (e.g., semantic or instance segmentation) can be
performed solely using the segmentation field, reducing both memory footprint and
training complexity.
Optimal Frame Selection. In a robotic context, video sequences of large-scale environments typically contain significant frame redundancy, making it computationally
expensive (and often unnecessary) to encode every image with LLaVA and SAM. To
balance efficiency and feature diversity when we have access to all images at once, we
sub-sample the training images used for learning the two feature fields (while still using
all frames for geometry). Rather than sampling uniformly, we apply a greedy selection
strategy that maximizes coverage in 3D camera space. At each step, we select the frame
most dissimilar from the current set, based on camera position and orientation. This
ensures that the features are distilled from a wide range of viewpoints, which is critical for
building a complete and robust 3D feature field that is not biased towards any single perspective. This process leads to a diverse and efficient subset of frames for distillation. The
impact of this strategy, which mimics a keyframe selection process in SLAM, is evaluated
in Section 6.3.3.
6.2.3
Object-centric Description of the scene
With the scene segmented into hierarchical objects and the LLaVA feature field distilled,
we now construct a VLM-compatible object-centric description of the scene.
124
6.2. METHOD
Omnidirectional Visual-Description of a 3D Object. For each object 𝑖, we aim to extract
𝑁 𝑖𝑓 features that jointly describe its semantics and spatial relationships. To do so, we
randomly cast rays through the supervision images and assign each resulting LLaVA
feature to its corresponding object segment. This process continues until each object
accumulates 𝑁 𝑖𝑓 features. However, to construct an effective object representation, several
questions must be addressed regarding feature selection, distribution, and quantity.
First, how can we ensure spatially balanced feature coverage across the whole object?
To address this, features are allocated regarding the objects hierarchy: first equally across
part regions, then across sub-parts. This guarantees a uniform distribution over the
components of the objects. Since the VLM processing is permutation-invariant with
respect to the features within a single object, their internal ordering does not affect
downstream tasks.
A second key question concerns the type of features that should be selected: should
they be purely view-independent (VI), purely view-dependent (VD), or a mixture of both?
VI features are better suited for object general semantics, while VD features excel at
capturing spatial relationships that are inherently viewpoint-sensitive.
We thus propose to distribute samples equally between VI and VD features. However,
in tasks that involve spatial relationships from an observer’s perspective (e.g. VQA), where
references to "left" or "right" are common, we adopt an alternative strategy. We define a
canonical viewing direction for each object and extract VD features only from rays that fall
within a specified angular threshold of this direction, VI features being extracted otherwise.
For simplicity, the canonical direction is defined as the most frequently observed viewing
direction of the object, computed via spherical binning over all ray directions used to
observe it.
Finally, how many features should be allocated per object ? We fix the number of
features per object to 𝑁 𝑓 = 𝑊/𝑂, where 𝑊 is the maximal VLM context window and 𝑂
is the number of objects. This strategy ensures uniform object representation. Ablations
in section 6.3.3 confirm that this design leads to improved performance over variable-size
allocations (depending on the object complexity for instance).
Object-centric Scene Description. The final step consists in aggregating those objects
visual descriptions into a coherent scene-level representation, suitable for VLM-based
reasoning. This process also raises important design questions, particularly around how
to structure the sequence of object features in the prompt, given the constraints of using a
frozen 2D VLM.
Unlike 3D MLLM approaches that introduce positional encodings that require LLM
fine-tuning, we explore how the arrangement of objects in the prompt affects how the
VLM understands spatial relations across them. To impose a consistent and layout-aware
order, we adopt a radar-inspired sorting strategy. We first compute the 3D centroid of
each object, then simulate a sweep originating from the scene center, sorting objects by
their polar angle around the vertical axis. To resolve ties between objects with similar
125
CHAPTER 6. INTERFACING NERF AND LARGE LANGUAGE MODELS FOR HOLISTIC
SCENE UNDERSTANDING
angles, we use a secondary term based on their radial distance from the center, with lower
weight. This results in a deterministic and geometry-informed object ordering.
Finally, scene description is provided to the VLM as multi-view images, each object
being tagged as a virtual image with a unique ID. This allows us to support grounding
tasks: when prompted, the VLM outputs the most relevant ID for a given query, which
can be mapped back to the corresponding segment in the NeRF reconstruction.
6.3
Experiments
6.3.1
Implementation Details and Hyperparameters
Our method is composed of several key components, each governed by its own set of
hyperparameters. However, it is important to emphasize that these hyperparameters are
not critical to the overall functionality of the approach. They primarily serve to balance
trade-offs between performance, memory consumption, and computation time. The
core behavior and effectiveness of the method remain consistent across a broad range of
reasonable values. Below, we provide the specific hyperparameter settings used in the
following experiments.
• ScanNet Reconstruction. ScanNet data often includes frames affected by motion blur,
which can degrade the quality of 3D reconstruction and downstream processing.
To mitigate this, we apply a Laplacian variance filter to quantify sharpness and
discard the 20% most blurred images in each scene. Additionally, the undistortion
step in the ScanNet preprocessing pipeline introduces black border artifacts. To
address this, we crop 20 pixels from each edge of every image, ensuring clean
inputs to the reconstruction process. Regarding the feature fields optimization, each
NeRF trained on ScanNet uses 400 images selected using the previously described
algorithm. As for the radiance field itself, we do not require fixed hyperparameters
beyond ensuring a visually and geometrically accurate reconstruction. Note that
we use both camera pose optimization and per-image appearance embeddings to
enhance rendering fidelity.
• Feature Field Reconstruction. We follow the previously described architecture and
have two different feature fields with different hyperparameter sets each. Regarding
the segmentation field, we use standard parameters, with 16 levels of grids with
resolutions ranging from 16 to 2048. The constrastive SAM feature size (outputted
from the SAM MLPs) is of 64 for each scale, which is smaller than what’s used
in Garfield, as the use of three discrete levels requires less complex features than
the continuous scale groupings, but most feature sizes are usable. Because, the
LLaVA feature field needs to store complex features, we take inspiration from LeRF
configurations and use 32 layers of hashgrids with resolutions from 16 to 512. For
both fields, each grid has a feature size of 8.
126
6.3. EXPERIMENTS
Figure 6.4: Qualitative performance of our method on 3D VQA and Grounding. By
decomposing into objects the view-dependent features, our method avoids several very
common issues in 3D VQA: (a) missing objects due to insufficient sampling, (b) weak interobject spatial relationships, (c) loss of objects details and (d) bad multi-view understanding
(i.e. relations between objects from different images). Our method can also perform
grounding from different types of queries ((e),(f),(g),(h)).
• Segmentation. We segment the scene by applying the HDBScan algorithm on each
individual scale. We draw 3 ∗ 8192 rays and use them for all three HDBScan with
each different hyperparameters to encourage coarser clusters as the scale increases.
Specifically, for the small, medium, and large scales, we set empirically the minimum
cluster size to 10, 30, and 50 respectively; the minimum samples to 3, 5, and 10; and
the cluster selection epsilon to 0.01, 0.05, and 0.1.
• Filtering and Feature Extraction. Our filtering hyperparameters have been tuned
empirically from a few test scenes and then fixed for every experiments. Regarding
the noisy classes suppression, we set a minimum of 10 samples per class and an
instance and CLIP variances percentiles of 85%. The over-segmentation merging
and under-segmentation splitting uses respectively similarity thresholds of 0.85 and
0.75. Regarding the feature extraction, we extract a total of 30000 rays distributed
amongst the objects, which is slightly less than the maximum context window size.
The following ablative experiment demonstrate the increase of performance.
• LLM and Image Encoders. We use in all our experiments siglip-so400m-patch14-384 as
LLaVA’s visual encoder and qwen2-7b as LLaVA’s LLM, from LLaVA-OV pre-trained
models. Regarding the SAM-CLIP feature field, we use the SAM from LangSplat
codebase (i.e. ViT-H) and CLIP ViT-H-14 as used in ConceptGraph and HOV-SG
experimentations.
All our experiments (NeRF training and LLM inference) were run on one A100 GPU.

CHAPTER 6. INTERFACING NERF AND LARGE LANGUAGE MODELS FOR HOLISTIC
SCENE UNDERSTANDING
Figure 6.5: Further Visual Question Answering Examples.
6.3.2
3D Visual Question Answering (3D VQA)
Datasets and Protocol. We use ScanNet-based datasets for evaluation, specifically the
ScanQA validation set and the MSR3D test set. For ScanQA, we evaluate performance using
standard n-gram-based metrics: CIDEr, METEOR, ROUGE, EM@1, and EM@1-Refined.
For MSR3D, we adopt their GPT-based correctness score and follow their implementation,
using GPT-4o as the notation model.
Qualitative Results. Figures 6.4, 6.5, 6.6 and 6.7 showcase various example of VQA with
respectively, different common errors solved by our method for the first two, open-ended
questions and object-centric VQA (i.e., feeding tokens of specific objects to the VLM rather
than the whole scene). Those examples span both real (ScanNet and one scene from LeRF)
and synthetic (Replica) scenes.
They illustrate the capacity of our method to reason about spatial relationships, object
existence, counting, and other complex scene-level concepts. Importantly, since our
approach does not rely on large-scale training specifically on ScanNet, it demonstrates
strong generalization capabilities to novel environments.
Results on ScanQA. Similarly to SplatTalk evaluation, we compare our methods against
several families of baselines on the ScanQA validation set and we report results of each in
Table 6.1. "3D LMMs" lists several 3D large multi-modal models trained across a diverse
range of tasks. However, some of those models use ScanQA train set, either during their
training or their fine-tuning. Please note that all those models uses ScanNet and other
indoor scenes as training dataset, thus limiting the generalization to other types of scenes
and inducing a boosting bias in their performance evaluations. "2D VLMs" refers to
models that only process multi-view images inputs. The object-based LLaVA-OV baseline
is constructed by applying SAM to multi-view images and extracting LLaVA features

multi-view images of the scene. Alongside SplatTalk (generalist and LoRA variant) and
our method, we introduce a Ray-NeRF baseline that samples LLaVA visual tokens from
random rays across the NeRF field, without object structuring. This baseline isolates the
benefit of object-centric feature extraction by contrasting it with an unstructured use of
the same NeRF representation, separating the value of object-based reasoning from that
of the 3D NeRF alone.
Most importantly, our base method outperforms all zero-shot NeRF and Gaussian
Splatting models and 2D VLMs. This demonstrates that we successfully derive a more
comprehensive and efficient scene representation for a VLM than traditional multi-view
input and random ray sampling. Specifically, both NeRF methods (especially ours) achieve
higher performance than LLaVA-OV although we use the same LLM and input feature
maps, meaning that although using a NeRF to ensure the use of visual tokens across
the whole scene helps (+5.18 CIDEr), adding the object-centric representation of LLaVA3
benefits significantly more (+22.31 CIDEr). However, the 2D object-based baseline (−3.60
against LLaVA-OV) demonstrates that this object-centric decomposition is beneficial only
for 3D representation.
When comparing to other paradigms, we notice better or comparable results to most
3D LMMs (except for LEO, our metrics range between −4.6% and +13.2% of the other
baselines with an average of +2% across all metrics), despite not using any 3D-specific
information, nor training our VLM on ScanNet-based data.
Results on MSR3D. To evaluate the impact of our different contributions, we showcase
quantitative results in Table 6.2 on the MSR3D dataset, which decomposes into multiple
question types, allowing fine-grained understanding of what brings each contribution. In
addition to the LLaVA-2D baselines, SplatTalk and LEO, we progressively build LLaVA3
on the Ray-NeRF baseline by adding our contributions one by one to demonstrate their
individual impact.
First, we analyze our contributions. Decomposing the scene into objects has the highest
effect on overall performance (+6.85), as it ensures a balanced distribution of the sampling
across the scene. More in-depth balancing of the features via hierarchicalsampling further
increases the results (+2.5); especially for questions related to visual appearance (e.g.
existence and attributes with resp. +3.04 and +3.08), as it gives the model more exhaustive
information on each object. As expected, improving the scene decomposition quality
by adding filtering steps slightly improves the results globally. We can also note that
ordering the scene description via our radar sweeping strategy also noticeably helps the
VLM understands the scene better. The increase is mostly global, resulting in a 2.09 score
bump.
Regarding the view dependency, although replacing VI features with VD features
results in similar overall performance (but still different detailed distribution), combining
both features in an even split improves the performance. It appears that VI features help
the model to reason over descriptive queries (counting, existence, attributes), while the
130
6.3. EXPERIMENTS
Figure 6.6: Example of Object-centric VQA and sub-scale grounding.
Figure 6.7: Open-Ended Visual Question Answering Examples.
VD features help with spatial and navigation questions. Finally, the adaptive VI-VD used
in LLaVA3 is better than splitting evenly the features, as choosing one canonical view
direction helps follow the observer’s perspective.
Our full model outperforms most comparative baselines across the majority of categories. LEO, which is specifically trained for ScanQA-style queries, struggles to generalize
to this dataset. The 2D baselines show overall lower performance, largely due to the small
number of input images. Similarly, the other hybrid methods such as SplatTalk and our
Ray-NeRF baseline under-perform on most categories, with respective drops of −3.09 and
−14.35 points.
6.3.3
Additional Ablative Experiments on 3D VQA
In the following, we present additional ablation experiments to further assess the impact
of various components and design choices in our method. Unlike the previous ablations
which were conducted on the test set of MSR3D, these additional evaluations are performed
on a subset of four ScanNet scenes (scene0000_00, scene0030_00, scene0046_00, and
scene0086_00), using the ScanQA dataset for the Visual Question Answering (VQA) task.
We report and discuss the results accordingly.

propose sub-sampling the number of images used for SAM and LLaVA encodings, from
thousands down to just 400. We employ a Coverage Maximization strategy (detailed in
Section 6.2.2) to select the most informative views. In Table 6.3, we compare this strategy

against two other image selection methodologies (random image sampling and uniform
linear sampling) across three paradigms: our method, LLaVA-OV, and a NeRF baseline
with random ray-based feature sampling. We also evaluate performance across varying
numbers of selected images.
As expected, Coverage Maximization consistently outperforms other sampling strategies, particularly when the number of views is limited. Increasing the number of images
generally improves performance, although gains taper off beyond a certain point. Interestingly, we observe that the 2D baseline LLaVA-OV performs better than NeRF-based
methods when using very few views (i.e. 10), which aligns with expectations: NeRF
requires dense coverage to reconstruct coherent feature fields (both LLaVA and segmentation), whereas LLaVA-2D is designed to handle sparse multi-view inputs. However, as
the number of views increases, NeRF-based methods surpass the 2D baseline. Especially,
our LLaVA3 outperforms the other two baselines.
Note that LLaVA-2D is constrained by a fixed context window and can only accept up
to 44 images at once.
Compressing LLaVA Tokens in Feature Field. One key characteristic of LLaVA visual
tokens is their high complexity: they are unnormalized, sparse, and extremely highdimensional (3584 dimensions). As a result, training a feature field to regress these tokens
requires stabilizing the learning process through the introduction of an intermediate,
lower-dimensional representation. As explained in Section 6.2.1, unlike SplatTalk which
employs a pre-trained, per-scene auto-encoder to learn stable features directly inside the
field, we opt to jointly learn both a stable feature field and a decoder that reconstructs the
full LLaVA tokens.
In Table 6.4, we compare several strategies for compressing LLaVA features: (i) using
a frozen auto-encoder trained prior to the field (i.e. as in SplatTalk), (ii) jointly training
the field and decoder from scratch during scene optimization, and (iii) a hybrid strategy
where we first pre-train the auto-encoder slightly and then fine-tune the decoder jointly
with the field. We report both VQA performance and the average training loss of the
feature field to highlight the limitations of using a frozen auto-encoder, which introduces
a “double compression” that hampers performance.

As expected, we observe improved performance when the decoder is allowed to update
during feature field training, effectively avoiding the drawbacks of double compression
by enabling end-to-end optimization. This benefit is reflected in both the final VQA
scores and the reduced average training losses. Interestingly, results are similar whether
or not the decoder is pre-trained prior to joint training, suggesting that the pre-training
step is not strictly necessary. A more detailed analysis reveals that a pre-trained decoder
leads to faster convergence of the LLaVA feature field, though the overall impact on final
performance remains limited.
Impact of View Direction Dependent Features. We evaluate the effect of incorporating
view dependency into the LLaVA features for the VQA task by testing different sampling
strategies per object, meaning that we vary the proportion of view-independent (VI) and
view-dependent (VD) features. Results are reported in Table 6.5.
We begin by assessing whether explicitly supervising both VI and VD channels is
necessary. Results indicate that without this dual supervision, performance drops significantly. This suggests that the VD-based deformation alone is insufficient for the network
to naturally learn meaningful VI features.
Next, we activate the dual loss and vary the proportion of VI and VD features. We find
that combining both feature types consistently outperforms using only one, highlighting
their complementary roles. Finally, our proposed adaptive VI-VD selection strategy yields
the best results. This is expected, as it not only leverages both feature types but also
constrains VD features to a dominant direction, which helps the LLM better reason about
spatial relationships. These results are consistent with those from MSR3D, described in
Section 6.3.2, where we use the question categories decomposition to further analyze the
impact of combining VI and VD features.
Filtering Impact. As described in Section 6.2.2, applying HDBScan to the segmentation
features can introduce segmentation errors that negatively affect downstream performance
when features are later extracted and fed to the LLM.

We identified three primary types of errors: over-segmentation, under-segmentation,
and noisy segmentation. Figure 6.8 provides qualitative examples of each issue, both
before and after filtering, to illustrate the necessity of the filtering step.
Table 6.6 presents the quantitative impact of each filtering sub-step on the ScanQA
scene subset. As mentioned earlier, noisy segmentation is the most common and detrimental issue, particularly in ScanNet reconstructions, where noisy image and depth data
reduce overall scene quality. Filtering these noisy regions results in non-negligible performance gains. Addressing both over-segmentation and under-segmentation also leads to
measurable improvements in VQA accuracy, albeit with smaller margins.
We consider all three types of errors to remain a source of imperfection in our pipeline,
with our filtering process not being foolproof. As our work does not aim to solve panoptic
segmentation, we view the current filtering step, and by extension our full 3D segmentation
process, as a component that remains open to further improvement.
Feature Extraction. We evaluate the impact of object feature ordering and multi-scale
extraction in Table 6.7.
Regarding the radar-inspired ordering strategy, we observe that arranging the features such that spatially close objects are also close in the prompt consistently improves
performance, regardless of whether multi-scale extraction is used.
The multi-scale experiments further demonstrate that sampling features from each subregion (i.e., fine- and medium-scale parts) significantly outperforms mono-scale extraction.
The “scale fixed size” parameter controls whether a fixed number of features is assigned
per object (“Large” scale) or per sub-class/region (“Small” scale). In other words, this
setting determines whether all objects contribute equally or proportionally to their internal
complexity. Results indicate an advantage in fixing the number of features per object.

We also evaluate the impact of filling the context window with
a different number of visual tokens. SplatTalk analysed that using the equivalent of a
single image’s worth of visual tokens produced results comparable to fully filling the
context window. Following a similar setup, as reported in Table 6.8, we observe that
while the number of features does have some impact, the effect is indeed relatively minor,
compared to our other ablatives. This suggests a high degree of redundancy in the
extracted features, and that the VLM can understand the scene even with significantly
fewer tokens. Nevertheless, the best performance is still achieved when the context
window is fully utilized, likely due to the inclusion of fine-grained cues that aid in more
detailed reasoning.
6.3.4
Grounding
Datasets. We evaluate grounding on ScanNet-based Sr3D+ and Nr3D, which provide natural language queries with ground-truth object instance IDs. We use a subset of ScanNet
scenes: 0011_00, 0030_00, 0046_00,0086_00, 0222_00, 0378_00, 0389_00 and 0435_00.
Protocol. Following standard baselines, we perform grounding by retrieving a binary
segmentation point cloud from our segmented NeRF using the instance ID predicted by
the LLM. To convert our NeRF into a point cloud, we adopt the OpenNeRF protocol: for
each supervision view, we render the segmentation mask and back-project it onto the
point cloud. For each point, we average the instance class feature across views and use
the centroids to determine the segmentation. We then compute standard detection-style
metrics: a prediction is correct if the segmentation IoU exceeds a threshold. We report
accuracy at 0.1 and 0.25 IoUs, following prior works.

Results. Results are reported in Table 6.9, with qualitative examples provided in Figure 6.4.
First, our method heavily outperforms existing NeRF-based open-vocabulary baselines
such as LeRF and OpenNeRF (resp. +245% and +60% in average). This improvement
is expected, as those methods rely solely on CLIP-based similarity measures, which are
effective when object names are explicitly mentioned in the query. In contrast, grounding
queries are phrased as spatial indications, making such non-reasoning approaches less
effective. The ConceptGraph baseline, which stores an explicit caption per-object, achieves
lower performance compared to ours, highlighting the fact that our object-centric tokenization gives the LLM the ability to reason over objects without any retraining. Using
our hierarchical representation, we can also perform sub-scale grounding, as illustrated
in Figure 6.6.
6.3.5
Semantic Segmentation and Other Segmentation Tasks
By leveraging the SAM-CLIP feature field (Section 6.2.2), we enable a broad range of segmentation tasks, a fundamental aspect of downstream 3D scene understanding. LLaVA3
supports semantic segmentation, but also open-vocabulary segmentation and instance
segmentation, all within a hierarchical framework. We provide qualitative evaluation
of all segmentation tasks and specifically perform quantitative evaluation for semantic
segmentation in the following.
Dataset. For both qualitative and quantitative evaluation, we use the same grounding
ScanNet subset and Replica.
Protocol. We perform quantitative evaluation only on the semantic segmentation downstream task. To assign semantic labels to each 3D point, we compute per-point and
per-scale CLIP embeddings from the NeRF and compare those to the encoded text labels.
For mono-scale ablations, predictions are made using features from a single scale while
the multi-scale version assigns to each point the label with the highest similarity across
all scales. We report mean Accuracy and mean IoU.
Qualitative Results.
Figure 6.10 presents qualitative examples of open-vocabulary seg-
mentation, a downstream task that is naturally supported by our method. As with
136
6.3. EXPERIMENTS

Figure 6.10: Open-Vocabulary Segmentation examples on Replica’s room0, using the

semantic segmentation, we follow a similar protocol: a textual query is provided, and
segmentation is performed using only the SAM-CLIP feature field, without relying on the

In addition, Figure 6.11 showcases a ScanNet example of our multi-scale instance
segmentation, demonstrating the ability of our approach to segment a scene into object
instances of varying granularities. We also include a complementary example on a Replica
scene in Figure 6.12, further highlighting the versatility and adaptability of our multi-scale
decomposition framework.
Quantitative Results. Amongst NeRF-based methods, our approach stands out by
achieving significantly better results by combining SAM’s high-quality masks with CLIP’s
expressive features. Unlike LeRF [76] which relies on DINO regularization and OpenNeRF [40]/DiSCO-3D which leverage less expressive OpenSeg models, our method benefits from an accurate and geometry-aligned supervision signal, enabling more reliable
and detailed scene understanding, achieving respectively up to +146% mIoU / +91%
mAcc, +21% / +7%, and +15% / +14% gains. Compared to graph-based methods, LLaVA³
compares favorably, outperforming ConceptGraph (+36% mIoU and +20% mAcc) and
matching HOV-SG (+4% and -3%), showing that continuous NeRF fields can yield highquality segmentations without relying on explicit point clouds representations.

It is noteworthy that LLaVA³ outperforms DiSCO-3D on this specific downstream
task. However, this result does not render DiSCO-3D obsolete; rather, it highlights the
complementary roles of the two methods.
LLaVA³ excels here because it is fundamentally designed to provide a structured,
object-centric decomposition of the entire scene. In contrast, DiSCO-3D operates as a more
flexible focusing tool. Its strength lies in its ability to find and group concepts that may
span across multiple objects (e.g., "all wooden surfaces") or defy rigid boundaries, making
it ideal for fine-grained usages.
6.4
Perspective: Bridging Global Reasoning and Local
Perception for Embodied AI
Integrating LLaVA³ and DiSCO-3D represents a natural evolution toward a unified 3D
reasoning system. Each component addresses a distinct aspect of task-driven scene understanding: LLaVA³, built upon a vision–language model, enables global comprehension
and high-level reasoning over neural fields; DiSCO-3D, in contrast, provides fine-grained,
spatially focused interpretation of specific areas in a NeRF. Used together, they form
complementary layers of perception essential for a powerful Cognitive Core.
Such integration directly addresses the limitations identified in the previous chapter,
where DiSCO-3D operated as the sole scene-understanding module accessible to the
138
6.4. PERSPECTIVE: BRIDGING GLOBAL REASONING AND LOCAL PERCEPTION FOR
EMBODIED AI
Figure 6.13: Combining LLaVA³ and DiSCO-3D inside an embodied agent’s Cognitive
Core.
LLM. Incorporating LLaVA³ provides the necessary global context, enabling the model
to better interpret the user’s request before invoking specific tools. This allows richer,
more coherent, and context-aware interactions. We display the typical Cognitive Core’s
pipeline this would induce in Figure 6.13.
For example, when faced with spatially grounded queries such as “Can you fetch the
plate in the living room?”, LLaVA³ first forms a global understanding of the environment
to localize the relevant area, before calling DiSCO-3D to focus on that specific region for
detailed retrieval. Similarly, in more complex tasks like “Place the red cup next to the
laptop on the desk,” the LLM could sequentially reason with LLaVA³ to identify the scene
layout and then use DiSCO-3D to precisely locate and manipulate the relevant objects.
Such chained reasoning illustrates how global comprehension and local perception can
complement each other within a single, coherent embodied reasoning framework.
Looking further, such an integration could also unlock a more dynamic control loop.
For instance, the LLM could use LLaVA³ to generate a multi-step plan, then call DiSCO-3D
to verify the presence of a specific target object. If DiSCO-3D fails (e.g., the "red cup" is
not found in the expected location), the agent can return to the LLaVA³ global context to
re-evaluate ("Perhaps it is on the other table?") or ask the user for clarification.
However, while this thesis presents LLaVA³ and DiSCO-3D as conceptually complementary, their full, practical integration into a single, closed-loop system was not
realized within the scope of this dissertation. The experiments in this work evaluated
each component’s strengths independently.
Despite this, their combination is a direct and compelling path for future work. This
integration is particularly feasible given the LLM-agent architecture introduced in section 5.5, which was already designed to operate with DiSCO-3D as a tool. Architecturally,
this would involve equipping the LLM-agent with LLaVA³’s omni-directional visual representation as a new, global context. The agent could then use this global view to inform
when and where to deploy its local DiSCO-3D tool, creating the powerful, hierarchical
139
CHAPTER 6. INTERFACING NERF AND LARGE LANGUAGE MODELS FOR HOLISTIC
SCENE UNDERSTANDING
reasoning system we have described.
6.5
Conclusion
We presented LLaVA³, a novel object-centric approach that improves the 3D scene understanding ability of VLMs from multi-view images without fine-tuning. Inspired by
the principles of Cubism, our method decomposes the scene into an object hierarchy and
computes for each of them an omnidirectional 2D visual representation that captures both
its semantic and spatial relationships with the scene. Experimental results demonstrate
that it enables VLMs to reason more effectively over 3D content, avoiding common pitfalls
such as object duplication or limited context windows, and outperforming other VLMbased solutions. LLaVA³ enables a large variety of downstream tasks, including 3D VQA,
3D grounding, and 3D semantic segmentation.
With this contribution, we have established the final component of our proposed
cognitive architecture, complementing the local discovery capabilities of DiSCO-3D and
the foundational perception layers established in previous chapters. Having addressed
the full spectrum of embodied requirements, we now proceed to the final chapter to
synthesize these contributions into a cohesive vision for the future of Embodied AI.
140
7
Conclusion
7.1
Thesis Recap: Augmenting NeRF with Structural
Improvements and Modules
In this thesis, we explored how Neural Radiance Fields (NeRFs), originally developed
for photorealistic novel view synthesis, can be evolved into the geometric backbone for
a comprehensive Embodied AI perception system. We proposed a modular ecosystem
where a robust NeRF serves as the central anchor for distinct interpretive layers, supporting
the full spectrum of robotic needs from incremental geometric reconstruction to high-level
reasoning. This line of work is motivated fundamentally by the dual requirements of
embodied agents: they need a reliable 3D map to ensure effective self-functioning, while
simultaneously requiring a rich, queryable interface to interpret user intent and act within
large-scale real-world scenes.
We proposed several technical contributions that progressively equip grid-based NeRFs
with these capabilities. Our first contribution, RING-NeRF, addressed the foundational
challenge of real-world reconstruction. It introduced a set of inductive biases that enables intrinsic Level-of-Detail (LOD) representations directly within the neural field. This
mechanism allows the model to inherently think in terms of multi-scale, which in turn significantly improves robustness and stability under difficult real-world constraints, notably
when dealing with sparse camera views or variations in observation distance. Beyond
just enhancing reconstruction accuracy, this contribution lays the groundwork for scalable
NeRF-based mapping systems capable of operating reliably in the wild, notably through
incremental reconstruction and dynamically adaptive levels of precision.
Building upon this geometric backbone, our second contribution focused on adapting
existing approaches of direct semantic segmentation in Vanilla NeRFs to grid-based
NeRFs. We improved the interpretability and reliability of feature grids through three
complementary additions: providing multi-scale information to the interpretation model,
feeding it shapes and textures rather than per-point density and color pairs, and injecting
broader contextual cues via higher-order feature grids interpolation. Together, these
refinements significantly improve the resulting NeRF semantic segmentations, even with
141
CHAPTER 7. CONCLUSION
sparse or noisy annotations. Beyond scene-specific supervision, we also addressed the
major limitation of requiring ground-truth 2D semantic maps. To this end, we explored
how our framework could be extended toward generalizable NeRF semantic segmentation
notably by exploring multi-scene optimization algorithms and by proposing mechanisms
to harmonize feature grids across scenes through the introduction of a codebook and
the use of pre-trained foundation models such as DINO. Enabling feed-forward NeRF
semantic segmentation remains an inherently challenging problem, but our work lays
the initial foundations for this direction, which is particularly important for an agent’s
Functional Core, where supervision-free semantic perception facilitates autonomous
operation in unknown environment.
We then extended our semantically aware and robust scene representation by focusing
on integrating user-driven interaction within the NeRF framework. Our third contribution,
DiSCO-3D, introduces a method for open-vocabulary segmentation and sub-concept
discovery designed as a module to be directly plugged into the neural field. Leveraging
pre-trained feature fields, our approach interprets arbitrary user queries, detects the
corresponding regions, and further decomposes them into meaningful sub-concepts
when necessary. This decomposition capability is particularly important, as real-world
queries often refer to heterogeneous categories (e.g., tools, furniture, food) that cannot be
treated as a single semantic class for embodied-related tasks. Through DiSCO-3D, the
neural representation becomes queryable, effectively bridging the gap between passive
perception and active, task-driven focus. In the context of embodied AI, such interactive
segmentation is essential: it enables an agent to selectively focus on relevant regions and
interpret instructions. As demonstrated, this module serves as a perceptual flexible tool
ready to be exploited by the Cognitive Core.
Finally, our fourth contribution, LLaVA3 , extends spatial scene understanding in NeRFs
toward global, high-level reasoning. Rather than introducing another module focused
on localized scene analysis, we propose a pipeline that transforms an entire NeRF into a
representation directly consumable by a vision–language model (VLM) without any VLM
fine-tuning. This enables global interpretation of large-scale neural fields, supporting
open-ended queries, high-level reasoning, and long-horizon planning without relying on
explicit keyword-based scene querying. This capacity for holistic 3D reasoning directly
from a neural field represents a significant step forward for scene understanding in realworld scenarios. In the context of embodied AI, LLaVA3 serves as a complementary
component to DiSCO-3D: while DiSCO-3D provides spatially precise, concept-driven
focus, LLaVA3 offers global situational awareness that can be fed into the agent’s cognitive
core. Together, they form a coherent framework that allows an agent to answer and act
upon a vast range of user-defined requests.
142
7.2. FROM NOVEL VIEW SYNTHESIS TO AN ECOSYSTEM TAILORED FOR EMBODIED AI
7.2
From Novel View Synthesis to an Ecosystem tailored for
Embodied AI
Beyond their individual technical contributions, the methods developed in this thesis are
not isolated improvements. They are purpose-built components of a coherent perceptual
ecosystem, which is specifically designed around the specific needs of embodied agents,
where it must continuously align perception, understanding, and action. The NeRF serves
as the foundational backbone for this system. By construction, it offers properties that
discrete representations (meshes, voxels or point clouds) lack: it is sensor-consistent as it is
optimized directly from raw images, continuous, allowing for any queries at any arbitrary
resolutions, and can be naturally enriched with semantics information.
The contributions of this thesis form distinct capabilities built upon this scene representation:
• The Geometric Backbone (Chapter 3): RING-NeRF provides the robust, adaptable
backbone, ensuring the agent can produce a reliable map for incremental localization
and navigation in the wild as part of its Functional Core.
• The Closed-Set Semantic Layer (Chapter 4): Our direct interpretation modules enhance the Functional Core with a dense semantic layer, enabling the fast identification
of fixed categories required for basic self-functioning such as navigation and object
manipulation.
• The Cognitive Interface (Chapters 5 & 6): Finally, our task-driven modules (DiSCO3D and LLaVA3 ) act as bridges between the scene representation and the agent’s
"brain," i.e., the Cognitive Core. They allow the high-level planner to both have a
holistic understanding of its environment and dynamically query the scene for more
focused information on specific sub-concepts.
This modular design transforms the NeRF from a passive rendering tool into a versatile
scene memory. It serves a dual purpose: acting as a precise geometric and semantic map
for the agent’s functional reflexes, while providing the task-driven modules essential for
an effective cognitive core.
7.3
Limitations of our System
Despite these advances, several limitations remain. First and foremost, while this thesis
has presented these four contributions as a single, coherent conceptual framework, it is
important to note that their full integration into a single applied, end-to-end system was
not part of this dissertation’s scope.
However, because they are all built upon the same foundational grid-based NeRF
representation, their practical integration is a clear and direct path for future work. This
143
CHAPTER 7. CONCLUSION
feasibility stems from their modular design: RING-NeRF and the semantic segmentation contributions were proven to be complementary enhancements to the main density/radiance field, while DiSCO-3D and LLaVA³ operate on their own independent
feature fields. With no problematic overlaps, consolidating these modules into a single
codebase and deploying it to real-life robot would be the final step in realizing the complete
embodied AI architecture this thesis has outlined.
The second set of limitations relates to ongoing research directions directly connected
to the individual contributions. As mentioned in the previous chapters, these include:
• For robust reconstruction, pushing the full potential of RING-NeRF to obtain a
fully adaptive and incremental process. This includes the model adapting its local
reconstruction precision based on region complexity and available resources, reconstructing large-scale scenes incrementally without performance degradation, and
ultimately performing efficient large-scale Simultaneous Localization and Mapping
(SLAM) directly within this representation.
• For semantic understanding, our closed-set segmentation module must be made
generalizable to operate without scene-specific ground-truth. This component
should be integrated within the SLAM framework to aid navigation and should also
be combined with grasping research to enable object interaction and manipulation.
• For task-driven reasoning, the DiSCO-3D and LLaVA³ contributions must be integrated into a single framework to unleash the full potential of the system to
understand a user query, plan a full course of action, and execute it.
Finally, other limitations stem from the intrinsic properties of NeRFs themselves. A
primary disadvantage is their comparatively low reconstruction and inference speed. This
performance bottleneck is not just an inconvenience; for many real-time robotic applications, it remains a significant hurdle. This slowness is especially stark when measured
against recent differentiable explicit representations like 3D Gaussian Splatting [75], which
achieve real-time rendering, or other hybrid representations [52, 114] that prioritize speed
over NeRF’s compactness.
Furthermore, the dominant paradigm of per-scene optimization is fundamentally
challenged by the emergence of feed-forward 3D foundation models [178, 88, 176, 74].
These models promise universal, zero-shot scene reconstruction, parsing multi-view
images instantly into 3D geometry using a single, pre-trained network. This capability
makes the iterative optimization pipeline of standard NeRFs appear comparatively slow.
Yet, despite these valid challenges, we argue that the foundational nature of NeRFs
remains uniquely suited to the goals of embodied AI. The strength of the framework
becomes apparent when contrasted with its competitors:
• Against Classical Representations: As mentioned in Chapter 2, while meshes, voxels,
and point clouds offer explicit geometry, they struggle to serve as a comprehensive
144
7.4. THE FUTURE OF 3D SCENE UNDERSTANDING: TOWARDS PREDICTIVE WORLD
MODELS
backbone for learning-based agents. Point clouds are sparse and unstructured;
voxels suffer from cubic memory scaling; and explicit meshes are difficult to optimize
directly from visual sensors due to fixed topologies.
• Against 3D Gaussian Splatting: While 3DGS is exceptional for view synthesis, the
goal of this thesis is not just to render scenes, but to understand them. Fundamentally,
they remain a collection of discrete surface primitives. Consequently, it struggles to
define a watertight topology or a continuous density field, lacking the 3D sampling
capabilities and volumetric coherence required for physical reasoning tasks like
collision checking.
• Against Foundation Models: Similarly, while foundation models offer impressive
feed-forward speed, they present critical trade-offs for an agent’s internal scene
model. Their outputs are usually ephemeral inferences rather than a persistent,
coherent state, leading to view-to-view inconsistencies. Moreover, these models predict sparse representations (i.e. point maps or splats) rather than dense, continuous
fields and thus share the limitations of point clouds. Finally, they cannot be easily
augmented with the dense and rich semantic fields necessary for interaction, thus
needing novel contributions with full large-model re-training.
This is precisely where the NeRF framework finds its unique strength. It bridges the
gap, acting as a persistent, coherent 3D state that is also learned directly from sensors. The
true power of NeRF, which this work has leveraged, is its continuity and queryability.
The ability to query any 3D coordinate for arbitrary, multi-modal features, from density
to semantics to language embeddings, transforms the representation from a scene renderer
into a scene model. For many robotic tasks, this coherent and expressive representation
is far more valuable than rendering speed alone, confirming that neural fields hold a
distinctive and relevant position in the future landscape of embodied perception.
Furthermore, the critique regarding reconstruction speed is often framed in the context
of offline processing. In an embodied context, the agent discovers the scene incrementally.
Neural Fields are uniquely adapted to this sequential setup. They allow for local updates,
optimizing only the newly discovered regions without the need to re-process the entire
map. This effectively mitigates the computational bottleneck, shifting the requirement
from instant full-scene reconstruction to real-time local refinement.
7.4
The Future of 3D Scene Understanding: Towards Predictive
World Models
Looking beyond the specific contributions of this thesis, I believe the field of Embodied AI
is at a critical inflection point. For the past decade, research was largely compartmentalized:
computer vision focused either on reconstruction or semantics, while robotics focused
on control or navigation. Today, we are witnessing the emergence of comprehensive
145
CHAPTER 7. CONCLUSION
Figure 7.1: Blueprint for a "Pre-Action" Embodied Architecture. Compared to our current
pipeline, we add a Predictive World Model that uses the NeRF to simulate and validate
the outcome of the proposed actions before physical execution, ensuring safety and logical
consistency. Also, the global pipeline (including the NeRF representation) can be updated
end-to-end to specify itself to its given tasks, for instance by using reinforcement learning
rewarding satisfying robot action.
systems attempting to do it all. The dominant trend is the rise of massive, end-to-end
Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models [113]. These systems aim to map sensory inputs
directly to robot actions, bypassing explicit state estimation entirely.
While the reflex-like capabilities of VLAs are undeniable, I fundamentally argue that
explicit, updatable 3D internal representations remain critical for rigorous embodied
intelligence. An agent cannot simply react to momentary observations; it must anchor
itself within a persistent reality. Without an internal scene map, end-to-end policies
are prone to hallucinations, predicting plausible but physically impossible actions, and
struggle with long-term consistency. Throughout this thesis, we have developed an
alternative: a modular ecosystem based on Neural Radiance Fields. We have demonstrated
that, through architectural improvements and auxiliary modules, these representations
possess unique essential properties: they enable incremental and adaptive reconstruction,
which is crucial for robust in-the-wild operation; facilitate semantic enrichment for the
Functional Core, and offer multiple connectivity options for the Cognitive Core. However,
we must acknowledge a fundamental limitation: while our system successfully integrates
the internal map with the agent’s intelligence, it remains a static description of the present.
The agent knows what is where, but it lacks the understanding to predict what will happen
if it interacts with the environment.
Bridging this gap requires evolving the representation from a static map into a predictive engine. Currently, our system excels at answering static queries ("Where is the mug?",
"Is this area traversable?"). However, real-world user intent often implies a transformation
of the state: "Clean the table" or "Can I fit this box in the trunk?" To answer these, an agent
cannot simply retrieve information from its current memory; it must simulate the future.
This necessity points toward the convergence of our 3D representations with the emerging
and very trending paradigm of World Models [44, 34, 45]. A World Model allows an
agent to predict the consequences of its actions (𝑆𝑡+1 ) given its current state (𝑆𝑡 ). In this
146
7.4. THE FUTURE OF 3D SCENE UNDERSTANDING: TOWARDS PREDICTIVE WORLD
MODELS
context, the NeRF eco-system developed in this thesis serves as the state 𝑆𝑡 : it provides
the grounding required to reliably "imagine" the outcome of a user’s task (e.g. verifying
if the box fits or if the table is reachable) before executing a single motion.
This convergence suggests a blueprint for a complete "Pre-Action" embodied system,
imagined in Figure 7.1, an architecture that integrates every contribution of this thesis
combined with a World Model into a comprehensive system. In this unified framework,
our augmented NeRF map serves as the persistent state memory. When a user provides a
complex instruction, the global reasoning module (LLaVA³) first interprets the intent and
formulates a high-level plan. It then queries the local discovery module (DiSCO-3D) to
ground specific target objects within the scene. Finally, instead of blindly executing, the
agent passes this grounded plan to a Predictive World Model, which uses the NeRF to
"hallucinate" the outcome of the action. The system can thus iterate, refining its plan in
this differentiable sandbox until it is logically sound and physically plausible. The result is
an agent that perceives, understands, plans, and validates its intentions, doing everything
required for intelligent behavior except the final transmission of motor commands.
Realizing the comprehensive "Pre-Action" architecture described above does not require distant, futuristic technology; and I believe current research is already on the cusp
of assembling these pieces into functional embodied agents. However, in my opinion,
the ultimate vision extends toward a convergence where the boundaries between the
agent’s cores and components dissolves entirely. Just like differentiable representations
innovated by propaging gradients from the 3D scene map to the 2D data, I believe we
can obtain a system where all the agent components are differentiable altogether, getting
updates from the same 2D data and learning from each other, as shown in Figure 7.1. In
this paradigm, we can imagine agents using Reinforcement Learning not just to optimize
motor commands, but to optimize their internal scene representations directly, simulating
physics, learning complex semantics, and refining navigation trajectories. Crucially, I
believe that Neural Fields remain perfectly adapted to serve as the scene representation
for this vision, notably thanks to their differentiability.
The journey of this thesis began with precisely this premise: that Neural Radiance
Fields could be more than just a tool for creating images. We sought to transform them
into a true 3D scene model, a foundation upon which an intelligent agent could build both
its Functional and Cognitive cores and deepen its understanding of the world. Through
the contributions of robust reconstruction, semantic grounding, and language-driven
reasoning, this work has laid a blueprint for that vision. The true and final test, however,
lies beyond the metrics and datasets. I look forward to the day when these neural
representations are no longer just the subject of research, but the living brain of a real
robot, navigating our world, understanding our requests, and, ultimately, let’s face it: I’m
just hoping it will one day help me fold my laundry.

Titre : De la Reconstruction au Raisonnement : Champs de Radiance Neuronaux pour une Compréhension Approfondie de Scènes 3D
Mots clés : Compréhension de Scènes 3D, NeRF, IA Incarnée, Reconstruction 3D, Vision par Ordinateur, Intelligence Artificielle
Résumé : L'intelligence artificielle incarnée nécessite des agents capables de percevoir, de comprendre et d'agir au sein d'environnements
complexes. Cela requiert un système de perception conciliant la précision de reconstruction pour l'action physique et la richesse sémantique
pour le raisonnement. Cette thèse propose d'utiliser les champs de radiance neuronaux (NeRF) comme fondation, les faisant évoluer de la
synthèse de vues vers une représentation de scène robuste et sémantique, socle d'un écosystème incarné modulaire.
Nous abordons d'abord le besoin fondamental d'une cartographie géométrique fiable en conditions réelles en introduisant RING-NeRF. Cette
architecture intègre des biais inductifs structurels pour combler les lacunes de robustesse et d'adaptabilité des NeRF standards, permettant une
reconstruction stable et incrémentale à partir d'images éparses ou imparfaites. Sur cette base, nous permettons à l'agent d'assurer son autofonctionnement en développant des méthodes d'interprétation sémantique intrinsèques. Nous proposons des améliorations architecturales et
des techniques de généralisation pour effectuer une segmentation sémantique au cœur même du modèle NeRF, offrant ainsi l'identification de
catégories prédéfinies indispensable à la navigation et à la manipulation d'objets.
Enfin, nous relevons le défi d'une compréhension de scène interactive, pilotée par les tâches données par l'utilisateur. Nous introduisons d'abord
DiSCO-3D, un module de segmentation NeRF à vocabulaire ouvert permettant à l'agent de focaliser son attention sur des concepts en réponse à
des requêtes textuelles libres et de les décomposer. En complément, nous développons LLaVA³, un système de raisonnement global qui
connecte la représentation NeRF à de grands modèles de vision-langage (VLM). Cette interface permet à l'agent d'appréhender l'environnement
de manière holistique et de planifier des tâches complexes sans nécessiter d'affinage (fine-tuning) du modèle de fondation.
À travers ces contributions, cette thèse établit un cadre complet où une représentation NeRF robuste et sémantique constitue le pivot de
l'écosystème, soutenant des modules désignés pour les réflexes fonctionnels et pour le raisonnement cognitif d'un agent incarné.
Title: From Reconstruction to Reasoning: Neural Radiance Fields for Comprehensive 3D Scene Understanding
Key words: 3D Scene Understanding, NeRF, Embodied AI, 3D Reconstruction, Computer Vision, Artificial Intelligence
Abstract: Embodied artificial intelligence requires agents that can perceive, navigate, understand, and act upon user-defined tasks within
complex environments. Achieving this demands a perceptual system that can bridge the gap between the reconstruction precision and the
semantic richness respectively required for control and reasoning. This thesis proposes that Neural Radiance Fields (NeRFs) can serve as the
foundation for such a system, evolving from their original role as a view-synthesis tool into a robust, queryable scene map that anchors a
modular embodied ecosystem.
We first address the foundational need for a reliable, in-the-wild geometric mapping, introducing RING-NeRF. This architecture integrates
structural inductive biases to solve the robustness gaps of standard NeRFs, enabling stable, incremental and adaptive reconstruction from
sparse or imperfect multi-view imagery. Building upon this, we enable the agent’s self-functioning capabilities by developing direct
interpretation methods. We propose architectural enhancements and generalization techniques to perform semantic segmentation directly
within the NeRF model, providing the fixed-category understanding essential for navigation and object manipulation.
Finally, we address the challenge of open-ended, user-driven scene understanding. We first introduce DiSCO-3D, an open-vocabulary NeRF
segmentation framework that allows the agent to focus locally and decompose specific concepts based on arbitrary text queries.
Complementing this local understanding, we develop LLaVA³, a global reasoning pipeline that interfaces the 3D NeRF representation with Large
Vision-Language Models, enabling the agent to interpret the environment holistically and plan complex tasks without fine-tuning of the
foundation model.
Through these contributions, the thesis establishes a comprehensive framework where a robust, semantically grounded NeRF serves as the
central state representation, supporting specifically designed modules for both the functional reflexes and the cognitive reasoning of an
embodied agent.
